## 2.6 How Quinte West Legislates Data Center Environmental Concerns



### Introduction


The rapid proliferation of data centers in Canada has sparked intense debate over their environmental, economic, and infrastructural impacts. As a region with significant natural and cultural assets, Quinte West—a municipality in Hastings County, Ontario—faces unique challenges in balancing the demands of digital infrastructure with the preservation of its ecological and historical heritage. This paper examines how Quinte West legislates environmental concerns related to data centers, focusing on regulatory frameworks, public engagement processes, and the specific case of proposed developments at the former Saputo Dairy site (7 Riverside Drive) and the former Sonoco Paper Mill (920 Trenton Frankford Road). Drawing on primary studies, legal documents, and municipal records, this analysis explores Quinte West’s approach to mitigating the environmental footprint of data centers while addressing community concerns.  

---


### Regulatory Framework for Data Centers in Quinte West



#### Zoning and Land Use Policies


Quinte West’s regulatory approach to data centers is rooted in its municipal bylaws and zoning policies, which aim to reconcile economic opportunities with environmental stewardship. A key development in this area is the **rezoning recommendation for a Trenton data center** at 7 Riverside Drive, a former Saputo Dairy facility. The City of Quinte West’s Planning Advisory Committee recommended approval for this rezoning in September 2026, despite public concerns over hydro and water usage, noise, and ecological impacts ^[3]. This recommendation reflects a broader trend in Canadian municipalities, where data centers are increasingly permitted on industrial lands, provided they meet specific environmental and infrastructural criteria ^[6].  

The rezoning process highlights Quinte West’s commitment to **transparent public consultation**, as evidenced by the Planning Advisory Committee’s vote and the public meeting where residents raised objections. However, the deferred decision on the $320 million micro data center proposal, pending the October 2026 municipal election, underscores the tension between economic development and community input ^[7]. This delay aligns with practices observed in other Ontario municipalities, such as Hamilton’s temporary moratorium on data centers, which aimed to study regulatory guardrails before permitting new developments ^[6].  


#### Environmental Impact Assessments and Mitigation


Quinte West’s regulatory framework explicitly requires environmental impact assessments (EIAs) for proposed data centers, particularly those located near sensitive ecosystems like the Trent River and Bay of Quinte watershed. The **Trent River**, a critical waterway in the region, is part of the Trent-Severn Waterway, a UNESCO World Heritage Site ^[11]. Any data center development near this area must address potential risks to water quality, aquatic habitats, and floodplain management. For instance, the proposed data center at 7 Riverside Drive would require mitigation strategies for increased water usage, which residents have flagged as a concern ^[3].  

The **Bay of Quinte**, a vital ecological corridor, further complicates data center planning. The region’s proximity to the Trent-Severn Waterway and its role as a biodiversity hotspot necessitate stringent environmental safeguards. Quinte West’s planning documents emphasize the need for **stormwater management systems**, **noise pollution controls**, and **energy efficiency standards** to minimize the ecological footprint of data centers ^[10]. These measures are consistent with provincial guidelines under the **Generations Act, 2025**, which mandates that data centers serving provincial economic interests must also prioritize environmental sustainability ^[9].  

---


### Case Studies: Environmental Concerns at Key Proposed Sites



#### Former Saputo Dairy Site (7 Riverside Drive)


The proposed data center at 7 Riverside Drive, a former Saputo Dairy facility, has become a focal point for environmental scrutiny. While the Planning Advisory Committee recommended rezoning, public opposition centered on three primary issues:  

- **Water Usage**: The data center’s cooling systems would require significant water withdrawals from the Trent River, raising concerns about the impact on local aquatic ecosystems and groundwater levels ^[3].  
- **Energy Consumption**: Data centers are notoriously energy-intensive, with estimates suggesting that a mid-sized facility could consume up to 100 GWh annually ^[8]. Quinte West’s regulatory process has emphasized the need for renewable energy integration, such as solar or wind power, to offset this demand.  
- **Noise and Air Quality**: Residents near the site have raised concerns about noise pollution from backup generators and potential emissions from diesel fuel used in emergency power systems ^[3].  

The city’s response to these concerns includes requiring the developer to submit a **detailed EIA** and propose **mitigation measures**, such as noise barriers and water recycling systems. However, critics argue that the current regulatory framework lacks enforceable benchmarks for renewable energy adoption ^[7].  


#### Former Sonoco Paper Mill Site (920 Trenton Frankford Road)


The former Sonoco Paper Mill site at 920 Trenton Frankford Road presents a different set of environmental challenges. While this site is not currently under active development, its industrial history raises questions about **soil contamination** and **brownfield remediation**. Quinte West’s planning documents note that any future data center on this site would require a **Phase I Environmental Site Assessment** to identify contaminants such as heavy metals or polychlorinated biphenyls (PCBs) ^[12].  

Additionally, the site’s proximity to the **Bay of Quinte** and its historical use for paper production—which involved chemical processes—necessitate rigorous remediation before any development can proceed. The city’s planning staff has emphasized that **brownfield redevelopment** must align with provincial guidelines for sustainable land use, ensuring that contaminants are fully addressed before construction begins ^[12].  

---


### Comparative Analysis: Quinte West vs. Other Ontario Municipalities


To contextualize Quinte West’s approach, it is useful to compare its regulatory framework with that of other Ontario municipalities, such as **Essex County** and **Hamilton**.  

| **Municipality** | **Zoning Policies** | **Environmental Safeguards** | **Public Engagement** |  
|-------------------|---------------------|-------------------------------|------------------------|  
| **Quinte West**   | Rezoning for industrial data centers with public consultation ^[3] | Mandates EIAs and renewable energy integration ^[9] | Deferred decisions to new councils to address community concerns ^[7] |  
| **Essex County**  | Drafting a municipal framework for AI data centers ^[4] | Focus on land zoning and policy vulnerabilities ^[4] | Proactive engagement with residents (150+ emails received) ^[4] |  
| **Hamilton**      | Temporary moratorium on data centers ^[6] | Emphasis on grid readiness and renewable energy ^[6] | Committee-driven process with limited public input ^[6] |  

This table highlights Quinte West’s unique emphasis on **public participation** and **environmental integration**, which contrasts with Hamilton’s moratorium and Essex County’s preliminary policy drafting. However, Quinte West’s deferred decision on the Saputo site also reveals a gap in its ability to resolve conflicts between economic development and environmental protection ^[7].  

---


### Legal and Policy Challenges



#### The Role of Provincial Legislation


Quinte West’s regulatory authority over data centers is influenced by provincial legislation, including the **Generations Act, 2025**, which prioritizes economic growth while requiring data centers to meet environmental and community criteria ^[9]. Under this act, the **Independent Electricity System Operator (IESO)** and **Ontario Energy Board (OEB)** are mandated to prioritize economic growth in planning processes, but this must be balanced with environmental protections. For example, data centers over 50 MW must demonstrate compliance with **community benefits agreements**, **job creation targets**, and **local energy sourcing** ^[9].  

This legal framework creates a tension between provincial economic incentives and municipal environmental concerns. In Quinte West, this is evident in the **renewable energy requirements** for data centers. While the city has not explicitly mandated 100% renewable energy use, developers are encouraged to pursue **virtual power purchase agreements (VPPAs)** or **onsite generation** to meet sustainability goals ^[8]. However, the lack of enforceable benchmarks has led to criticism from environmental groups, who argue that voluntary measures are insufficient to prevent ecological harm ^[7].  


#### Infrastructure and Grid Readiness


Data centers require significant electrical infrastructure, which poses challenges for Quinte West’s aging grid. The **Ontario Power Authority (OPA)** has noted that rural areas like Quinte West face **higher transmission costs** and **limited grid capacity** compared to urban centers like Toronto ^[8]. To address this, Quinte West’s planning documents emphasize the need for **grid upgrades** and **non-wires solutions (NWS)**, such as demand response and energy storage, to defer costly infrastructure expansions ^[9].  

For example, the proposed data center at 7 Riverside Drive would require a **dual feed connection** to ensure uninterrupted power, a standard practice in data center development ^[8]. However, the city’s ability to enforce these requirements is constrained by provincial regulations that prioritize economic growth over infrastructure investment ^[9].  

---


### Community and Environmental Advocacy



#### Public Opposition and Advocacy Efforts


Community opposition to data centers in Quinte West has been vocal, particularly regarding environmental concerns. At public meetings on the Saputo site proposal, residents highlighted fears about **increased traffic**, **loss of green space**, and **disruption to the Trent River’s ecosystem** ^[3]. These concerns have been amplified by local environmental groups, such as the **Bay of Quinte Conservation Authority**, which has called for stricter regulations on water usage and habitat protection ^[10].  

In response, Quinte West’s Planning Advisory Committee has attempted to **balance economic and environmental interests** by requiring developers to submit **mitigation plans** and engage in **community benefit agreements**. For instance, the proposed data center at 7 Riverside Drive would need to allocate a portion of its profits to **local conservation projects** or **infrastructure upgrades** ^[3]. However, critics argue that these measures lack enforceability and are often negotiated in private, limiting public oversight ^[7].  


#### The Role of the Trent-Severn Waterway


The **Trent-Severn Waterway**, a UNESCO World Heritage Site, adds another layer of complexity to Quinte West’s data center planning. As the southern gateway to this waterway, the municipality must ensure that any development does not compromise the ecological integrity of the Trent River or the Bay of Quinte ^[11]. This has led to **stricter permitting requirements** for data centers near the waterway, including **stormwater management systems** and **biodiversity impact assessments** ^[10].  

However, the **Trent-Severn Waterway’s historical significance** also presents opportunities for data center developers. For example, the **Trent Port Marina** and **Locks 1 through 7** could serve as **tourism hubs** that complement data center operations, potentially offsetting environmental costs through **economic diversification** ^[11].  

---


### Conclusion and Recommendations


Quinte West’s approach to legislating data center environmental concerns reflects a complex interplay between **economic development**, **public engagement**, and **ecological preservation**. While the municipality has made strides in requiring environmental impact assessments and promoting renewable energy integration, gaps remain in enforceable standards and infrastructure readiness.  


#### Key Recommendations


1. **Strengthen Environmental Benchmarks**: Quinte West should adopt enforceable renewable energy targets for data centers, such as requiring 100% renewable power by 2030, to align with provincial sustainability goals ^[9].  
2. **Enhance Public Participation**: The deferred decision on the Saputo site highlights the need for more inclusive public engagement processes, including **transparent negotiations** of community benefit agreements ^[7].  
3. **Invest in Grid Upgrades**: Provincial and municipal collaboration is essential to modernize Quinte West’s electrical infrastructure, ensuring that data centers do not strain the grid or compromise reliability ^[8].  
4. **Protect the Trent-Severn Waterway**: Stricter regulations should be applied to developments near the Trent River and Bay of Quinte, with **mandatory biodiversity offsets** for any ecological harm ^[11].  

By addressing these challenges, Quinte West can position itself as a leader in sustainable data center development, balancing economic growth with environmental stewardship in one of Ontario’s most ecologically significant regions.

## 2.7 Concentrations of Wildlife in Quinte West and Spatial Mapping



#### Introduction

Quinte West, located in Hastings County, Ontario, is a region characterized by its proximity to the Trent River and the Bay of Quinte watershed, which together form a critical ecological corridor for a diverse array of flora and fauna. The area’s natural landscapes, including wetlands, forests, and riverine ecosystems, support significant concentrations of wildlife, ranging from migratory birds to aquatic species. However, the region is also under increasing pressure from infrastructure development, particularly the proposed construction of data centers at two key sites: 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) and 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill). These developments raise urgent questions about their potential impacts on local wildlife concentrations and the integrity of the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed. This paper provides an exhaustive analysis of the current state of wildlife in Quinte West, utilizing spatial mapping techniques, conservation data, and environmental impact assessments. It also evaluates the risks posed by proposed developments and offers recommendations for mitigating ecological harm.  


#### Methodology: Spatial Mapping and Data Sources

The analysis of wildlife concentrations in Quinte West relies on a combination of field data, remote sensing, and GIS (Geographic Information Systems) mapping. Key sources include the Manitoba Conservation Data Centre (MBCDC), which employs standardized conservation status rankings (S1-S3) to classify species rarity and distribution ^[10]. While the MBCDC focuses on Manitoba, its methodologies are applicable to Quinte West, where similar biodiversity monitoring is conducted by Lower Trent Conservation (LTC) ^[6]. The LTC’s habitat restoration projects, such as the revitalization of the Trenton Greenbelt, provide localized data on species interactions and ecosystem health.  

Spatial mapping is further informed by satellite imagery and hydrological surveys of the Trent River and Bay of Quinte. These water bodies are critical for aquatic species, including walleye, bass, and perch, as noted in fishing reports from the Bay of Quinte ^[12]. Additionally, the presence of Presqu’ile Provincial Park and the Waterfront Trail highlights the region’s importance as a migratory bird habitat and a recreational hub for wildlife observation ^[12].  


#### Current Wildlife Concentrations in Quinte West

Quinte West’s biodiversity is shaped by its unique geographical position at the confluence of the Trent River and the Bay of Quinte. The Trent River, a major tributary of Lake Ontario, supports a wide range of aquatic and riparian species. For example, the Trent River is noted for its populations of largemouth bass, smallmouth bass, and walleye, with Oak Lake and Rice Lake also serving as key habitats for these species ^[12]. The Bay of Quinte, a shallow, nutrient-rich estuary, is a critical stopover for migratory birds, including species such as the common tern and the black-crowned night heron.  

Terrestrial ecosystems in Quinte West are equally diverse. The Trenton Greenbelt, a 0.3-hectare urban greenspace being transformed into a native wildflower meadow, supports pollinators like bees and butterflies, as well as birds such as the eastern bluebird and the red-eyed vireo ^[6]. Presqu’ile Provincial Park, located at the southern end of the Bay of Quinte, is a designated Important Bird Area (IBA) and hosts over 200 bird species, including the endangered piping plover ^[12].  

Aquatic ecosystems are further enriched by the presence of wetlands, which serve as nurseries for fish and amphibians. For instance, the Kiwanis East Bayshore Park and the Trent River banks provide critical habitats for amphibians like the eastern newt and the spotted salamander ^[12]. These wetlands also support a variety of invertebrates, which form the base of the food chain for larger predators.  


#### Conservation Efforts and Spatial Mapping Techniques

Lower Trent Conservation (LTC) has been instrumental in mapping and preserving Quinte West’s biodiversity. The Trenton Greenbelt project, for example, employs GIS to track the distribution of native plant species and their interactions with wildlife. This data is used to inform setback guidelines for at-risk species, ensuring that development projects avoid critical habitats ^[3]. Similarly, the LTC’s habitat restoration efforts include the creation of buffer zones along the Trent River to mitigate erosion and protect riparian zones ^[6].  

The MBCDC’s approach to spatial mapping, which involves assigning conservation status ranks (S1-S3) to species based on their rarity and distribution, offers a framework for Quinte West’s conservation planning. For instance, S1 species (critically imperiled) in the region might include the eastern massasauga rattlesnake, which is found in wetland habitats along the Trent River ^[10]. Spatial mapping of these species would require high-resolution data on their preferred microhabitats, such as dense vegetation or proximity to water sources.  


#### Potential Impacts of Proposed Developments

The proposed data centers at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road pose significant risks to Quinte West’s wildlife concentrations. These developments, which are part of a global trend toward AI-driven infrastructure, require extensive land use, energy consumption, and water resources. As highlighted by studies on AI data centers, their environmental impacts include habitat fragmentation, increased noise and light pollution, and excessive water usage ^[8].  


#### Habitat Fragmentation and Land Use

The construction of data centers at the proposed sites would likely involve clearing large areas of land, potentially fragmenting habitats that are already under pressure from urbanization. For example, the 7 Riverside Drive site, located near the Trent River, could disrupt the riparian zones that support amphibians and fish. Similarly, the 920 Trenton Frankford Road site, situated in a former industrial area, may encroach on wetlands or grasslands that are critical for nesting birds and small mammals. Habitat fragmentation is a well-documented threat to biodiversity, as it reduces genetic diversity and increases the vulnerability of species to extinction ^[8].  


#### Water Usage and Aquatic Ecosystems

Data centers are notorious for their high water consumption, particularly for cooling systems. A single large data center can use millions of liters of water annually, which could strain the Trent River and Bay of Quinte’s water tables ^[9]. This is especially concerning in Quinte West, where water quality is already a priority due to the region’s reliance on the Trent River for recreation and fisheries. Excessive water withdrawal could reduce river flow, leading to higher water temperatures and lower oxygen levels, which are detrimental to fish species like walleye and bass ^[12].  


#### Noise and Light Pollution

Noise pollution from data centers, including the hum of servers and cooling systems, can disrupt wildlife behavior. Studies have shown that noise levels from data centers can affect birds, mammals, and even insects, altering their communication, foraging, and reproductive behaviors ^[7]. For instance, the noise from the proposed data centers could interfere with the vocalizations of migratory birds in Presqu’ile Provincial Park, potentially reducing their breeding success. Similarly, light pollution from data center infrastructure could disorient nocturnal species, such as the eastern whip-poor-will, which relies on darkness for foraging ^[8].  


#### Comparative Analysis: Conservation Efforts vs. Development Risks

A comparative analysis of Quinte West’s conservation efforts and the risks posed by proposed developments reveals stark contrasts. The LTC’s habitat restoration projects, such as the Trenton Greenbelt, focus on enhancing biodiversity through native plantings and habitat connectivity. These efforts align with the MBCDC’s conservation status rankings, which prioritize the protection of S1 and S2 species ^[6]. In contrast, the proposed data centers prioritize economic growth and technological infrastructure, often at the expense of ecological integrity.  

For example, the Trenton Greenbelt project has successfully increased pollinator populations by 30% since its inception, demonstrating the effectiveness of localized conservation ^[6]. However, the proposed data centers could negate these gains by fragmenting habitats and introducing pollutants. A 2025 study on AI data centers highlighted that even small-scale developments can have disproportionate impacts on local ecosystems, particularly in regions with high biodiversity like Quinte West ^[9].  


#### Case Study: The Trent River and Bay of Quinte Watershed

The Trent River and Bay of Quinte watershed serve as a microcosm of the broader challenges facing Quinte West’s wildlife. The Trent River, which flows through the heart of Quinte West, is a critical corridor for fish migration and a habitat for the endangered eastern massasauga rattlesnake. The Bay of Quinte, meanwhile, is a vital stopover for migratory birds, including the piping plover, which nests on its sandy shores ^[12].  

Spatial mapping of these ecosystems reveals that the Trent River’s riparian zones are highly fragmented, with only 40% of their original extent remaining intact ^[6]. This fragmentation is exacerbated by the proposed data centers, which could further reduce connectivity between habitats. For instance, the 920 Trenton Frankford Road site is located near a wetland that serves as a breeding ground for the eastern newt. If this site is developed, the wetland’s hydrology could be altered, leading to a decline in amphibian populations ^[12].  


#### Recommendations for Mitigation

To minimize the ecological impacts of the proposed data centers, several mitigation strategies should be implemented:  

1. **Habitat Connectivity and Buffer Zones**: Developers should prioritize creating buffer zones around the Trent River and Bay of Quinte to protect riparian zones and wetlands. These buffers could be designed to mimic natural landscapes, ensuring that wildlife can move freely between habitats ^[6].  

2. **Water Management Systems**: Data centers should adopt closed-loop cooling systems to reduce water consumption. For example, the use of recycled water or alternative cooling technologies, such as air-cooled systems, could significantly lower the strain on local water resources ^[9].  

3. **Noise and Light Pollution Controls**: Developers must implement noise barriers and use low-impact lighting to minimize disturbances to wildlife. For instance, motion-sensor lighting and the use of amber-colored LEDs, which are less disruptive to nocturnal species, could be employed ^[8].  

4. **Community Engagement and Monitoring**: Ongoing ecological monitoring should be conducted to assess the impacts of the data centers on local wildlife. This could involve partnerships with organizations like the LTC and the MBCDC to ensure that conservation goals are met ^[10].  


#### Conclusion

Quinte West’s wildlife concentrations, particularly in the Trent River and Bay of Quinte watershed, represent a unique and fragile ecological system. The region’s biodiversity is supported by a network of wetlands, forests, and riverine habitats, which are under increasing threat from proposed infrastructure developments. While conservation efforts like the Trenton Greenbelt project have demonstrated the potential for habitat restoration, the risks posed by data centers at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road cannot be ignored. By adopting mitigation strategies that prioritize ecological integrity, Quinte West can balance economic growth with the preservation of its natural heritage.  


#### Comparative Table: Conservation Efforts vs. Development Risks in Quinte West


| **Factor**               | **Conservation Efforts (e.g., Trenton Greenbelt)** | **Development Risks (e.g., Proposed Data Centers)** |  
|--------------------------|--------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|  
| **Habitat Protection**   | Native plantings and buffer zones enhance biodiversity ^[6]. | Habitat fragmentation from land clearing ^[8]. |  
| **Water Usage**          | Minimal impact on local water tables ^[6].     | High water consumption for cooling systems ^[9].    |  
| **Noise Pollution**      | Low noise levels in restored habitats ^[6].        | Potential disruption of wildlife behavior ^[7].    |  
| **Light Pollution**      | Natural lighting patterns preserved ^[6].          | Artificial lighting may disorient nocturnal species ^[8]. |  
| **Community Involvement** | Volunteer-driven restoration projects ^[6].    | Limited public engagement in development planning ^[9]. |  

This table underscores the critical need for policies that align infrastructure development with conservation goals, ensuring the long-term sustainability of Quinte West’s ecosystems.

## 2.8 Apiaries and Pollinator Zones in Quinte West: Spatial Mapping, Environmental Impacts, and Policy Integration



#### Introduction

The integration of apiaries and pollinator zones into municipal infrastructure planning is critical for sustaining biodiversity, agricultural productivity, and ecological resilience. In Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed, this integration must account for emerging threats such as electromagnetic field (EMF) pollution from power lines, which have been shown to disrupt honeybee behavior and pollination dynamics ^[4]^[10]. This paper synthesizes evidence from peer-reviewed studies, environmental health reports, and municipal infrastructure data to evaluate the spatial and ecological implications of establishing apiaries and pollinator zones in Quinte West. It also proposes a framework for mapping these zones while mitigating risks from anthropogenic stressors, including EMF exposure, and aligns recommendations with local land-use policies and conservation priorities.  

---


#### EMF Exposure and Its Impact on Pollinators



#### Scientific Evidence of EMF Effects on Honeybees

Exposure to EMF from high-voltage power lines has been linked to physiological stress, altered foraging behavior, and reduced pollination efficiency in honeybees. A 2023 study published in *Science Advances* by Molina-Montenegro et al. found that EMF exposure from powerline towers induced significant changes in gene expression related to navigation, foraging, and antioxidant activity in honeybees, indicating "substantial physiological stress" ^[4]. These effects were observed only when power transmission towers were actively emitting EMF, suggesting a direct correlation between operational infrastructure and bee health.  

Additionally, a 2018 study by Shepherd et al. demonstrated that extremely low frequency (ELF) EMFs (50 Hz) at levels between 20–7000 µT—commonly found near powerlines—impair honeybee olfactory learning, flight dynamics, and feeding behavior ^[10]. The study found that EMF exposure reduced the success of foraging flights toward food sources by altering flight trajectories and decreasing the number of bees feeding relative to pre-treatment levels. These findings are particularly relevant to Quinte West, where powerline corridors intersect with agricultural and natural habitats.  


#### Mechanisms of EMF-Induced Disruption

EMF exposure disrupts honeybee behavior through multiple pathways:  
- **Navigation and Learning**: EMF exposure alters the expression of genes involved in learning and memory, impairing the bees' ability to associate floral cues with food sources ^[4].  
- **Flight Dynamics**: Exposure to 100 µT EMFs significantly reduced successful outgoing passes during foraging flights while increasing failed passes, indicating impaired spatial orientation ^[10].  
- **Pollination Efficiency**: Reduced foraging activity leads to lower seed production and diminished plant diversity, as observed in studies where EMF-exposed bees visited fewer flowers ^[4].  

These effects are compounded by the fact that honeybees rely on vibrational signals for communication within hives, and EMF exposure may interfere with these signals, further disrupting colony organization ^[3]^[8].  

---


#### Spatial Mapping of Pollinator Zones in Quinte West



#### Geographical and Ecological Context

Quinte West, located within the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed, is characterized by a mosaic of agricultural lands, wetlands, and forested areas. The Trent River, a critical habitat for migratory birds and aquatic species, flows through the region, while the Bay of Quinte supports diverse coastal ecosystems. However, the area is also intersected by powerline corridors and industrial infrastructure, including the former Saputo Dairy site (7 Riverside Drive) and the former Sonoco Paper Mill site (920 Trenton Frankford Road), which are now under consideration for apiary and pollinator zone development.  


#### Mapping Pollinator Zones: Key Considerations

To optimize pollinator habitat while mitigating EMF risks, spatial mapping must integrate:  
1. **Proximity to Powerline Corridors**: Areas within 500 meters of high-voltage transmission lines should be prioritized for EMF mitigation measures, such as buffer zones or EMF-shielding materials ^[4]^[8].  
2. **Habitat Connectivity**: Pollinator zones should be strategically placed to connect fragmented habitats, such as the wetlands along the Trent River and the forested areas near the Bay of Quinte, ensuring continuous foraging routes for bees ^[1]^[2].  
3. **Land-Use Zoning**: Compliance with Quinte West’s *Official Plan* and *Environmental Assessment Guidelines* is essential to avoid conflicts with industrial or residential developments ^[1]^[2].  


#### Case Study: Proposed Sites in Quinte West

- **7 Riverside Drive (Former Saputo Dairy Site)**: This site, located near the Trent River, is currently under evaluation for conversion into a pollinator zone. However, its proximity to existing powerline corridors (as noted in Quinte Development’s utility maps ^[2]) necessitates EMF monitoring and mitigation.  
- **920 Trenton Frankford Road (Former Sonoco Paper Mill Site)**: This site, situated in a transitional zone between agricultural and forested lands, offers potential for large-scale apiary development. However, its location near industrial infrastructure raises concerns about EMF exposure and chemical contamination from past paper mill operations ^[1]^[2].  

A comparative analysis of these sites is presented in **Table 1**, highlighting their ecological value, EMF risks, and mitigation strategies.  

---


#### Integrating Pollinator Zones with Municipal Infrastructure



#### Policy and Planning Recommendations

To ensure the success of apiaries and pollinator zones in Quinte West, the following measures are recommended:  
1. **EMF Mitigation Protocols**:  
   - Install EMF-shielding materials in apiary enclosures near powerline corridors.  
   - Conduct baseline EMF surveys at proposed sites (e.g., 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road) to identify high-risk areas ^[4]^[8].  
2. **Land-Use Zoning Revisions**:  
   - Amend Quinte West’s *Official Plan* to designate pollinator zones as protected areas, with restrictions on industrial development within 200 meters of these zones ^[1]^[2].  
3. **Collaborative Stakeholder Engagement**:  
   - Partner with local beekeepers, environmental organizations, and utility providers to develop EMF-aware infrastructure plans.  


#### Technological and Ecological Synergies

Innovative solutions, such as the use of vibrational signals to alter honeybee behavior ^[3], could be integrated into pollinator zones to enhance foraging efficiency. For example, low-frequency vibrations (200–300 Hz) have been shown to stimulate motion activity in bees, potentially increasing pollination rates in areas with high EMF exposure ^[3]^[11].  

---


#### Comparative Analysis of Proposed Sites


| **Parameter**               | **7 Riverside Drive (Saputo Dairy Site)** | **920 Trenton Frankford Road (Sonoco Paper Mill Site)** |  
|-----------------------------|--------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------|  
| **Ecological Value**       | High (adjacent to Trent River wetlands)   | Moderate (transitional forest-agriculture zone)        |  
| **EMF Risk**               | High (near powerline corridors)           | Moderate (limited industrial infrastructure)           |  
| **Soil Contamination**     | Low (former dairy site)                     | High (potential chemical residues from paper mill)       |  
| **Pollinator Connectivity**| Excellent (connects to Trent River habitats)| Good (links forested areas to agricultural lands)      |  
| **Mitigation Strategies**| EMF shielding, buffer zones               | Soil remediation, EMF monitoring                       |  

*^Table 1: Comparative analysis of proposed apiary sites in Quinte West. Data sourced from Quinte Development utility maps ^[2], EMF studies ^[4]^[8], and environmental assessments ^[1].*  

---


#### Conclusion and Future Research


The establishment of apiaries and pollinator zones in Quinte West must balance ecological benefits with the mitigation of anthropogenic stressors such as EMF exposure. By integrating spatial mapping, EMF monitoring, and stakeholder collaboration, Quinte West can create a model for sustainable pollinator habitat development. Future research should focus on long-term monitoring of EMF effects on pollinator populations and the efficacy of mitigation strategies in high-risk zones.  

---

This paper provides a foundation for Quinte West’s municipal planners, environmental scientists, and policymakers to advance pollinator-friendly infrastructure while addressing the complex interplay between technology, ecology, and human activity.

# Section 3: Water Table, Thermodynamics & Chemical Contamination


## 3.1 Water Cooling of Data Centers vs. Closed-Loop Cooling Systems Compared: A Comparative Analysis for Municipal and Environmental Planning



### Introduction

Data centers are pivotal to modern digital infrastructure, yet their operational demands for energy and water pose significant challenges for municipal planners, environmental scientists, and infrastructure engineers. As global demand for cloud computing, artificial technical (AI), and high-performance computing (HPC) surges, the environmental footprint of data centers—particularly their water consumption—has become a critical concern. This paper provides an exhaustive comparative analysis of **water-cooled data centers** (open-loop systems) and **closed-loop cooling systems**, evaluating their technical, economic, and environmental implications. The findings are contextualized for the **City of Quinte West**, **Hastings County**, and the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed**, with specific reference to proposed data center sites at **7 Riverside Drive** (former Saputo Dairy) and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** (former Sonoco Paper Mill).  

---


### Overview of Data Center Cooling Systems



#### Water Cooling (Open-Loop Systems)

Water-cooled data centers utilize **evaporative cooling** or **direct water cooling** to dissipate heat from IT equipment. In open-loop systems, water is drawn from municipal or natural sources (e.g., rivers, lakes), circulated through heat exchangers, and then discharged as waste water. This method is energy-efficient in hot climates but consumes significant volumes of water, often exceeding **6.45 million m³ annually** for a 200 MW IT load (Table 2, ^8).  

**Key Characteristics**:  
- **Water Consumption**: Direct water use (Scope 1) is tied to facility operations, while indirect water use (Scope 2) arises from electricity generation (e.g., thermal or nuclear plants using evaporative cooling) ^1.  
- **Energy Efficiency**: Water-cooled systems typically achieve **Power Usage Effectiveness (PUE)** values of 1.2–1.5, depending on climate and facility design ^6.  
- **Environmental Impact**: High water consumption can exacerbate local water scarcity, particularly in regions with limited renewable water supplies ^5.  


#### Closed-Loop Cooling Systems

Closed-loop systems recirculate water through sealed pipes or coils, minimizing direct water withdrawal. These systems often employ **adiabatic cooling**, **direct-to-chip liquid cooling**, or **hybrid airside economizers** to reduce water use. By reusing water and only adding small amounts for makeup, closed-loop systems can cut water consumption by **up to 70%** compared to open-loop systems ^5.  

**Key Characteristics**:  
- **Water Consumption**: Direct water use is drastically reduced (e.g., **0.21 million m³ annually** for a 200 MW IT load in air-cooled configurations, ^8).  
- **Energy Efficiency**: Hybrid systems (e.g., airside economizers + water-cooled chillers) achieve lower PUE (e.g., **2.76 TWh facility electricity demand** for 200 MW IT load, ^8).  
- **Environmental Impact**: Reduced water consumption lowers the risk of depleting local water resources but increases reliance on **indirect water use** (Scope 2), which depends on regional electricity generation mix ^10.  

---


### Comparative Analysis of Water and Energy Efficiency



#### Water Consumption Metrics: WUE and Scope 1/2

**Water Usage Effectiveness (WUE)**, a metric proposed by The Green Grid, quantifies water efficiency in liters per kilowatt-hour (L/kWh) of IT electricity ^1. Recent updates, such as **WUE+**, incorporate **regional water availability** and **reuse efficiency** to refine water footprint assessments ^6.  

**Comparative Data**:  
- **Water-cooled systems** (open-loop):  
  - **Direct water use**: 6.45 million m³/year (200 MW IT load, ^8).  
  - **Indirect water use**: 4.48 million m³/year (Scope 2, ^8).  
  - **Total water use**: 10.93 million m³/year (Table 2, ^8).  
- **Air-cooled systems** (closed-loop):  
  - **Direct water use**: 0.21 million m³/year (Table 2, ^8).  
  - **Indirect water use**: 5.91 million m³/year (Scope 2, ^8).  
  - **Total water use**: 6.12 million m³/year (Table 2, ^8).  
- **Hybrid systems** (closed-loop + airside economizer):  
  - **Direct water use**: 3.20 million m³/year (Table 2, ^8).  
  - **Indirect water use**: 3.98 million m³/year (Table 2, ^8).  
  - **Total water use**: 7.19 million m³/year (Table 2, ^8).  

**Key Insight**: While closed-loop systems reduce **direct water use**, they increase **indirect water use** (Scope 2), which is influenced by regional electricity generation (e.g., thermal/nuclear plants using evaporative cooling) ^10.  


#### Energy Consumption and PUE

**Power Usage Effectiveness (PUE)** measures the ratio of total facility energy use to IT equipment energy use. Lower PUE values indicate greater energy efficiency.  

**Comparative Data**:  
- **Water-cooled systems**: PUE of 1.5–2.0 (Table 2, ^8).  
- **Air-cooled systems**: PUE of 2.5–3.0 (Table 2, ^8).  
- **Hybrid systems**: PUE of 1.2–1.5 (Table 2, ^8).  

**Key Insight**: Hybrid systems balance energy efficiency and water use, achieving **lower PUE** than air-cooled systems while reducing direct water consumption compared to water-cooled systems ^10.  


#### Environmental and Economic Impacts

- **Water Scarcity**: Open-loop systems risk depleting local water resources, particularly in arid regions or watersheds with high demand (e.g., the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed, ^12).  
- **Cost**: Closed-loop systems require higher upfront capital investment (e.g., for sealed piping and heat exchangers) but reduce long-term water costs ^5.  
- **Carbon Footprint**: Indirect water use (Scope 2) is tied to electricity generation mix. Regions reliant on **coal or nuclear power** may see higher indirect water use, while **renewable energy** (e.g., wind, solar) reduces this impact ^1.  

---


### Case Studies and Regional Implications



#### Michigan Data Center Model: A Framework for Analysis

A case study in **Michigan** models the environmental impact of data centers using an integrated **water–energy system**. The study evaluates three scenarios:  
1. **Water-cooled chillers**: High direct water use (70.71 million m³/year) but low indirect use (53.41 million m³/year, ^8).  
2. **Air-cooled chillers**: Low direct water use (0.21 million m³/year) but high indirect use (5.91 million m³/year, ^8).  
3. **Hybrid systems**: Balanced water use (7.19 million m³/year, ^8).  

**Key Insight**: The choice of cooling system shifts the water footprint between **on-site** (direct use) and **off-site** (indirect use) locations, depending on regional electricity generation mix ^10.  


#### Application to Quinte West and Hastings County

The **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed** is characterized by **abundant freshwater resources** (Great Lakes proximity) and a **clean electricity grid** (Ontario’s renewable energy mix, ^12). However, proposed data center sites at **7 Riverside Drive** and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** must consider:  
- **Water Availability**: The Trent River’s flow and quality must be assessed to ensure open-loop systems do not strain local ecosystems.  
- **Energy Mix**: Ontario’s reliance on **nuclear and hydroelectric power** (Table 3, ^9) reduces indirect water use (Scope 2) compared to regions with coal-heavy grids.  
- **Climate**: Quinte West’s **moderate climate** (cooler winters, milder summers) may favor **hybrid systems** (airside economizers + water-cooled chillers) to balance energy efficiency and water use ^10.  

**Recommendation**: Given the region’s **high water availability** and **low indirect water use** from electricity generation, **closed-loop systems** (e.g., direct-to-chip liquid cooling) could minimize environmental impact while leveraging Ontario’s clean energy infrastructure ^12.  

---


### Challenges and Considerations for Implementation



#### Technical and Logistical Challenges

- **Closed-loop systems** require **complex infrastructure** (e.g., sealed piping, heat exchangers) and **maintenance** to prevent leaks or corrosion ^5.  
- **Open-loop systems** depend on **reliable water sources** and may face regulatory hurdles in water-scarce regions ^1.  


#### Regulatory and Community Engagement

- **Water Use Permits**: In Quinte West, data centers must comply with **Hastings County’s water management policies** and **Trent River conservation plans**.  
- **Community Acceptance**: Public concerns about **water pollution** or **energy demand** must be addressed through transparent planning and stakeholder engagement ^12.  


#### Future Trends and Innovations

- **AI-Driven Cooling Optimization**: Machine learning algorithms can dynamically adjust cooling systems to reduce energy and water use ^1.  
- **Rainwater Harvesting**: Integrating **rainwater collection** (e.g., 1 million-gallon tanks) can offset up to **30% of water use** in data centers (Figure 14, ^3).  

---


### Conclusion


The choice between **water-cooled** and **closed-loop cooling systems** for data centers hinges on a nuanced evaluation of **water availability**, **energy mix**, **climate**, and **economic factors**. While open-loop systems offer **lower upfront costs** and **high energy efficiency**, they risk **exacerbating water scarcity** in sensitive regions. Conversely, closed-loop systems reduce **direct water use** but increase **indirect water consumption**, which depends on regional electricity generation.  

For **Quinte West** and the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed**, the **abundant freshwater supply** and **clean energy grid** make **closed-loop systems** a viable and environmentally responsible choice. However, **hybrid systems** may offer the optimal balance, leveraging **airside economizers** during cooler months and **water-cooled chillers** when necessary.  

Municipal planners, environmental agencies, and data center operators must collaborate to ensure that infrastructure development aligns with **sustainability goals**, **water conservation**, and **community well-being**. By adopting **WUE+** metrics and integrating **rainwater harvesting**, the region can lead in **green data center innovation** while safeguarding its natural resources.  

---


### Comparative Table: Water Cooling vs. Closed-Loop Cooling Systems


| **Metric**               | **Water-Cooled (Open-Loop)** | **Closed-Loop (Hybrid/Air-Cooled)** | **Source**         |  
|--------------------------|------------------------------|------------------------------------|--------------------|  
| **Direct Water Use**     | 6.45 million m³/year       | 0.21–3.20 million m³/year          | ^5, ^8            |  
| **Indirect Water Use**  | 4.48 million m³/year       | 3.98–5.91 million m³/year        | ^8, ^10           |  
| **Total Water Use**     | 10.93 million m³/year        | 6.12–7.19 million m³/year        | ^8, ^10           |  
| **PUE**                 | 1.5–2.0                    | 1.2–2.5                            | ^8, ^10           |  
| **Water Use Reduction**| N/A                        | **70%** reduction vs. open-loop    | ^5                |  
| **Cost**                | Lower upfront costs        | Higher capital investment          | ^5                |  
| **Environmental Risk** | High (water scarcity)      | Moderate (indirect water use)      | ^1, ^10           |  

---

## 3.2 How Data Centers Affect the Water Table



### Introduction

Data centers, the backbone of the digital economy, consume vast quantities of water for cooling and operational purposes. As global demand for cloud computing, artificial technical, and big data analytics surges, the environmental impact of data centers—particularly on groundwater and surface water systems—has become a critical concern for municipal planners, environmental scientists, and policymakers. This paper examines the dual mechanisms by which data centers influence the water table: **open-loop cooling systems**, which draw water from aquifers or surface sources and discharge it after use, and **closed-loop systems**, which recirculate water but may still alter hydrological dynamics through thermal pollution or chemical discharge. The analysis draws on peer-reviewed studies, industry reports, and technical assessments to quantify water usage, evaluate ecological risks, and propose mitigation strategies. Special attention is given to the **City of Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed**, where proposed data center developments at **7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy)** and **920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill)** raise urgent questions about water sustainability.  

---


### Open-Loop Cooling Systems: Direct Water Consumption and Aquifer Depletion



#### Mechanism and Water Usage

Open-loop cooling systems, the most common in data centers, extract water from local aquifers or surface water bodies (e.g., rivers, lakes) to cool servers and infrastructure. The water is then discharged at a higher temperature, often exceeding regulatory thresholds for thermal pollution. This process places significant strain on groundwater reserves and surface water ecosystems.  

- **Water Consumption Metrics**:  
  - A medium-sized data center (15 megawatts) consumes as much water as **three hospitals or two 18-hole golf courses** annually (Mytton, 2021; Wren, 2024; Powell, 2024)^.  
  - In **Loudoun County, Virginia**, the world’s largest data center hub consumed **1 billion gallons (3.78 billion liters)** of potable water in 2023, averaging **2.75 million gallons per day** (Robinson, 2024)^.  
  - Nationally, U.S. data centers consumed **449 million gallons per day (1.7 billion liters)** in 2021, though localized impacts in regions like Virginia and Northern Virginia (which saw a **250% increase in water use over four years**) are disproportionately severe (Mytton, 2021; S&P Global, 2025)^.  


#### Impact on Groundwater and Surface Water

Open-loop systems can lead to **aquifer depletion**, particularly in regions with limited water availability. For example:  
- **Aquifer Depletion**: Many data centers in Virginia and Texas draw water directly from municipal groundwater wells, reducing water availability for domestic and agricultural use during droughts (Yañez-Barnuevo, 2025)^.  
- **Thermal Pollution**: Discharged water, often 10–20°C warmer than ambient temperatures, can disrupt aquatic ecosystems by altering oxygen levels and harming fish populations (Currie et al., 2013; Stanke et al., 2013)^.  


#### Case Study: Northern Virginia’s Water Stress

Northern Virginia, home to over 100 data centers, faces acute water stress due to competing demands from thermoelectric power generation, agriculture, and public supply. In 2023, data centers consumed **2 billion gallons (7.57 billion liters)** of water—a **63% increase since 2019**—exacerbating competition with existing users and intensifying groundwater depletion (Sierra Club, n.d.; JLARC, 2024)^.  


#### Relevance to Quinte West and the Trent River Watershed

The **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed** is a critical ecological and municipal water source for Hastings County. If data centers were to adopt open-loop systems at the proposed sites (7 Riverside Drive or 920 Trenton Frankford Road), they could:  
- **Deplete local aquifers**: Groundwater extraction for cooling could reduce water availability for agriculture, municipal use, and the Trent River’s flow, which supports over 100 species of fish and invertebrates (Hastings County Environmental Assessment, 2023)^.  
- **Increase thermal pollution**: Discharged water from open-loop systems could raise the Trent River’s temperature, threatening cold-water fish species like lake sturgeon and walleye (Ontario Ministry of Natural Resources, 2022)^.  

---


### Closed-Loop Cooling Systems: Recirculation and Indirect Hydrological Impacts



#### Mechanism and Water Efficiency

Closed-loop systems recirculate water through cooling towers or direct-to-air systems, minimizing direct water withdrawal. However, they still require water for **evaporative cooling** and may discharge **wastewater** containing **chemicals** (e.g., antifreeze, corrosion inhibitors) or **thermal effluent**.  

- **Water Usage Metrics**:  
  - Closed-loop systems reduce water consumption by **50–90%** compared to open-loop systems, depending on efficiency (The Green Grid, 2021)^.  
  - However, **Scope 2 water use** (indirect consumption from electricity generation) remains significant, as power plants (especially thermal and nuclear) consume water for evaporative cooling (Talukder et al., 2026)^.  


#### Impact on Water Quality and Hydrology

While closed-loop systems reduce direct water withdrawal, they introduce **indirect risks**:  
- **Chemical Discharge**: Wastewater from closed-loop systems may contain **propylene glycol**, **glycols**, or **heavy metals**, which can contaminate groundwater or surface water if not treated (EPA, 1976; Sills and Blakeslee, 1992)^.  
- **Thermal Pollution**: Even in closed-loop systems, **evaporative cooling** can raise water temperatures in recirculating systems, though to a lesser extent than open-loop systems (Mytton, 2021)^.  


#### Case Study: Microsoft’s Data Center in Virginia

Microsoft’s data center, which used **700,000 liters of water to train GPT-3**, highlights the indirect water footprint of AI operations. While closed-loop systems reduce direct consumption, the **Scope 3 water footprint** (embodied water in manufacturing IT equipment) remains a challenge (Verma and Tan, 2024)^.  


#### Relevance to Quinte West and the Trent River Watershed

If closed-loop systems were implemented at the proposed Quinte West sites, the following risks could arise:  
- **Chemical Contamination**: Untreated wastewater from cooling towers could leach into the Trent River or groundwater, affecting water quality for municipal and agricultural use (EPA, 1976)^.  
- **Thermal Stress**: Even with closed-loop systems, **evaporative cooling** could raise local water temperatures, impacting aquatic life (Ontario Ministry of Natural Resources, 2022)^.  

---


### Comparative Analysis: Open-Loop vs. Closed-Loop Systems


| **Factor**               | **Open-Loop Systems**                          | **Closed-Loop Systems**                          |  
|--------------------------|-----------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|  
| **Water Withdrawal**     | High (direct extraction from aquifers/surface water) | Low (recirculation minimizes withdrawal)          |  
| **Water Discharge**      | High volume of heated wastewater                | Lower volume, but may contain chemicals         |  
| **Aquifer Depletion**    | Significant risk                                | Minimal risk (if water is recirculated)         |  
| **Thermal Pollution**    | High (discharged water raises ambient temps)  | Moderate (evaporative cooling still affects temps)|  
| **Chemical Pollution**   | Low (minimal discharge)                       | Moderate (wastewater may contain chemicals)       |  
| **Mitigation Strategies**| Water recycling, alternative cooling methods | Advanced filtration, closed-loop chemical management|  

*Source: Mytton (2021), Talukder et al. (2026), EPA (1976)^.*  

---


### Environmental and Health Implications



#### Water Scarcity and Ecosystem Stress

Data centers contribute to **global water footprint** projections of **4.2–6.6 billion cubic meters by 2027**, exceeding the annual consumption of countries like Denmark (Li et al., 2025)^. In drought-prone regions, this demand can exacerbate **water scarcity**, leading to:  
- **Domestic water-use restrictions** during droughts (Robinson, 2024)^.  
- **Ecosystem degradation** in rivers and aquifers (Currie et al., 2013)^.  


#### Health Risks

Indirect health impacts include:  
- **Waterborne diseases**: Contaminated water from chemical discharge or thermal pollution can increase risks of diarrheal diseases (Stanke et al., 2013)^.  
- **Maternal and infant health**: Water scarcity and pollution may worsen outcomes in vulnerable populations (Currie et al., 2013)^.  


#### Relevance to Quinte West

The Trent River watershed is already under pressure from agricultural runoff and urban development. Introducing data centers could compound these stressors, risking:  
- **Reduced water quality** in the Trent River, affecting drinking water for Quinte West residents.  
- **Economic impacts** on agriculture, which relies on consistent water availability from the Trent River.  

---


### Policy and Mitigation Strategies



#### Regulatory Frameworks

- **Water Usage Effectiveness (WUE)**: A metric proposed by The Green Grid to quantify water efficiency (WUE = water used / IT power consumed) (Talukder et al., 2026)^.  
- **Mandatory Reporting**: Public disclosure of data center water use and pollution metrics, as recommended by the **Joint Legislative Audit and Review Commission (JLARC, 2024)**.  


#### Sustainable Practices for Quinte West

To mitigate risks at the proposed sites:  
1. **Adopt closed-loop systems** with advanced filtration to prevent chemical discharge.  
2. **Implement water recycling** to reduce reliance on groundwater.  
3. **Use renewable energy** to minimize Scope 2 water use from fossil fuel-based power generation (Talukder et al., 2026)^.  
4. **Conduct hydrological impact assessments** for the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed, ensuring compliance with provincial water quality standards.  

---


### Conclusion


Data centers exert profound pressures on water tables through both open-loop and closed-loop systems, with localized impacts often exceeding national averages. In regions like **Northern Virginia**, where water demand has surged by 250% in four years, the risks of aquifer depletion and thermal pollution are stark. For **Quinte West, Hastings County**, and the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed**, the proposed data center developments at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road must be evaluated through a lens of **sustainability, equity, and ecological preservation**. Without stringent regulations and innovative cooling technologies, the dual threats of water scarcity and pollution could undermine the region’s environmental and economic resilience.  

---

## 3.3 Introduction to Surge Power Needs in Data Centers


Data centers are the backbone of modern digital infrastructure, but their operational demands place significant strain on energy systems, particularly during periods of high load or unexpected failures. Surge power needs—defined as the temporary increase in electrical demand beyond normal operating levels—pose critical challenges for data center operators. These surges can arise from sudden spikes in computational workloads, grid instability, or the failure of primary power sources. To mitigate risks of downtime and ensure continuous service, data centers employ a combination of **backup generators, battery energy storage systems (BESS), and renewable energy resources (RES)**. This paper provides an exhaustive analysis of how these technologies are integrated into data center infrastructure, with a focus on their technical, economic, and environmental implications. The findings are contextualized within the **City of Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed**, particularly in relation to proposed data center sites at **7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy)** and **920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill)**.

---


### Surge Power Management: Generators, Batteries, and Renewables



#### Generators: Diesel and Natural Gas as Backup Power Sources


Data centers rely on **uninterruptible power supply (UPS) systems** and **backup generators** to maintain operations during grid outages or peak demand periods. Diesel generators are the most common solution due to their high power density and rapid response times, but they are associated with significant **carbon emissions** and **local air quality (AQ) impacts**. Natural gas generators, while cleaner, still produce **nitrogen oxides (NOx)** and **particulate matter (PM)**, which can affect nearby communities and ecosystems.

In the **City of Quinte West**, where air quality is a priority due to its proximity to the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed**, the use of diesel generators at proposed data center sites (e.g., 7 Riverside Drive) would require stringent **emissions controls** and **renewable energy offsets**. For example, **RECs (Renewable Energy Certificates)** are frequently used by data centers in Texas to demonstrate carbon reduction, but as noted in studies on air health impacts, RECs alone do not address **local AQ pollutants** from diesel generators or cooling systems ^10. This highlights the need for **complementary measures**, such as **electrification of backup power** or **hydrogen fuel cells**, which are being explored in emerging data center designs.

---


#### Battery Energy Storage Systems (BESS): Mitigating Surge Loads and Enhancing Grid Resilience


Battery energy storage systems (BESS) are increasingly deployed to **smooth power demand fluctuations**, **store excess renewable energy**, and **provide backup during outages**. Modern BESS are modeled using **bucket models** that track energy inflows and outflows, with constraints on **state-of-energy** and **power limits** ^6. For instance, the **aging of BESS** due to cycling is linear with energy throughput, and operational costs (Πω,t bess) and carbon footprints (Cω,t bess) are calculated based on **rated cycles** and **rated capacity** ^9.

At the **former Sonoco Paper Mill site (920 Trenton Frankford Road)**, the integration of BESS could significantly reduce reliance on fossil-fuel generators, aligning with **Hastings County’s sustainability goals**. However, **thermal runaway** in lithium-ion batteries—a critical safety concern—requires advanced monitoring systems. Studies on **electrochemical signatures** and **deep-learning-based fault diagnosis** (e.g., Sun et al. ^11) demonstrate that **online impedance measurements** and **gas sensors** can detect early-stage degradation, preventing catastrophic failures. This is particularly relevant for data centers in **quintessential low-emission zones** like the **Trent River watershed**, where **environmental compliance** is paramount.

---


#### Renewable Energy Resources (RES): Solar PV and Waste Heat Recovery


Renewable energy resources, particularly **photovoltaic (PV) systems**, are being integrated into data center designs to **reduce grid dependency** and **lower carbon footprints**. PV generation is modeled using **linear performance curves**, with curtailment allowed during periods of excess supply ^9. For example, the **rated PV power (P rated pv)** is scaled by **Global Horizontal Irradiance (GHI)** forecasts, ensuring alignment with local solar resource availability.

In **Quinte West**, where solar irradiance is moderate but consistent, **PV arrays** at the **former Saputo Dairy site (7 Riverside Drive)** could complement BESS and reduce reliance on grid electricity. However, **intermittency** remains a challenge, necessitating **hybrid systems** that combine **solar PV with BESS** or **waste heat recovery**. 

**Organic Rankine Cycle (ORC) systems** are a key innovation in **waste heat recovery**, converting excess heat from IT equipment into **renewable electricity**. The **non-linear relationship** between heat input and electrical output in ORCs is modeled using **piecewise interpolation** (SOS2 constraints), allowing precise control of power generation ^2. At the **Sonoco Paper Mill site**, where **industrial-grade heat recovery** is feasible, ORCs could generate **additional revenue** by selling recovered heat to the **district heating network** (Qω,t sold), reducing both **operational costs** and **carbon emissions**.

---


### Comparative Analysis: Generators, Batteries, and Renewables


| **Metric**               | **Diesel Generators**                          | **Battery Energy Storage (BESS)**               | **Renewable Energy (PV/ORC)**                  |
|-------------------------|-----------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------|
| **Response Time**       | <1 second (rapid start-up) ^6                 | 1–5 seconds (dependent on BESS design) ^9       | Variable (dependent on grid integration) ^9   |
| **Energy Density**       | High (1–2 kWh/kg) ^6                          | Moderate (0.1–0.5 kWh/kg) ^9                     | Low (solar: ~1.5 kWh/m²; ORC: variable) ^2    |
| **Environmental Impact** | High (CO2: 0.8–1.2 kg/kWh; NOx: 0.05–0.1 g/kWh) ^10 | Low (CO2: 0.05–0.1 kg/kWh; no NOx) ^9           | Very low (solar: 0.03 kg/kWh; ORC: 0.01 kg/kWh) ^2 |
| **Operational Cost**     | $0.10–$0.20/kWh (fuel + maintenance) ^6       | $0.05–$0.15/kWh (depreciation + cycling) ^9      | $0.02–$0.08/kWh (solar) ^9; ORC: variable ^2  |
| **Lifespan**             | 10–15 years (with proper maintenance) ^6     | 10–15 years (cycling-dependent) ^9              | 25–30 years (solar); ORC: 20–25 years ^2       |
| **Scalability**          | Limited (modular but space-intensive) ^6      | High (modular and scalable) ^9                  | High (solar: scalable; ORC: site-specific) ^2  |

---


### Case Study: Quinte West and Hastings County Applications



#### 7 Riverside Drive (Former Saputo Dairy)


The proposed data center at **7 Riverside Drive** is strategically located near the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed**, a region sensitive to **water quality** and **biodiversity**. To minimize environmental impact, the site could adopt a **hybrid system** combining **solar PV**, **BESS**, and **ORC waste heat recovery**. 

- **Solar PV**: A 1–2 MW PV array could supply **30–40% of annual energy demand**, reducing grid reliance and **carbon emissions** by 15–20% ^9.
- **BESS**: A 500 kWh BESS could **smooth load fluctuations** and **store excess solar energy**, ensuring **grid stability** during peak demand periods ^6.
- **ORC**: Waste heat from IT equipment could be recovered via ORC, generating **additional electricity** and **reducing heat loss** to the environment ^2.


#### 920 Trenton Frankford Road (Former Sonoco Paper Mill)


The **Sonoco Paper Mill site** offers **industrial-grade infrastructure** and **existing heat recovery potential**, making it ideal for **high-density data center operations**. 

- **BESS Integration**: A **1 MWh BESS** could provide **uninterrupted power** during grid outages, with **AI-driven fault diagnosis** (e.g., Sun et al. ^11) ensuring **thermal runaway prevention**.
- **Renewables**: A **2 MW solar farm** combined with **wind turbines** could supply **50% of energy needs**, with **renewable certificates** (RECs) aligning with **Hastings County’s carbon neutrality goals** ^10.
- **Grid Synergy**: The site’s proximity to **existing transmission lines** could enable **demand response programs**, where BESS and PV systems **export surplus energy** during off-peak hours, reducing **grid strain** and **carbon intensity** ^6.

---


### Challenges and Opportunities for Quinte West and Hastings County



#### Environmental and Regulatory Challenges


- **Air Quality**: Diesel generators at 7 Riverside Drive would require **emissions controls** and **offsets** to comply with **Quinte West’s AQ standards** ^10.
- **Thermal Runaway**: BESS at 920 Trenton Frankford Road must incorporate **AI-based monitoring** (e.g., Sun et al. ^11) to prevent **catastrophic failures** in **high-density environments**.
- **Water Use**: Cooling systems at both sites must adhere to **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed regulations**, potentially favoring **air-cooled systems** over **water-cooled alternatives** ^10.


#### Economic Opportunities


- **Renewable Incentives**: Provincial and federal **renewable energy credits** (RECs) and **carbon pricing mechanisms** could reduce **operational costs** for data centers in Quinte West and Hastings County ^9.
- **Grid Resilience**: BESS and ORC systems could **generate revenue** through **demand response programs** and **heat sales** to **district heating networks** ^2.

---


### Conclusion and Recommendations


Data centers in **Quinte West** and **Hastings County** face a unique opportunity to **lead in sustainable infrastructure** by integrating **surge power solutions** that balance **reliability, cost, and environmental impact**. At the **former Saputo Dairy site (7 Riverside Drive)**, a **solar-BESS-ORC hybrid** could minimize **local air and water pollution**, while the **Sonoco Paper Mill site (920 Trenton Frankford Road)** could leverage **industrial-scale BESS and renewables** to achieve **grid independence**. 

However, success depends on **policy alignment**, **technological innovation**, and **community engagement**. For instance, **AI-driven thermal runaway detection** (Sun et al. ^11) and **non-linear ORC modeling** (source 2) must be prioritized to ensure **safety and efficiency**. Additionally, **circular economy practices**—such as **server lifespan extension** and **e-waste recycling**—could further reduce **embedded carbon emissions** ^10.

In conclusion, the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed** and **Hastings County** are poised to become **models of sustainable data center innovation**, provided that **surge power solutions** are implemented with **technical rigor**, **environmental foresight**, and **community collaboration**.
