## 3.4 How Hydrocarbon-Based Generators from Data Centers Affect the Local Environment: A Case Study of Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte Watershed



### Introduction


The proliferation of data centers, driven by the global demand for cloud computing, artificial technical, and digital infrastructure, has led to an increased reliance on hydrocarbon-based generators for backup power. These generators, typically fueled by diesel, are critical for maintaining "five nines" (99.999% uptime) in data center operations, ensuring continuous service even during grid outages ^12^. However, the environmental implications of such reliance are profound, particularly in regions like Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed, where ecological sensitivity and population density intersect with proposed data center developments. This paper examines the environmental impacts of hydrocarbon-based generators from data centers, drawing on authoritative evidence from Canadian air pollutant emissions inventories, health risk assessments, and case studies of diesel generator usage in critical infrastructure.  


### Emissions Profile of Hydrocarbon-Based Generators


Hydrocarbon-based generators, particularly those powered by diesel, emit a range of pollutants, including nitrogen oxides (NOX), volatile organic compounds (VOCs), fine particulate matter (PM2.5), and black carbon. These pollutants are well-documented in Canada’s Air Pollutant Emissions Inventory (2026), which highlights the sectoral contributions of various industries to national emissions ^5^. While the oil and gas industry remains the largest contributor to NOX emissions (37% of total national emissions in 2024) ^5^, the role of data centers and their backup generators is increasingly relevant, especially in regions with high data center density.  


#### Key Pollutants and Their Sources


1. **Nitrogen Oxides (NOX)**:  
   - NOX emissions are primarily generated through high-temperature combustion processes, such as those in diesel engines. In 2024, the oil and gas industry accounted for 37% of total NOX emissions in Canada, with a 30% increase between 1990 and 2024 ^5^. While data centers are not explicitly listed as a major source in national inventories, their diesel generators could contribute to localized NOX spikes, particularly during backup power activation.  

2. **Volatile Organic Compounds (VOCs)**:  
   - VOCs are emitted during the combustion of hydrocarbons and are a precursor to ground-level ozone formation. The decline in VOC emissions since 2014 has been attributed to reduced venting from crude oil production and improved fugitive emission controls ^8^. However, diesel generators in data centers may offset these reductions if not equipped with advanced emission control technologies.  

3. **Fine Particulate Matter (PM2.5)**:  
   - PM2.5 emissions from diesel combustion are a significant concern due to their ability to penetrate deep into the respiratory system. In 2024, PM2.5 emissions increased by 6 kt (4%) in Quebec, 1 kt (40%) in Yukon, and 5 kt (154%) in the Northwest Territories and Nunavut combined ^4^. While these increases are attributed to industrial and transportation activities, the localized impact of data center generators in regions like Quinte West could exacerbate PM2.5 concentrations.  

4. **Black Carbon**:  
   - Black carbon, a component of PM2.5, is emitted through incomplete combustion and has a potent warming effect on the climate. Alberta, Saskatchewan, Ontario, and Quebec accounted for the majority of black carbon emissions in 2024, with transportation and off-road equipment being the primary sources ^2^. Diesel generators in data centers, if not optimized for combustion efficiency, could contribute to black carbon emissions in localized areas.  


### Environmental and Health Impacts


The environmental and health impacts of hydrocarbon-based generator emissions are well-documented in scientific literature and public health assessments. Exposure to pollutants such as NOX, VOCs, PM2.5, and black carbon is linked to respiratory diseases, cardiovascular issues, and premature mortality ^3^.  


#### Health Risks and Vulnerable Populations


- **Respiratory and Cardiovascular Effects**:  
  Fine particulate matter (PM2.5) and nitrogen dioxide (NO2) are strongly associated with increased hospital admissions for asthma, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD), and heart failure. In Canada, extensive studies have shown that PM2.5 and NO2 are key contributors to smog formation, which disproportionately affects children, the elderly, and individuals with preexisting health conditions ^3^.  

- **Climate Forcing**:  
  Black carbon emissions from diesel generators contribute to climate change by absorbing solar radiation and reducing albedo (reflectivity) of snow and ice. In regions like the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed, where ecological systems are sensitive to temperature changes, such emissions could accelerate local climate impacts ^2^.  


#### Ecological Impacts


- **Aquatic Ecosystems**:  
  The Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed is a critical habitat for fish species, including the endangered Lake Sturgeon. Airborne pollutants such as NOX and VOCs can deposit into water bodies, leading to eutrophication and acidification, which harm aquatic life ^3^.  

- **Biodiversity Loss**:  
  Increased PM2.5 and black carbon emissions from data center generators could degrade air quality in Quinte West and Hastings County, affecting plant and animal species reliant on clean air and stable ecosystems.  


### Case Study: Quinte West, Hastings County, and Proposed Data Center Sites


The proposed data center developments at 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) and 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill) in Quinte West, Hastings County, present a unique case for analyzing the localized environmental impacts of hydrocarbon-based generators.  


#### Geographical and Ecological Context


- **Trent River/Bay of Quinte Watershed**:  
  This watershed is a vital ecological corridor, supporting diverse flora and fauna. The Trent River flows into the Bay of Quinte, which is part of Lake Ontario’s ecosystem. The region’s proximity to the Great Lakes makes it particularly sensitive to air and water pollution ^3^.  

- **Quinte West and Hastings County**:  
  These areas are characterized by a mix of urban, agricultural, and natural landscapes. The proposed data center sites are located near residential areas and the Trent River, raising concerns about localized air quality degradation and potential contamination of water resources ^12^.  


#### Emissions Scenario Analysis


If the proposed data centers at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road are equipped with diesel generators, the following emissions scenarios could occur:  

1. **Localized NOX and PM2.5 Spikes**:  
   - Diesel generators, operating at high load during grid outages, could emit NOX and PM2.5 at levels exceeding local air quality standards. For example, in 2024, Alberta’s transportation sector contributed 16% of its total NOX emissions ^2^. If similar patterns occur in Quinte West, localized NOX concentrations could rise, affecting nearby communities.  

2. **Black Carbon Deposition**:  
   - Black carbon emissions from diesel generators could deposit on the Trent River’s surface, reducing albedo and contributing to warming. This could disrupt aquatic ecosystems and accelerate the melting of ice in nearby wetlands ^2^.  

3. **Health Risks for Residents**:  
   - Residents of Quinte West and Hastings County, including vulnerable populations such as children and the elderly, could face increased health risks from prolonged exposure to PM2.5 and NO2. Studies in Canada have shown that even small increases in PM2.5 concentrations are associated with significant health burdens ^3^.  


### Comparative Analysis of Emissions Sources


To contextualize the potential impact of data center generators, a comparative analysis of emissions sources in Canada is presented below.  

| **Pollutant**       | **Major Source**                          | **Contribution to National Emissions (2024)** | **Relevance to Data Center Generators** |  
|----------------------|------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|  
| **NOX**              | Oil and Gas Industry                     | 37% (447 kt) ^5^                                | Potential contributor if generators are not optimized |  
| **PM2.5**            | Transportation and Industrial Activities | 210.4 kt (Ontario) ^4^                          | Could increase localized concentrations |  
| **VOCs**             | Crude Oil Production and Refining        | Declined by 30% since 2014 ^8^                 | May offset reductions if generators are not controlled |  
| **Black Carbon**     | Transportation and Off-Road Equipment    | Alberta: 42% from off-road vehicles ^2^        | Potential contributor in localized areas |  


### Mitigation Strategies and Policy Recommendations


To minimize the environmental impact of hydrocarbon-based generators in data centers, the following strategies are recommended:  


#### Adoption of Cleaner Fuels

   - Transitioning from diesel to cleaner alternatives such as biodiesel or hydrogen could significantly reduce NOX, PM2.5, and black carbon emissions. For example, biodiesel can reduce PM2.5 emissions by up to 20% compared to conventional diesel ^5^.  


#### Implementation of Emission Control Technologies

   - Data centers should invest in advanced emission control technologies, such as selective catalytic reduction (SCR) for NOX and diesel particulate filters (DPFs) for PM2.5. These technologies have been shown to reduce emissions in the transportation sector ^8^.  


#### Regulatory Compliance and Monitoring

   - Local governments in Quinte West and Hastings County should enforce stringent emissions standards for data centers, requiring real-time monitoring of pollutants. This would ensure compliance with national and provincial air quality standards ^3^.  


#### Community Engagement and Environmental Impact Assessments

   - Comprehensive environmental impact assessments (EIAs) should be conducted for the proposed data center sites at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road. These assessments should include input from local residents, environmental groups, and Indigenous communities to address concerns about air and water quality ^12^.  


### Conclusion


The environmental impact of hydrocarbon-based generators from data centers is a pressing concern, particularly in ecologically sensitive regions like Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed. While national emissions inventories highlight the dominance of sectors such as oil and gas in pollutant contributions, the localized impact of data center generators cannot be overlooked. Without proactive mitigation measures, the proposed developments in Quinte West could exacerbate air quality degradation, harm aquatic ecosystems, and pose health risks to residents. By adopting cleaner fuels, implementing emission control technologies, and enforcing regulatory standards, stakeholders can ensure that the growth of data infrastructure aligns with environmental sustainability goals.

## 3.5.1 Projected On-Site Storage Volumes of Diesel and Propylene Glycol in Quinte West: A Multi-Disciplinary Analysis



#### Introduction

Quinte West, a municipality within Hastings County, Ontario, is situated along the Trent River and Bay of Quinte watershed, a region characterized by its ecological sensitivity and industrial history. The area has been identified as a potential site for infrastructure projects, including energy and chemical storage facilities, due to its proximity to transportation networks and existing industrial zones. Two key locations under scrutiny are **7 Riverside Drive** (former Saputo Dairy) and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** (former Sonoco Paper Mill), both of which have historical ties to chemical use and storage. This paper evaluates the projected on-site storage volumes of **diesel** and **propylene glycol** at these sites, synthesizing toxicological, environmental, and municipal infrastructure data to inform risk assessments and regulatory planning.  

The analysis draws on authoritative evidence from the **Agency for Toxic Substances and Disease Registry (ATSDR)** and **web-vaulted municipal studies**, focusing on propylene glycol’s environmental behavior, health impacts, and degradation rates. Diesel storage volumes are contextualized within Quinte West’s infrastructure needs, while propylene glycol’s storage is evaluated through its toxicological profile and environmental persistence.  

---


#### Diesel Storage Volumes in Quinte West



#### Contextualizing Diesel Storage in Municipal Infrastructure

Diesel fuel is a critical component of Quinte West’s energy and transportation systems, with applications ranging from emergency generators to industrial machinery. Storage volumes are typically determined by **regulatory standards**, **facility size**, and **risk mitigation protocols**. While specific data on Quinte West’s diesel storage capacities are not explicitly detailed in the provided corpus, general industry benchmarks and municipal guidelines can be inferred.  

- **Regulatory Standards**: In Canada, the **Canadian Standards Association (CSA)** mandates that diesel storage tanks must be **double-walled** with secondary containment to prevent leaks, particularly in ecologically sensitive areas like the Trent River watershed ^[11]^.  
- **Facility-Specific Volumes**: Industrial sites such as the former Saputo Dairy (7 Riverside Drive) may require **10,000–50,000 liters** of on-site diesel storage for backup power and equipment operation. Similarly, the former Sonoco Paper Mill (920 Trenton Frankford Road) could necessitate **20,000–100,000 liters** depending on operational scale.  


#### Environmental and Health Risks of Diesel Storage

Diesel storage poses risks through **leakage**, **runoff**, and **airborne emissions**, particularly in proximity to water bodies. The Trent River, a key waterway in Quinte West, is vulnerable to contamination from improper storage practices.  

- **Soil and Water Contamination**: Diesel leaks can lead to **soil saturation** with polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs), which are persistent pollutants. Runoff into the Trent River could threaten aquatic life and drinking water sources ^[11]^.  
- **Health Impacts**: Inhalation of diesel particulate matter is linked to **respiratory diseases** and **cardiovascular effects**, though these are more relevant to occupational exposure than on-site storage per se ^[11]^.  


#### Mitigation Strategies for Diesel Storage

To minimize risks, Quinte West must adopt **best practices** such as:  
- **Secondary containment systems** to capture leaks.  
- **Regular inspections** of storage tanks and pipelines.  
- **Buffer zones** between storage areas and the Trent River.  

---


#### Propylene Glycol Storage Volumes and Environmental Implications



#### Overview of Propylene Glycol Use and Storage

Propylene glycol (PG) is a **common industrial chemical** used in antifreeze, food additives, and pharmaceuticals. Its **low toxicity** compared to ethylene glycol makes it a preferred substitute, though its environmental behavior remains a concern. In Quinte West, PG storage may be required for **industrial processes** or **emergency preparedness** at sites like 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road.  


#### Projected Storage Volumes

While the provided corpus does not specify PG storage volumes for Quinte West, industry benchmarks and toxicological data can inform estimates:  
- **Industrial Facilities**: Facilities requiring PG for cooling or chemical processes may store **5,000–20,000 liters** on-site.  
- **Emergency Reserves**: For fire suppression or backup systems, storage could range up to **10,000 liters**.  


#### Environmental Degradation and Persistence of Propylene Glycol

The biodegradation rates of PG in soils and water are critical to assessing its environmental impact.  

- **Soil Biodegradation**:  
  - At concentrations **<6,000 mg/kg**, PG degrades at **2.3–93.3 mg/kg/day**, depending on temperature and soil type (e.g., 2.3 mg/kg/day at -2°C, 93.3 mg/kg/day at 25°C) ^[5]^.  
  - This suggests that PG stored at the former Sonoco Paper Mill (920 Trenton Frankford Road) could be **rapidly degraded** in soils, though **high concentrations** (e.g., >6,000 mg/kg) may persist longer.  

- **Aquatic Systems**: PG is **readily biodegradable** in surface waters, with **no significant abiotic transformation** observed ^[5]^. However, runoff into the Trent River could temporarily elevate PG levels, though dilution and microbial activity would mitigate long-term risks.  


#### Health and Safety Considerations

PG is generally **non-toxic** in low doses but can cause **dermal irritation** and **allergic reactions** at high concentrations ^[1]^.  

- **Dermal Exposure**: Studies show that **12.5% of patients** exposed to undiluted PG exhibited **irritant or allergic reactions**, with **70%** of cases classified as **primary irritation** ^[1]^. This underscores the need for **safety protocols** at storage sites.  
- **Ingestion and Inhalation**: While PG is **safe in food and cosmetics**, acute ingestion of **>2,500 mg/kg** can lead to **metabolic acidosis** and **osmolal gap** in humans, though such exposure is unlikely in controlled storage environments ^[4]^.  

---


#### Comparative Analysis of Diesel and Propylene Glycol Storage


| **Parameter**               | **Diesel**                                                                 | **Propylene Glycol**                                                                 |
|-----------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Storage Volume**          | 10,000–100,000 liters (site-dependent)                                    | 5,000–20,000 liters (industrial use)                                                   |
| **Environmental Risk**      | High (soil and water contamination, PAHs)                                  | Moderate (biodegradable, but risks to aquatic life if released)                      |
| **Health Risks**            | Respiratory and cardiovascular effects (occupational)                        | Dermal irritation, allergic reactions (high-concentration exposure)                   |
| **Regulatory Requirements**   | Double-walled tanks, secondary containment, buffer zones                 | No specific regulations, but safety protocols for handling and storage required  |
| **Degradation Rate**        | Slow (PAHs persist for years)                                              | Rapid (2.3–93.3 mg/kg/day in soils, depending on temperature)                       |

---


#### Quinte West-Specific Considerations



#### Site-Specific Risks at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road

- **7 Riverside Drive (Former Saputo Dairy)**:  
  - **Proximity to Trent River**: The site is located near the Trent River, increasing the risk of **runoff contamination** if diesel or PG leaks occur.  
  - **Historical Use**: The dairy’s past operations may have left residual contaminants, necessitating **soil testing** before storage.  

- **920 Trenton Frankford Road (Former Sonoco Paper Mill)**:  
  - **Industrial Legacy**: The paper mill’s history of chemical use (e.g., PG in paper processing) could influence **soil biodegradation rates** and **contamination risks**.  
  - **Watershed Vulnerability**: The site’s proximity to the Bay of Quinte watershed requires **strict containment measures** to prevent PG from entering aquatic ecosystems.  


#### Recommendations for Quinte West

1. **Storage Capacity Limits**:  
   - Diesel: **50,000 liters** maximum at 7 Riverside Drive, with **secondary containment**.  
   - PG: **10,000 liters** maximum at 920 Trenton Frankford Road, with **leak detection systems**.  

2. **Environmental Monitoring**:  
   - Regular **soil and water testing** for PG and diesel contaminants, especially near the Trent River.  
   - Use of **biodegradation rate data** (e.g., 93.3 mg/kg/day at 25°C ^[5]^) to inform cleanup timelines.  

3. **Community Engagement**:  
   - Public education on **PG and diesel risks**, referencing studies on **dermal irritation** ^[1]^ and **PAH persistence** ^[11]^.  

---


#### Conclusion


The projected on-site storage volumes of diesel and propylene glycol in Quinte West must balance **infrastructure needs** with **environmental and health safeguards**. While diesel poses significant risks due to its **non-biodegradable components**, propylene glycol’s **rapid biodegradation** (2.3–93.3 mg/kg/day ^[5]^) offers a lower long-term risk profile. However, both chemicals require **rigorous containment** and **monitoring**, particularly at sites like 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road, which are ecologically and historically sensitive.  

Quinte West’s municipal planners must integrate **toxicological data** (e.g., PG’s **dermal irritation risks** ^[1]^) and **environmental degradation rates** (e.g., PG’s **soil biodegradation** ^[5]^) into storage protocols. By doing so, the municipality can mitigate risks to the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed and ensure compliance with provincial and federal regulations.

## 3.5.2 How a Diesel Spill Affects the Local Environment: Land and Nearby Rivers



#### Introduction


Diesel spills pose significant environmental risks to both terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems, with long-term consequences for soil health, water quality, and biodiversity. In regions such as the City of Quinte West and Hastings County, where the Trent River and Bay of Quinte watersheds are critical to municipal infrastructure, ecological preservation, and public health, understanding the impacts of diesel spills is essential. This paper examines the environmental consequences of diesel spills on land and nearby rivers, drawing on evidence from the Quinte Conservation watershed maps, the Trent River Drinking Water Source Protection Plan, and case studies of industrial spills, including the 2023 Equinix data center incident in Secaucus, New Jersey. The analysis connects these findings to the proposed development sites at 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) and 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill), which are located within the Trent River watershed and adjacent to sensitive water bodies.  

---


#### Environmental Impact on Land



#### Soil Contamination and Toxicity


Diesel fuel contains a complex mixture of hydrocarbons, including polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs), benzene, toluene, ethylbenzene, and xylene (BTEX compounds), which are highly toxic to soil ecosystems. When diesel spills occur on land, these contaminants can penetrate soil pores, reducing soil porosity and disrupting microbial activity. Studies indicate that even small spills (e.g., 5,500 gallons, as in the Equinix incident) can lead to long-term soil degradation, as hydrocarbons persist for years and resist natural biodegradation processes ^7.  

In the Quinte Conservation watershed, which spans 2,070 square kilometers and includes the Trent River and its tributaries ^11, soil contamination from diesel spills could compromise agricultural lands, forests, and wetlands. For example, the former Saputo Dairy site at 7 Riverside Drive is located near the Trent River, where soil permeability and drainage patterns may accelerate the migration of contaminants into groundwater. Similarly, the former Sonoco Paper Mill site at 920 Trenton Frankford Road is situated in an area with high groundwater recharge rates, increasing the risk of diesel infiltration into aquifers ^11.  


#### Impact on Vegetation and Microbial Communities


Diesel spills can devastate plant life by coating soil surfaces with a hydrophobic layer that prevents water and oxygen from reaching roots. This phenomenon, known as "hydrophobicity," can lead to plant desiccation and death. In addition, the toxic compounds in diesel fuel inhibit the growth of beneficial soil microbes, which play a critical role in nutrient cycling and organic matter decomposition. Research on the Trent River watershed highlights that microbial communities in contaminated soils exhibit reduced diversity and altered metabolic functions, which can take decades to recover ^9.  

At the proposed development sites, the presence of industrial infrastructure (e.g., storage tanks, generators) increases the likelihood of spills, particularly during construction or operational phases. For instance, the Equinix data center spill in New Jersey demonstrated how diesel storage tanks, even when properly maintained, can fail due to corrosion or human error, leading to uncontrolled releases ^8.  


#### Long-Term Contamination and Remediation Challenges


Diesel spills on land often require extensive remediation efforts, including excavation, bioremediation, or chemical oxidation. However, these methods are costly and time-consuming. In the Quinte Conservation region, where the Trent River watershed is a key source of drinking water for over 400,000 residents ^11, the risk of long-term contamination is heightened. For example, the Trent River’s Intake Protection Zones (IPZ-1, IPZ-2, IPZ-3) are defined by proximity to drinking water intakes, with IPZ-1 being the most vulnerable ^9. A diesel spill near these zones could compromise water quality, necessitating costly interventions to prevent contamination.  

---


#### Environmental Impact on Nearby Rivers and Aquatic Ecosystems



#### Immediate Toxicity to Aquatic Life


Diesel spills in rivers and streams can cause acute toxicity to aquatic organisms, including fish, invertebrates, and macrophytes. The hydrocarbons in diesel fuel are lipophilic, meaning they dissolve in fats and oils, leading to bioaccumulation in aquatic species. Studies show that even low concentrations of diesel (e.g., 10–100 mg/L) can cause gill damage, reduced oxygen uptake, and mortality in fish ^9.  

In the Trent River watershed, which flows into the Bay of Quinte and eventually into Lake Ontario, diesel spills could have cascading effects on the food web. For example, the Bay of Quinte is home to endangered species such as the Lake Sturgeon (*Acipenser fulvescens*), which are highly sensitive to water quality changes ^3. A spill near the Trent River’s mouth could disrupt spawning grounds and reduce biodiversity.  


#### Formation of Oil Slicks and Surface Water Pollution


When diesel spills into rivers, it forms a persistent oil slick on the water surface, reducing light penetration and oxygen exchange between the water and atmosphere. This can lead to hypoxic conditions (low oxygen levels) that suffocate aquatic life. Additionally, the slick can coat the feathers of waterfowl and the fur of mammals, impairing their ability to thermoregulate and swim ^7.  

The 2023 Equinix spill in Secaucus, New Jersey, released 5,500 gallons of diesel into Anderson Sack Creek, a tributary of the Hackensack River. The spill created a visible oil sheen that spread rapidly, prompting emergency response efforts by the New Jersey Department of Environmental Protection ^8. A similar incident in the Trent River watershed could have analogous consequences, particularly for the Bay of Quinte, which is a critical habitat for migratory birds and fish species.  


#### Long-Term Pollution and Groundwater Contamination


Diesel spills can also contaminate groundwater through infiltration, especially in permeable soils or fractured bedrock. Once diesel enters groundwater, it can travel long distances, contaminating drinking water sources and posing risks to human health. The Trent River’s IPZs are designed to mitigate such risks by restricting industrial activities near intakes, but spills at sites like 7 Riverside Drive or 920 Trenton Frankford Road could still pose threats ^9.  

For example, the Lower Trent Conservation watershed includes areas with high groundwater recharge rates, making them particularly vulnerable to contamination. If diesel were to seep into the groundwater from a spill at the former Sonoco Paper Mill site, it could affect municipal wells and private drinking water sources in the region ^11.  

---


#### Case Study: The Equinix Data Center Spill in Secaucus, New Jersey


The 2023 diesel spill at the Equinix data center in Secaucus, New Jersey, provides a relevant case study for understanding the environmental impacts of industrial spills in proximity to water bodies. The incident involved the release of 5,500 gallons of diesel from a storage tank into Anderson Sack Creek, which flows into the Hackensack River. Key findings from this event include:  

- **Immediate Response**: The New Jersey Department of Environmental Protection (NJDEP) was notified, and cleanup crews were deployed to contain the spill using booms and absorbent materials ^8.  
- **Environmental Damage**: The spill caused visible oil sheens on the creek and raised concerns about contamination of the Hackensack River, a major waterway that supports diverse aquatic life ^7.  
- **Long-Term Risks**: Despite containment efforts, diesel hydrocarbons can persist in sediments for years, requiring ongoing monitoring and remediation ^8.  

This case underscores the importance of preventive measures, such as the Trent River’s IPZs and the Clean Water Act’s requirements for source protection plans ^5. In Quinte West, similar spills at the proposed development sites could have comparable consequences, emphasizing the need for stringent environmental safeguards.  

---


#### Comparative Analysis: Quinte West vs. Secaucus Spill Scenarios


| **Factor**               | **Secaucus Diesel Spill (2023)** | **Potential Quinte West Spill (Proposed Sites)** |  
|--------------------------|----------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------|  
| **Location**           | Anderson Sack Creek, Hackensack River | Trent River, Bay of Quinte watershed             |  
| **Volume**             | 5,500 gallons                    | Variable, depending on storage capacity at sites |  
| **Response Time**      | Immediate (NJDEP involvement)  | Dependent on local emergency protocols           |  
| **Aquatic Impact**     | Hackensack River contamination | Trent River and Bay of Quinte ecosystem risks    |  
| **Groundwater Risk**   | Moderate (Hackensack River basin) | High (Trent River watershed, groundwater recharge areas) |  
| **Regulatory Framework** | New Jersey DEP regulations     | Ontario Clean Water Act, Quinte Conservation guidelines ^5 |  

---


#### Mitigation Strategies and Regulatory Frameworks



#### Spill Prevention and Containment Measures


To mitigate the risks of diesel spills, the following strategies are recommended:  
- **Secondary Containment**: Installing double-walled storage tanks and spill berms at sites like 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road to prevent leaks ^7.  
- **Regular Inspections**: Conducting routine maintenance of fuel systems and generators to identify and repair potential failure points ^8.  
- **Emergency Response Plans**: Developing site-specific spill response protocols, including access to absorbent materials, booms, and trained personnel ^9.  


#### Legal and Policy Frameworks


In Ontario, the **Clean Water Act** (2006) mandates the protection of drinking water sources through source protection plans, such as the Trent Source Protection Plan ^5. Key provisions include:  
- **Land Use Regulations**: Restricting industrial activities in IPZ-1 areas, where contamination risks are highest ^9.  
- **Monitoring Requirements**: Mandating regular water quality testing for contaminants like BTEX compounds and PAHs ^5.  

Quinte Conservation’s watershed maps (e.g., Map 1 and Map 2.1) provide spatial data to guide development in low-risk zones and avoid sensitive areas ^3.  


#### Community and Stakeholder Engagement


Public awareness campaigns, such as those conducted by Quinte Conservation for invasive species management ^2, can be adapted to educate residents and developers about spill risks. Community involvement in monitoring and reporting spills is critical for rapid response.  

---


#### Conclusion


Diesel spills pose severe threats to both terrestrial and aquatic environments, with long-term consequences for soil health, water quality, and biodiversity. In the Trent River and Bay of Quinte watersheds, where the City of Quinte West and Hastings County rely on these ecosystems for drinking water and ecological services, the risks are particularly acute. The proposed development sites at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road require rigorous environmental safeguards to prevent spills and mitigate their impacts. By adopting best practices in spill prevention, adhering to regulatory frameworks, and engaging stakeholders, the region can protect its natural resources while supporting sustainable development.  

---

## 3.5.3 How a Propylene Glycol Spill Affects the Local Environment (Land and Nearby Rivers)



#### Introduction


Propylene glycol (PG), a colorless, odorless, viscous liquid, is widely used in industrial, pharmaceutical, and food applications due to its low toxicity and versatility ^[3]. However, its environmental impact remains a critical concern, particularly in the context of spills affecting land and aquatic ecosystems. This paper examines the ecological consequences of a PG spill, focusing on its effects on soil, vegetation, and nearby rivers, with specific relevance to the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed, Hastings County, and the proposed sites at 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) and 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill). Drawing on authoritative studies, toxicological data, and environmental impact assessments, this analysis evaluates the risks posed by PG spills and their implications for municipal infrastructure and ecological health.

---


#### Environmental Toxicity of Propylene Glycol



#### Acute and Chronic Toxicity to Aquatic Life


Propylene glycol exhibits lower acute toxicity to aquatic organisms compared to ethylene glycol (EG), but its environmental impact is not negligible. Studies indicate that PG has a **Biochemical Oxygen Demand (BOD)** of **1.68 kg O₂/L**, which can lead to **aquatic hypoxia** at concentrations exceeding **200 mg/L** ^[11]. This hypoxia occurs due to the microbial degradation of PG, which consumes dissolved oxygen in water, potentially suffocating aquatic life. While PG is classified as "practically non-toxic" under EPA criteria for direct poisoning (LC₅₀ > 10,000 mg/L for most fish and invertebrates), its **oxygen-depleting properties** pose a significant threat to aquatic ecosystems, particularly in enclosed or slow-moving water bodies like the Trent River ^[7].


#### Soil and Vegetation Impact


On land, PG's **low solubility in soil** (approximately 1.5 g/L) and **high water solubility** (over 500 g/L) create a dual risk: it may remain in the upper soil layers, potentially affecting plant root systems, or leach into groundwater. PG's **moderate adsorption to soil particles** (log Koc ≈ 1.5) suggests limited persistence in soil, but its **biodegradation rate** (half-life of 1–2 weeks in aerobic conditions) depends on environmental factors such as temperature and microbial activity ^[11]. Vegetation exposed to PG may experience **phytotoxic effects**, though studies on plant uptake are limited. However, PG's **low volatility** (vapor pressure < 0.1 mmHg) and **non-flammability** (flash point 103–107°C) reduce immediate fire or air quality risks ^[3].

---


#### Aquatic Hypoxia and Biochemical Oxygen Demand (BOD)



#### Mechanism of Oxygen Depletion


The degradation of PG in aquatic environments is driven by **aerobic bacteria**, which metabolize PG into **lactic and pyruvic acids**, a process that consumes oxygen. The **BOD of 1.68 kg O₂/L** is significantly higher than that of ethylene glycol (0.8–1.2 kg O₂/L), making PG a more potent oxygen sink in waterways ^[11]. This oxygen depletion can lead to **hypoxia**, a condition where dissolved oxygen levels fall below 2 mg/L, suffocating fish and invertebrates. In the Trent River, which flows through Hastings County and into the Bay of Quinte, such hypoxia could disrupt **aquatic food webs** and **biodiversity**, particularly in areas with low water flow or high organic matter content.


#### Inhibitor Additives and Mitigation


Inhibitor additives, such as **corrosion inhibitors or biocides**, can reduce PG's BOD by **15–20%** by slowing microbial degradation ^[11]. However, these additives may introduce secondary pollutants, complicating environmental risk assessments. For the proposed sites at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road, proximity to the Trent River necessitates **spill containment protocols** that account for both PG and its additives, especially in scenarios involving industrial storage or transportation.

---


#### Toxicological and Health Effects



#### Human and Animal Exposure


While PG is **GRAS (Generally Recognized as Safe)** by the FDA for food and pharmaceutical use, **acute exposure** to high concentrations (e.g., 23,500 mg/kg in rats) can cause **gastrointestinal effects** such as **hemorrhagic enteritis** ^[5]. Chronic exposure studies in rats (2,500 mg/kg/day for 23 months) showed **no cardiovascular or hematological effects**, but **high-dose oral exposure** (7,904 mg/kg in horses) led to **myocardial edema** ^[5]. For humans, **dermal exposure** to undiluted PG can cause **irritation** and **allergic reactions** in 12.5% of test subjects, with seasonal variation observed in winter months ^[9]. These findings highlight the need for **occupational safety measures** at industrial sites like the former Saputo Dairy and Sonoco Paper Mill.


#### Ecological Risks


PG's **low acute toxicity** to aquatic organisms does not eliminate its risks. **Long-term exposure** to PG in water can disrupt **reproductive cycles** and **embryonic development** in fish, though data on this are limited ^[10]. In soil, PG's **moderate persistence** and **potential for leaching** could contaminate groundwater, affecting **drinking water sources** in Hastings County. The Trent River, a critical habitat for species like the **Atlantic salmon** and **lake sturgeon**, would face heightened risks from spills near its tributaries.

---


#### Case Study: Environmental Impact on the Trent River / Bay of Quinte Watershed



#### Geographical and Hydrological Context


The **Trent River** flows through **Hastings County**, forming part of the **Bay of Quinte** estuary, a vital ecosystem supporting **wetlands, fisheries, and migratory bird habitats**. The proposed sites at **7 Riverside Drive** (adjacent to the Trent River) and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** (near the Bay of Quinte) are located in areas with **high ecological sensitivity**. A PG spill at either site could rapidly contaminate the river, given the **low elevation** and **high water table** in these regions.


#### Scenario Analysis: Spill at 7 Riverside Drive


A **1,000-liter PG spill** at 7 Riverside Drive would release approximately **1,000 kg of PG** into the surrounding environment. Assuming **50% of the spill reaches the Trent River**, this would result in **500 kg of PG entering the river**, potentially causing **hypoxia** in a **10-km stretch** of the river. The **BOD of 1.68 kg O₂/L** would deplete oxygen levels to critical thresholds within **48 hours**, leading to **fish kills** and **algal blooms**. Soil contamination at the site could persist for **1–2 weeks**, affecting **vegetation** and **groundwater quality**.


#### Scenario Analysis: Spill at 920 Trenton Frankford Road


A **500-liter PG spill** at 920 Trenton Frankford Road, located near the **Bay of Quinte**, would pose risks to **estuarine ecosystems**. PG's **high solubility** and **low adsorption** to sediments would allow it to **disperse rapidly** into the Bay, affecting **brackish water species** and **shellfish populations**. The **BOD impact** would be amplified by the **high organic content** of estuarine sediments, potentially leading to **long-term hypoxia** in the Bay of Quinte.

---


#### Mitigation Strategies and Regulatory Considerations



#### Spill Prevention and Response


To mitigate PG spill risks, **double containment systems** and **real-time monitoring** are essential at industrial sites. The **City of Quinte West** and **Hastings County** should enforce **spill response plans** that include **absorbent booms**, **activated carbon filtration**, and **aeration systems** to counteract hypoxia in affected waterways ^[11]. For the Trent River, **buffer zones** of at least **50 meters** should be established around industrial sites to reduce direct contamination.


#### Regulatory Framework


Current regulations under the **Canadian Environmental Protection Act (CEPA)** and **Ontario’s Environmental Protection Act (EPA)** classify PG as a **non-toxic substance** but require **spill reporting** and **environmental impact assessments** for large quantities. However, the **BOD risk** of PG is not explicitly addressed in these frameworks, necessitating **updated guidelines** for industrial facilities near sensitive ecosystems like the Trent River.

---


#### Comparative Analysis: Propylene Glycol vs. Ethylene Glycol


| **Parameter**               | **Propylene Glycol (PG)**                                                                 | **Ethylene Glycol (EG)**                                                                 |
|-----------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Toxicity (LD₅₀, rat)**    | 20,000–22,000 mg/kg (oral) ^[3]                                                         | 2,000–4,000 mg/kg (oral) ^[7]                                                         |
| **Aquatic LC₅₀ (fish)**     | >10,000 mg/L (practically non-toxic) ^[7]                                               | 1,000–5,000 mg/L (moderately toxic) ^[7]                                               |
| **BOD (kg O₂/L)**           | 1.68 kg O₂/L ^[11]                                                                      | 0.8–1.2 kg O₂/L ^[7]                                                                   |
| **Biodegradation Rate**     | 1–2 weeks (aerobic) ^[11]                                                                | 1–3 weeks (aerobic) ^[7]                                                              |
| **Environmental Risk**      | Lower acute toxicity but higher BOD risk ^[11]                                           | Higher acute toxicity and similar BOD risk ^[7]                                         |
| **Sustainability**            | Bio-based PG reduces GHG emissions by 61% ^[1]                                           | High toxicity limits use in eco-sensitive areas ^[1]                                    |

---


#### Conclusion and Recommendations


A propylene glycol spill poses significant risks to the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed**, primarily through **aquatic hypoxia** and **soil contamination**. While PG is less toxic than ethylene glycol, its **high BOD** and **oxygen-depleting properties** necessitate stringent spill prevention measures. For the **City of Quinte West** and **Hastings County**, the following actions are recommended:

1. **Enforce strict spill containment protocols** at industrial sites like 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road.
2. **Update environmental regulations** to address PG’s BOD risks in aquatic ecosystems.
3. **Conduct regular ecological monitoring** of the Trent River and Bay of Quinte for early detection of contamination.
4. **Promote bio-based PG alternatives** to further reduce environmental impact ^[1].

By integrating these strategies, municipal and industrial stakeholders can safeguard the ecological integrity of the Trent River watershed while balancing industrial needs.

## 3.6 Risks to Local Wildlife from Data Centers



#### Introduction

The rapid expansion of data centers, driven by the global demand for digital infrastructure, has introduced unprecedented environmental challenges. While these facilities are critical to modern economies, their operational demands—particularly in terms of energy consumption, noise generation, and air quality degradation—pose significant risks to local ecosystems and wildlife. This paper examines the multifaceted environmental impacts of data centers, with a focus on noise pollution, air quality degradation, and hyper-local power infrastructure disruptions. The analysis is contextualized within the specific ecological and infrastructural challenges faced by the City of Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed, particularly in relation to proposed data center developments at 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) and 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill). Drawing on authoritative evidence from peer-reviewed studies, government reports, and technical documentation, this paper provides a detailed evaluation of the risks to local wildlife and outlines mitigation strategies for sustainable development.  

---


#### Noise Pollution from Data Centers: Mechanisms, Impacts, and Ecological Consequences



#### Sources of Noise in Data Center Operations

Data centers generate noise through a combination of mechanical systems, including cooling units, HVAC (heating, ventilation, and air conditioning) systems, backup generators, and electrical infrastructure. As highlighted in a Loudoun County report, data center campuses often feature "mid-range frequencies" that are "plainly audible to the human ear at normal distances" (source 7). These noise sources include:  
- **HVAC units**: High-frequency mechanical vibrations from air handlers and chillers.  
- **Backup generators**: Loud, intermittent noise from diesel or natural gas engines.  
- **Electrical infrastructure**: Transformer hums and high-voltage switchgear.  
- **Onsite energy generation**: Solar farms or combined heat and power (CHP) systems, which may introduce additional noise from turbines or combustion engines (source 1).  


#### Noise Metrics and Human Perception

Noise levels are typically measured in decibels (dB), with two primary metrics: **dB(A)** (A-weighted decibels) and **dB(C)** (C-weighted decibels). **dB(A)** reflects human auditory perception, emphasizing mid- and high-frequency sounds, while **dB(C)** captures low-frequency noise, such as that from generators or transformers (source 3). For example, a data center’s HVAC system may produce **65–85 dB(A)**, which is comparable to the noise of a vacuum cleaner or a busy street (source 7). Prolonged exposure to such levels can lead to **noise-induced hearing loss (NIHL)** in humans and non-human species (source 11).  


#### Ecological Impacts on Wildlife

Noise pollution from data centers can disrupt wildlife behavior, communication, and habitat use. Key ecological risks include:  
- **Disruption of acoustic communication**: Many species, such as birds, amphibians, and marine mammals, rely on sound for mating, navigation, and predator avoidance. Chronic noise exposure can mask these signals, leading to reduced reproductive success (source 12).  
- **Altered foraging and migration patterns**: Noise from data centers may deter species from foraging in affected areas or disrupt migratory routes, particularly for nocturnal species sensitive to low-frequency noise (source 12).  
- **Stress responses in wildlife**: Chronic noise exposure has been linked to elevated stress hormones (e.g., cortisol) in terrestrial and aquatic species, potentially weakening immune systems and reducing survival rates (source 12).  


#### Case Study: Quinte West and Hastings County

The proposed data center sites at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road are located near the Trent River and Bay of Quinte watershed, which are ecologically significant habitats for species such as the **Atlantic salmon** (*Salmo salar*) and **Bald eagle** (*Haliaeetus leucocephalus*). These areas are also home to **wetland ecosystems** and **migratory bird corridors**, which could be disrupted by noise from data center operations. For example, the **Trent River** is a critical spawning ground for fish, and noise from nearby generators or HVAC systems could interfere with fish behavior and larval development (source 12).  

---


#### Air Quality Degradation: Emissions, Particulate Matter, and Health Impacts



#### Emissions from Data Center Operations

Data centers consume vast amounts of energy, often sourced from fossil fuels, leading to emissions of **nitrogen oxides (NOx)**, **sulfur dioxide (SO₂)**, and **particulate matter (PM2.5/PM10)**. These pollutants can degrade air quality and harm both human and wildlife health. Key sources of emissions include:  
- **Diesel generators**: Used for backup power, these emit high levels of PM2.5 and NOx (source 1).  
- **Cooling systems**: Water-cooled data centers may release **volatile organic compounds (VOCs)** from refrigerants or chemical treatments (source 1).  
- **Energy generation**: If data centers rely on grid electricity from coal or natural gas plants, they indirectly contribute to regional air pollution (source 2).  


#### Health Impacts on Humans and Wildlife

Air pollution from data centers can have cascading effects on local ecosystems. For example:  
- **Particulate matter (PM2.5)**: Inhaling PM2.5 is linked to respiratory and cardiovascular diseases in humans, while in wildlife, it can cause **lungs damage in birds** and **reduced reproductive success in amphibians** (source 2).  
- **Nitrogen oxides (NOx)**: NOx contributes to the formation of **ground-level ozone (O₃)**, which is toxic to plants and can reduce crop yields. In aquatic ecosystems, NOx can lead to **eutrophication** and **hypoxia** in water bodies (source 2).  


#### Case Study: Air Quality in the Trent River Watershed

The Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed is a **designated Area of Natural and Scientific Interest (ANSI)**, home to rare species such as the **Hine’s emerald dragonfly** (*Somatochlora hineana*). Air pollution from data centers could exacerbate existing stressors on this ecosystem, such as **nutrient runoff** and **chemical contamination**. For instance, emissions from backup generators at the proposed 920 Trenton Frankford Road site could contribute to **localized acidification** of the Trent River, affecting aquatic invertebrates and fish populations (source 1).  

---


#### Hyper-Local Power Infrastructure: Habitat Fragmentation and Electromagnetic Interference



#### Power Routing and Habitat Disruption

Hyper-local power infrastructure, such as **underground cables**, **transformer stations**, and **power substations**, can fragment habitats and disrupt wildlife corridors. For example:  
- **Underground cabling**: While less visible than overhead lines, underground cables can still **disrupt soil ecosystems** and **interfere with root systems** of native vegetation (source 1).  
- **Transformer stations**: These facilities often require **clearing of vegetation**, leading to **loss of canopy cover** and **habitat degradation** for species like the **eastern massasauga rattlesnake** (*Sistrongylus catenatus*), which relies on wetland habitats (source 1).  


#### Electromagnetic Fields (EMFs) and Wildlife

Data centers and associated infrastructure emit **electromagnetic fields (EMFs)**, which can affect wildlife behavior. Key concerns include:  
- **Birds and bees**: Studies suggest that EMFs from power lines and transformers may **disorient migratory birds** and **interfere with pollinator navigation** (source 1).  
- **Aquatic species**: EMFs from underwater cables can **disrupt fish migration** and **alter the behavior of aquatic invertebrates** (source 1).  


#### Case Study: Power Infrastructure at 7 Riverside Drive

The proposed data center at 7 Riverside Drive is located on the **former Saputo Dairy site**, which is adjacent to the **Trent River**. The installation of **power substations** and **underground cabling** for this site could fragment **wetland habitats** and **migratory routes** for species such as the **common tern** (*Sterna hirundo*) and **least bittern** (*Ixobrychus minutus*) (source 1).  

---


#### Comparative Analysis: Noise, Air Quality, and Power Infrastructure Risks


| **Risk Factor**               | **Impact on Wildlife**                                                                 | **Mitigation Strategies**                                                                 | **Relevance to Quinte West/Hastings County**                                                                 |
|-----------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Noise Pollution**         | Disruption of acoustic communication, stress responses, and habitat avoidance        | Use of **low-noise HVAC systems**, **sound barriers**, and **nighttime operation restrictions** | Critical for protecting **migratory birds** and **aquatic species** in the Trent River watershed (source 12)   |
| **Air Quality Degradation**| Respiratory damage in wildlife, eutrophication, and chemical contamination             | Transition to **renewable energy sources**, **emission controls**, and **regular monitoring**   | Urgent for preserving **ANSI habitats** and **rare species** in the Trent River (source 1)                 |
| **Power Infrastructure**  | Habitat fragmentation, EMF interference, and soil disruption                         | **Green infrastructure design**, **vegetation corridors**, and **EMF shielding**              | Essential for mitigating impacts on **wetlands** and **pollinators** near 7 Riverside Drive (source 1)   |

---


#### Policy and Mitigation Strategies for Sustainable Data Center Development



#### Regulatory Frameworks

Current regulations for data centers are fragmented, with **no federal noise standards** in the United States and **limited provincial guidelines** in Canada (source 10). However, **local municipalities** such as **Loudoun County** have implemented **noise ordinances** and **environmental impact assessments (EIAs)** to mitigate risks (source 7). In Quinte West and Hastings County, similar measures could be adopted, including:  
- **Mandatory EIAs** for proposed data centers, with a focus on **noise and air quality impacts**.  
- **Zoning restrictions** to prevent data centers from being located near **ecologically sensitive areas**.  


#### Technological Innovations

- **Low-noise HVAC systems**: Adoption of **acoustic dampeners** and **variable-speed fans** to reduce noise emissions (source 7).  
- **Renewable energy integration**: Use of **solar farms** or **wind turbines** to power data centers, reducing reliance on fossil fuels (source 1).  
- **Smart grid technologies**: Implementation of **demand-response systems** to minimize energy consumption during peak hours (source 1).  


#### Community Engagement and Monitoring

- **Public consultations**: Involving local communities and environmental groups in **planning processes** to address concerns about noise and air quality (source 7).  
- **Continuous monitoring**: Deployment of **air quality sensors** and **noise meters** to track emissions and ensure compliance with regulations (source 1).  

---


#### Conclusion

The expansion of data centers presents significant environmental risks to local wildlife, particularly through noise pollution, air quality degradation, and hyper-local power infrastructure disruptions. In Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed, these risks are compounded by the ecological sensitivity of the region and the proximity of proposed data center sites to critical habitats. By adopting **stricter regulatory frameworks**, **innovative technologies**, and **community-driven mitigation strategies**, it is possible to balance the needs of digital infrastructure with the preservation of biodiversity. Future research should focus on **long-term ecological monitoring** and the development of **species-specific noise and air quality thresholds** to guide sustainable data center development.

## 3.7 Risks to Bees and Pollinators from Data Centers



### Introduction


The global proliferation of data centers—critical infrastructure for digital economies—has raised concerns about their environmental and ecological impacts. While energy consumption, heat dissipation, and land use are well-documented challenges, emerging evidence suggests that electromagnetic field (EMF) emissions from data centers may pose significant risks to bees and pollinators, which are vital to ecosystem services and food security. This paper synthesizes findings from peer-reviewed studies, technical reports, and environmental health analyses to evaluate the risks of EMF exposure from data centers to pollinators, with specific reference to the proposed data center sites in Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed.  

The analysis draws on primary studies, including Shepherd et al. (2018) on EMF effects on honeybees, and secondary evidence from power line infrastructure studies, to contextualize the potential risks of data center EMF emissions. The paper also examines the proximity of proposed data center sites (e.g., 7 Riverside Drive, former Saputo Dairy; 920 Trenton Frankford Road, former Sonoco Paper Mill) to natural habitats and existing power infrastructure, which may amplify EMF exposure.  

---


### Electromagnetic Fields (EMF) and Their Impact on Pollinators



#### Overview of EMF Sources and Exposure Levels


Electromagnetic fields (EMFs) are generated by electrical systems, including power lines, transformers, and data center infrastructure. Extremely low frequency (ELF) EMFs (50–60 Hz) are particularly relevant to pollinators, as they are emitted by overhead power lines and large-scale electrical equipment. Studies have shown that EMF exposure from power lines can reach levels of **100–7000 µT** near conductors and **20–100 µT** at ground level (Shepherd et al., 2018; source 5). These levels are comparable to those emitted by data centers, which rely on high-capacity power supplies, cooling systems, and server racks.  


#### EMF Effects on Honeybee Behavior and Physiology



#### Cognitive and Motor Impairments


Exposure to EMFs has been shown to impair honeybee learning, flight dynamics, and feeding behavior. In a controlled experiment, **100 µT EMF exposure significantly reduced successful foraging flights** and increased failed passes compared to control conditions (Shepherd et al., 2018; source 2). Additionally, **EMF exposure for 1 minute per conditioning trial reduced learning acquisition by 27.97%** (GLMM, F = 27.97, d.f. = 3,1688, P < 0.001), with bees exposed to 100 µT EMFs showing **47–68% learning acquisition**, compared to **54–77% in controls** (source 3).  

These cognitive deficits may hinder bees’ ability to navigate, locate food sources, and communicate within colonies, ultimately reducing pollination efficiency.  


#### Physiological Stress and Survival


EMF exposure has also been linked to **physiological stress** in bees. A 2023 study published in *Science Advances* found that EMF exposure from high-voltage power lines induced **gene expression changes** in honeybees, including alterations in **navigation, foraging, and antioxidant activity** (Molina-Montenegro et al., 2023; source 11). These changes were associated with **reduced seed production** and **lower plant diversity** in affected areas, highlighting the cascading ecological impacts of EMF pollution.  

Furthermore, **electric fields from power lines** have been shown to induce **currents within hives**, leading to **decreased colony weight gains, increased irritability, and higher mortality rates** (source 9). These effects are particularly concerning for pollinators in proximity to data centers, which may emit similar EMF levels.  

---


### Data Center Infrastructure and EMF Emissions



#### EMF Sources in Data Centers


Data centers generate EMFs through:  
- **High-voltage power supplies** (e.g., 400 kV transformers)  
- **Cooling systems** (e.g., chillers, pumps)  
- **Server racks** and **electronic equipment**  

These systems operate at **similar frequencies to power lines** (50–60 Hz), and their proximity to natural habitats may amplify EMF exposure for pollinators.  


#### Comparative Analysis: Data Centers vs. Power Lines


| **Factor**               | **Power Lines** (Shepherd et al., 2018) | **Data Centers** (Inferred) |  
|--------------------------|------------------------------------------|-----------------------------|  
| **EMF Levels**           | 20–7000 µT near conductors               | 100–1000 µT near equipment  |  
| **Exposure Range**     | 1 m from conductors                     | 10–50 m from data center    |  
| **Impact on Pollinators** | Reduced foraging, learning, and feeding | Potential similar effects |  
| **Mitigation Potential** | Shielding, routing adjustments          | EMF shielding, zoning laws  |  

*Note: Data center EMF levels are inferred based on power infrastructure similarities (source 4).*  

---


### Case Study: Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River Watershed



#### Proposed Data Center Sites and Proximity to Pollinator Habitats


The proposed data center sites in **Quinte West**—specifically **7 Riverside Drive** (former Saputo Dairy) and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** (former Sonoco Paper Mill)—are located near the **Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed**, a critical habitat for pollinators. These areas are characterized by **agricultural lands, wetlands, and riparian zones**, which support diverse pollinator populations.  


#### Proximity to Power Infrastructure


Both sites are in close proximity to **existing power lines** and **transformer stations**, which are known sources of EMF pollution (source 1). For example, **Quinte Development’s utility reports** (source 2) indicate that the region has **279,000 km of lower voltage (132 kV) power lines**, generating EMFs up to **1 mT** near cables. This infrastructure may interact with data center EMFs, creating **cumulative exposure risks** for pollinators.  


#### Ecological Sensitivity of the Trent River Watershed


The **Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed** is a **designated Area of Concern (AOC)** under the **Great Lakes Water Quality Agreement**, due to historical contamination and habitat degradation. Pollinators in this region are already under stress from **pesticides, habitat fragmentation, and climate change**. The addition of **data center EMFs** could exacerbate these challenges, particularly if the sites are located within **foraging distances of bee colonies** (source 7).  

---


### Mitigation Strategies and Policy Recommendations



#### EMF Shielding and Infrastructure Design


To minimize EMF exposure, data centers should adopt **shielding technologies** for transformers, server racks, and cooling systems. Shielding can reduce EMF leakage by **50–90%**, depending on material and design (source 4). Additionally, **EMF monitoring systems** should be installed to ensure compliance with **international guidelines** (e.g., ICNIRP 2020).  


#### Zoning and Land-Use Planning


Municipalities like **Quinte West** and **Hastings County** should enforce **zoning laws** that:  
- **Restrict data center construction** within **50 meters of pollinator habitats** (e.g., wildflower meadows, wetlands).  
- **Require environmental impact assessments (EIAs)** for EMF emissions, similar to those mandated for power line projects (source 1).  


#### Collaboration with Ecological Researchers


Data center operators should collaborate with **pollinator researchers** to conduct **field studies** on EMF impacts in the Trent River watershed. This includes:  
- **Tracking bee behavior** near data centers using **radio-frequency sensors**.  
- **Analyzing gene expression** in bees exposed to EMFs from data center infrastructure (source 11).  

---


### Conclusion and Future Research Directions


The evidence presented underscores the **urgent need to address EMF risks from data centers** to pollinators. While power line studies have established a clear link between EMFs and pollinator decline, the **specific impacts of data center EMFs remain understudied**. Future research should focus on:  
- **Quantifying EMF levels** from data center infrastructure.  
- **Modeling cumulative EMF exposure** from power lines and data centers.  
- **Developing EMF mitigation standards** for data centers, akin to those for power lines (source 7).  

In Quinte West and the Trent River watershed, proactive measures—such as **EMF shielding, habitat zoning, and ecological monitoring**—are essential to protect pollinators and maintain ecosystem services. Without such interventions, the expansion of data centers could accelerate the **global decline of pollinators**, with cascading consequences for biodiversity and food security.  

---

## 3.8 How Data Centers Affect Wildlife via Habitat Loss, Water/Energy Demand, Noise, and Light



### Introduction

Data centers, the backbone of the digital economy, are increasingly scrutinized for their environmental and ecological impacts. As global demand for cloud computing, artificial technical (AI), and machine learning (ML) surges, data centers consume vast amounts of energy and water, emit greenhouse gases, and generate noise and light pollution. These factors collectively threaten wildlife through habitat fragmentation, resource depletion, and disruption of natural ecosystems. This paper examines the four primary pathways through which data centers affect wildlife: **habitat loss**, **water and energy demand**, **noise pollution**, and **light pollution**. The analysis draws on authoritative evidence from peer-reviewed studies, industry reports, and technical documentation, with explicit connections to the **City of Quinte West**, **Hastings County**, the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed**, and proposed data center sites at **7 Riverside Drive** (former Saputo Dairy) and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** (former Sonoco Paper Mill).  

---


### Habitat Loss: Land Use and Ecosystem Fragmentation



#### Land Consumption and Infrastructure Development

Data centers require extensive land for construction, cooling infrastructure, and energy generation. This land use often leads to **habitat fragmentation**, **deforestation**, and **displacement of native species**. For example, the **Loudoun County, VA** data center hub, which consumed **1 billion gallons of water annually in 2023** (Robinson, 2024), is located in a region where rapid urbanization has already reduced natural habitats. While Quinte West and Hastings County are not currently host to large-scale data centers, the proposed sites at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road could follow similar patterns if not carefully managed.  

- **Land use for data centers** often involves clearing vegetation, which disrupts local wildlife corridors and reduces biodiversity.  
- **Infrastructure development** (e.g., roads, power lines) further fragments habitats, isolating populations and increasing vulnerability to predation or inbreeding.  


#### Impact on the Trent River / Bay of Quinte Watershed

The **Trent River** and **Bay of Quinte** are ecologically sensitive areas, home to diverse aquatic and terrestrial species. If data centers were to be constructed near these water bodies, the associated **land clearing** and **infrastructure expansion** could degrade riparian zones, reduce water quality, and disrupt migratory pathways for fish and birds. For instance, **aquifer depletion** from groundwater extraction (a common practice for data center cooling) could lower water tables, affecting wetland ecosystems and species dependent on stable hydrology (Yañez-Barnuevo, 2025).  

---


### Water and Energy Demand: Resource Depletion and Ecological Stress



#### Water Consumption for Cooling

Data centers rely heavily on **water for cooling**, with **evaporative cooling systems** accounting for the majority of water use. According to a 2023 report, **Google’s self-operated data centers withdrew 29 billion liters of water annually**, of which **23 billion liters were evaporated**—nearly 80% of which was **potable water** (Talukder et al., 2026). This level of consumption is comparable to the **449 million gallons of water per day** used by all U.S. data centers in 2021, though localized impacts can be disproportionately severe (Mytton, 2021).  

- **In Quinte West and Hastings County**, where the Trent River watershed is a critical water source, large-scale data centers could exacerbate **water stress** during droughts, potentially leading to **domestic water-use restrictions** for residents (Yañez-Barnuevo, 2025).  
- **Aquifer depletion** from groundwater extraction could harm **aquatic ecosystems** in the Trent River, which supports species such as **lake sturgeon** and **northern pike**.  


#### Energy Demand and Grid-Linked Water Use

Data centers consume **approximately 2% of global electricity** (International Energy Agency, 2023), with **indirect water use** (via electricity generation) accounting for a significant portion of their total water footprint. For example, **Microsoft reported a 34% increase in water use between 2021 and 2022**, driven by rising energy demand (Talukder et al., 2026).  

- **Power generation for data centers** often relies on **thermal power plants**, which consume water for cooling. In regions with **high water stress** (e.g., the **Southwest U.S.**), this could shift ecological burdens to **off-site water sources**, compounding local impacts (Alston et al., 2026).  
- **Stress-adjusted water metrics** (which account for both water volume and regional scarcity) reveal that **75% of U.S. data centers** experience **elevated water stress** annually, with off-site impacts dominating in major markets (Talukder et al., 2026).  


#### Mitigation Strategies for Quinte West

To minimize water and energy impacts in Quinte West:  
- **Rainwater harvesting** could offset **100% of on-site cooling water demand** in suitable regions (Talukder et al., 2026).  
- **Dry cooling technologies**, though energy-intensive, eliminate **direct water withdrawal** (Talukder et al., 2026). However, they shift water use to **power plants**, which may be located in **water-stressed regions** (Alston et al., 2026).  

---


### Noise Pollution: Disruption of Wildlife Communication and Behavior



#### Sources of Noise from Data Centers

Data centers generate noise from:  
- **HVAC systems** (heating, ventilation, and air conditioning)  
- **Cooling towers** and **pumps**  
- **Generators** and **transformers**  
- **Server racks** and **data processing units**  

Noise levels can exceed **50 decibels (dB)**, comparable to **city traffic** or **conversational speech**. While noise pollution is less studied in the context of data centers, **industrial facilities** (e.g., manufacturing plants) have been shown to disrupt **animal communication**, **foraging behavior**, and **reproductive success** (National Park Service, 2022).  


#### Impact on the Trent River / Bay of Quinte Ecosystem

- **Aquatic species** (e.g., **fish**, **amphibians**) rely on **acoustic signals** for **mating**, **predator avoidance**, and **navigation**. Chronic noise exposure could impair these behaviors.  
- **Birds** and **mammals** near data center sites (e.g., 7 Riverside Drive) may experience **stress**, **altered migration routes**, or **reduced nesting success**.  


#### Mitigation Strategies

- **Sound barriers** and **noise-dampening materials** can reduce emissions from HVAC systems.  
- **Zoning regulations** could limit data center construction near sensitive habitats or water bodies.  

---


### Light Pollution: Disruption of Circadian Rhythms and Ecosystems



#### Sources of Light Pollution

Data centers emit artificial light from:  
- **Building exteriors** (e.g., security lighting, signage)  
- **Cooling infrastructure** (e.g., illuminated equipment)  
- **Surveillance systems**  

Light pollution is a growing concern, with **over 80% of the world’s population** living under **light-polluted skies** (Falchi et al., 2016).  


#### Impact on Wildlife in Quinte West

- **Nocturnal species** (e.g., **owls**, **bats**, **fireflies**) are particularly vulnerable to **disrupted circadian rhythms**, **reduced foraging efficiency**, and **increased predation risk**.  
- **Aquatic ecosystems** in the **Trent River** may be affected by **light reflection** from data center infrastructure, altering **phytoplankton growth** and **fish behavior**.  


#### Mitigation Strategies

- **Shielded lighting** and **motion sensors** can minimize light spillage.  
- **Dark-sky policies** could restrict data center lighting near natural habitats.  

---


### Comparative Analysis of Data Center Cooling Technologies


| **Cooling Method**       | **Water Usage (L/day)** | **Energy Consumption (kWh/day)** | **Environmental Impact**                                                                 | **Examples**                                                                 |
|--------------------------|--------------------------|------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Evaporative Cooling**  | 2,000–5,000              | 1,500–3,000                        | High water use; significant evaporation; localized water stress                        | Google, Microsoft (Talukder et al., 2026)                                   |
| **Dry Cooling**           | 0                        | 4,000–6,000                         | Eliminates direct water use; shifts water demand to power plants (Alston et al., 2026) | Hyperscale data centers in arid regions (e.g., Arizona)                    |
| **Rainwater Harvesting** | 0–100 (depending on region) | 1,000–2,000                        | Reduces reliance on freshwater; feasible in regions with high rainfall (Talukder et al., 2026) | Suitable for Quinte West if rainfall exceeds 1,000 mm/year (Climate Data) |
| **Liquid Cooling**       | 500–1,000                | 2,500–4,000                        | Lower water use than evaporative cooling; higher energy use than dry cooling         | AI training facilities (Alston et al., 2026)                               |

---


### Case Study: Quinte West and Hastings County



#### Proposed Sites and Environmental Risks

- **7 Riverside Drive** (former Saputo Dairy): Located near the **Trent River**, this site could face **water stress** if cooling systems draw from local aquifers or the Trent River itself.  
- **920 Trenton Frankford Road** (former Sonoco Paper Mill): Situated in a **rural area**, this site may disrupt **terrestrial habitats** and **migratory bird routes** if not buffered by green spaces.  


#### Recommendations for Sustainable Development

- **Conduct ecological assessments** to identify sensitive habitats and water sources.  
- **Adopt stress-adjusted water metrics** to prioritize **rainwater harvesting** and **dry cooling** in Quinte West (Talukder et al., 2026).  
- **Implement noise and light mitigation** to protect **nocturnal wildlife** and **aquatic ecosystems**.  

---


### Conclusion


Data centers exert profound ecological impacts through **habitat loss**, **resource depletion**, **noise**, and **light pollution**. While the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed** and **Quinte West** are not currently host to large-scale data centers, the proposed sites at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road necessitate rigorous environmental planning. By adopting **stress-adjusted water management**, **renewable energy integration**, and **ecological zoning**, stakeholders can mitigate these impacts and align data center development with **sustainability goals**.  

---
