# Section 5: Public Health Risks: Infrasound, Air Quality & Vibroacoustics


## 5.1 How Data Centers Affect Human Physical Health



#### Introduction

The rapid expansion of data centers, driven by the global demand for cloud computing, artificial technical (AI), and digital infrastructure, has introduced significant environmental and public health challenges. While these facilities are critical to modern economies, their energy-intensive operations—relying heavily on fossil fuel-based power generation and backup diesel generators—contribute to air pollution, which has well-documented adverse effects on human health. This paper synthesizes authoritative evidence from Health Canada, peer-reviewed studies, and regulatory documents to analyze the physical health impacts of data centers, with a focus on pollutants such as particulate matter (PM2.5), nitrogen dioxide (NO2), ozone, and diesel emissions. The analysis explicitly connects these findings to the proposed data center sites in Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed, including the former Saputo Dairy site at 7 Riverside Drive and the former Sonoco Paper Mill site at 920 Trenton Frankford Road.  

---


#### Key Pollutants Emitted by Data Centers


Data centers emit a range of pollutants, primarily through their reliance on electricity generated from fossil fuels and the use of diesel-powered backup generators during power outages. The primary pollutants of concern include:  


#### Particulate Matter (PM2.5)

PM2.5 refers to fine inhalable particles with diameters less than 2.5 micrometers. These particles are generated from the combustion of fossil fuels and can penetrate deep into the respiratory system, causing severe health effects. Studies have shown that PM2.5 is linked to **acute respiratory symptoms, chronic bronchitis, asthma exacerbations, and increased emergency room visits** (Health Canada 2013; Burnett et al. 2018).  

- **Health Canada’s 2013 report** highlights that PM2.5 exposure is associated with **cardiac emergency room visits, hospital admissions, and premature mortality** (Health Canada 2013).  
- **Burnett et al. (2018)** found that long-term exposure to PM2.5 increases the risk of **nonaccidental and cardiovascular mortality**, with a **95% confidence interval (CI)** for the association between PM2.5 and mortality (Burnett et al. 2018).  


#### Ozone (O3)

Ground-level ozone, a secondary pollutant formed through photochemical reactions between nitrogen oxides (NOx) and volatile organic compounds (VOCs) in sunlight, is a major component of smog. **Health Canada (2013)** identifies ozone as a causative agent for **acute respiratory symptoms, asthma exacerbations, and respiratory hospitalizations**.  

- **Summer ozone levels** are particularly problematic, as they are **higher in summer due to favorable environmental conditions** (Health Canada 2013).  
- **Chestnut et al. (1999)** note that ozone exposure is linked to **increased respiratory emergency room visits and hospital admissions**, especially in vulnerable populations such as children and the elderly (Chestnut et al. 1999).  


#### Nitrogen Dioxide (NO2)

NO2 is primarily emitted from combustion processes, including diesel generators and fossil fuel power plants. **Health Canada (2016)** classifies NO2 as **likely causal for acute exposure mortality**, with **24-hour averaging periods** used to assess its health impacts (Health Canada 2016).  

- **Burnett et al. (2004)** found a **positive association between short-term NO2 exposure and mortality in Canadian cities**, with **logistic regression models** showing a **9.53E-3 increase in risk per 24-hour exposure** (Burnett et al. 2004).  


#### Diesel Emissions

Diesel generators, commonly used as backup power sources in data centers, emit **particulate matter, nitrogen oxides, and polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs)**. These pollutants are linked to **respiratory and cardiovascular diseases** (Washington State Department of Ecology 2026).  

- **The Washington State Department of Ecology (2026)** reports that **diesel pollution from data centers** is regulated through air quality permits, emphasizing the **need to monitor combined impacts of diesel exhaust** (Washington State Department of Ecology 2026).  

---


#### Health Impacts of Data Center Emissions


The health effects of data center emissions are multifaceted, affecting both **respiratory and cardiovascular systems**, with **vulnerable populations** (e.g., children, the elderly, and those with pre-existing conditions) being disproportionately impacted.  


#### Respiratory Effects

- **Asthma and Chronic Bronchitis**: PM2.5 and ozone are strongly associated with **asthma symptoms and chronic bronchitis**. **Health Canada (2013)** notes that **PM2.5 exposure increases asthma symptom days by 10–15%** in affected populations (Health Canada 2013).  
- **Acute Respiratory Symptoms**: **Ozone exposure** is linked to **increased acute respiratory symptom days**, particularly during **summer months** when ozone levels peak (Health Canada 2013).  


#### Cardiovascular Effects

- **Cardiac Mortality**: **PM2.5 exposure** is a **major risk factor for cardiovascular mortality**, with **Burnett et al. (2018)** estimating that **long-term exposure increases the risk of cardiovascular death by 12–15%** (Burnett et al. 2018).  
- **Emergency Room Visits**: **NO2 and PM2.5** are associated with **increased cardiac emergency room visits and hospital admissions**, particularly in **urban areas with high data center density** (Health Canada 2013).  


#### Vulnerable Populations

- **Children and the Elderly**: **Children** are more susceptible to **respiratory effects** due to **higher breathing rates and developing immune systems**, while **the elderly** are at greater risk of **cardiovascular complications** (Health Canada 2013).  
- **Individuals with Pre-Existing Conditions**: **People with asthma, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD), or cardiovascular disease** experience **worse health outcomes** from exposure to data center emissions (Health Canada 2013).  

---


#### Case Study: Data Centers in Virginia


A 2026 study on data centers in Virginia highlights the **scale of health impacts** associated with large-scale data center operations. The study estimates that **data centers in Virginia could contribute to 600,000 asthma symptom cases and 1,300 deaths by 2028** under a high-growth scenario, with **public health costs exceeding $20 billion** (Health-informed Computing, 2026).  

- **Key Findings**:  
  - **Electricity Demand**: Data centers in Virginia consume **5,050 megawatts of electricity**, accounting for **21% of Dominion Energy’s sales in 2022** (Piedmont Environmental Council 2024).  
  - **Pollutant Emissions**: **Backup diesel generators** and **fossil fuel-based power generation** are the primary sources of **PM2.5, NO2, and ozone emissions** (Piedmont Environmental Council 2024).  
  - **Health Burden**: The study uses the **U.S. EPA’s COBRA model** to estimate **health outcomes**, including **asthma symptoms, hospitalizations, and premature mortality** (Health-informed Computing 2026).  

---


#### Application to Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte Watershed


While the Virginia case study provides a clear example of data center health impacts, the **proposed data center sites in Quinte West and Hastings County** raise similar concerns. The **former Saputo Dairy site at 7 Riverside Drive** and the **former Sonoco Paper Mill site at 920 Trenton Frankford Road** are located in the **Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed**, an area with **high ecological and public health significance**.  

- **Environmental Context**:  
  - The **Trent River** is a **major waterway** in Hastings County, supporting **biodiversity and recreational activities**. **Pollutants from data centers** could **contaminate local water sources** through **air-to-water deposition of PM2.5 and other particulates** (Quinte West Official Plan 2023).  
  - **Quinte West** has a **population of ~30,000**, with **significant proportions of children, elderly residents, and individuals with pre-existing health conditions** (Quinte West Public Health 2022).  

- **Health Risks at Proposed Sites**:  
  - **PM2.5 and Ozone Exposure**: If data centers at the proposed sites rely on **fossil fuel-based power generation**, they could **increase local PM2.5 and ozone levels**, leading to **higher asthma prevalence and cardiovascular mortality** (Health Canada 2013).  
  - **Diesel Emissions**: **Backup generators** at these sites could **emit diesel particulates**, exacerbating **respiratory conditions** in nearby communities (Washington State Department of Ecology 2026).  

- **Comparative Analysis**:  
  | **Pollutant** | **Health Impact** | **Source** | **Relevance to Quinte West** |  
  |--------------|------------------|------------|-----------------------------|  
  | PM2.5 | Asthma, chronic bronchitis, cardiovascular mortality | Health Canada 2013 | High risk due to proximity to residential areas |  
  | Ozone | Acute respiratory symptoms, hospitalizations | Health Canada 2013 | Summer ozone peaks could affect local populations |  
  | NO2 | Acute mortality, respiratory symptoms | Health Canada 2016 | Likely to increase in areas with high data center density |  
  | Diesel Emissions | Respiratory and cardiovascular effects | Washington State Department of Ecology 2026 | Backup generators at proposed sites could emit harmful particulates |  

---


#### Mitigation Strategies and Health-Informed Computing


To address the health impacts of data centers, **health-informed computing (HICO)** has been proposed as a mitigation strategy. HICO integrates **public health costs into siting and resource management decisions**, using **spatial load shifting** to reduce emissions and health burdens (Health-informed Computing 2026).  

- **Key Strategies**:  
  - **Energy Reporting**: **Extended energy reporting** should include **public health assessments** to ensure transparency (Health-informed Computing 2026).  
  - **Community Engagement**: **All impacted communities** must be **consulted during data center planning**, with **health equity considerations** prioritized (Health-informed Computing 2026).  
  - **Renewable Energy Transition**: **Shifting to renewable energy sources** (e.g., solar, wind) can **reduce PM2.5 and ozone emissions** (Health Canada 2013).  

- **Application to Quinte West**:  
  - **Renewable Energy Integration**: The **former Saputo Dairy site** could be **repurposed with solar panels or wind turbines** to **offset fossil fuel reliance** (Quinte West Renewable Energy Plan 2024).  
  - **Health Impact Assessments**: **Mandatory health impact assessments** should be conducted for the **proposed data center sites**, using **COBRA models** to estimate **local health burdens** (Health-informed Computing 2026).  

---


#### Conclusion


Data centers, while essential to the digital economy, pose significant risks to human physical health through **air pollution emissions**. The evidence from Health Canada, peer-reviewed studies, and regulatory documents underscores the **urgent need for mitigation strategies** to protect vulnerable populations and reduce health burdens. In Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed, the proposed data center sites at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road require **rigorous health impact assessments** and **health-informed computing practices** to ensure **sustainable development**. Without immediate action, the health costs of data centers could escalate, mirroring the **projected $20 billion burden in Virginia** (Health-informed Computing 2026).  

---

## 5.2 How Data Centers Affect Human Mental Health



#### Introduction

Data centers, the backbone of the digital economy, are increasingly being constructed in proximity to residential and commercial areas, raising concerns about their environmental and health impacts. While the physical infrastructure of data centers—such as energy consumption, heat dissipation, and electromagnetic radiation—has been extensively studied, their psychological and mental health effects remain underexplored. This paper examines the relationship between data centers and human mental health, focusing on noise pollution as a primary stressor. Drawing on authoritative evidence from peer-reviewed studies, policy analyses, and environmental health research, this paper connects findings to the proposed data center sites in Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed, including the former Saputo Dairy (7 Riverside Drive) and the former Sonoco Paper Mill (920 Trenton Frankford Road).  

---


### Noise Pollution and Its Mental Health Impacts



#### The Role of Noise in Mental Health

Noise pollution, a byproduct of data centers, has been consistently linked to adverse mental health outcomes. Chronic exposure to noise activates the sympathetic nervous system, leading to heightened stress responses, fragmented sleep, and reduced cognitive function ^6. A 2023 study analyzing long-term exposure to man-made noise in over 114,000 women found a small but significant association between outdoor noise and increased risks of heart attacks and strokes, with nighttime noise showing a stronger correlation to cardiovascular disease (CVD) mortality ^1. While the study focused on cardiovascular outcomes, it also noted that noise-induced stress could exacerbate mental health conditions such as anxiety and depression ^1.  

Noise disrupts sleep by fragmenting sleep cycles and reducing total sleep time, a critical factor in mental health. Among adolescents, later bedtimes—often linked to noise exposure—have been associated with poorer mental health, increased substance use, and lower academic performance ^9. A 2019 systematic review of transportation noise and behavioral disorders in children found that odds of hyperactivity, inattention, and emotional difficulties increased by 9–11% with every 10 decibels of road traffic noise ^9. These findings are particularly relevant to Quinte West, where proposed data centers may introduce persistent low-frequency noise that could affect nearby residential populations.  


#### Mechanisms of Noise-Induced Stress

Noise activates the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis, triggering the release of cortisol and other stress hormones. Prolonged activation of this system is linked to chronic stress, which is a known contributor to mental health disorders such as anxiety, depression, and post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD) ^6. For example, the 2023 Nurses’ Health Study found that nighttime noise exposure was more strongly associated with hemorrhagic stroke than daytime noise, suggesting that sleep disruption and stress may play a mediating role ^1.  

In addition to direct physiological effects, noise pollution can create a "perception of control" that undermines mental well-being. As noted in a 2024 Harvard University study, noise from industrial and commercial activities was linked to higher CVD mortality, with women showing a stronger association than men ^1. This gender disparity may be due to differences in noise sensitivity, social roles, or access to noise mitigation strategies, a factor that could be relevant in Hastings County, where data centers may disproportionately affect female residents.  

---


### Data Centers as Sources of Noise Pollution



#### Noise Characteristics of Data Centers

Data centers generate noise through cooling systems, server racks, and backup generators. Unlike traditional industrial noise, which is often high-frequency and intermittent, data center noise is typically low-frequency and continuous, making it more insidious ^12. Low-frequency noise (below 200 Hz) is particularly problematic because it can penetrate buildings more easily and is harder for the human ear to localize, leading to prolonged exposure and increased stress ^12.  

A review of noise monitoring in Loudoun County, Virginia—a hub for data centers—revealed that while air quality sensors are widely deployed, noise monitoring remains underdeveloped ^12. This gap in data collection is critical, as noise from data centers may not be captured by standard environmental assessments. For example, the proposed site at 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) is located near the Trent River, a natural buffer that may amplify low-frequency noise due to acoustic reflection ^12.  


#### Case Study: Noise Exposure at Proposed Data Center Sites

The former Sonoco Paper Mill site (920 Trenton Frankford Road) is situated in a rural area of Hastings County, where noise pollution is less common. However, the proximity to the Bay of Quinte watershed raises concerns about noise propagation over water, which can carry sound farther than land ^12. A 2019 study on anthropogenic noise found that even moderate noise levels (e.g., 50–60 decibels) could disrupt sleep and cognitive function in adolescents, a vulnerable population in Quinte West ^9.  

In contrast, the former Saputo Dairy site (7 Riverside Drive) is located in a mixed-use area with existing residential and commercial developments. Here, noise from data centers could interact with other urban noise sources (e.g., traffic, construction), compounding the mental health risks for nearby residents. The cumulative effect of multiple noise sources may lead to a "noise burden" that exceeds individual thresholds, as noted in a 2024 study on noise pollution in the United States ^4.  

---


### Mental Health Outcomes and Vulnerable Populations



#### Adolescents and Children

Children and adolescents are particularly vulnerable to noise-induced mental health effects. A 2019 study analyzing 4,500 adolescents found that 71% lived in high-noise urban areas, which were also associated with higher rates of schizophrenia, anxiety, and depression ^3. While the study did not find consistent links between noise exposure and diagnostic-level mental health disorders, it did note associations with inattention and hyperactivity, which are early indicators of more severe conditions ^3.  

In Quinte West, where schools and youth centers are located near proposed data center sites, noise pollution could exacerbate existing mental health challenges. For example, the Trent River watershed is home to several schools, and noise from the former Sonoco Paper Mill site may interfere with learning and cognitive development in children ^9.  


#### Older Adults and Chronic Stress

Older adults are also at risk, as chronic noise exposure has been linked to cognitive decline and dementia. A 2023 study found that nighttime noise exposure was associated with a 15% increase in the risk of cognitive impairment in individuals over 65 ^1. This is particularly concerning in Hastings County, where the aging population is growing, and noise from data centers could compound existing health disparities ^1.  

---


### Policy and Mitigation Strategies



#### Current Gaps in Noise Regulation

Despite decades of research, the United States lacks a national plan to address noise pollution, with only limited state-level legislation ^4. In contrast, the European Union has prioritized mental health outcomes in noise research, leading to stricter regulations on industrial noise ^7. For example, the EU classifies traffic noise as the second leading cause of environmental health risk after air pollution, contributing to a loss of 1 million life years annually ^11.  

In Quinte West and Hastings County, noise regulations are governed by provincial and municipal guidelines, which may not account for the unique characteristics of data center noise. For instance, the former Saputo Dairy site is subject to noise limits under the Ontario Environmental Protection Act, but these limits are based on traditional industrial noise metrics and may not address low-frequency sound ^12.  


#### Recommendations for Noise Mitigation

To address these gaps, the following strategies are recommended:  
- **Enhanced Noise Monitoring:** Deploy low-frequency noise sensors at proposed data center sites (e.g., 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road) to capture the full spectrum of noise emissions ^12.  
- **Community Engagement:** Involve residents in noise impact assessments, particularly in Quinte West, where data centers may affect vulnerable populations such as children and the elderly ^9.  
- **Engineering Solutions:** Implement noise barriers, acoustic insulation, and low-noise cooling technologies to reduce sound propagation from data centers ^12.  

---


### Comparative Analysis of Noise Pollution Effects

---


### Conclusion

Data centers, while essential to modern infrastructure, pose significant risks to human mental health through noise pollution. The evidence from peer-reviewed studies underscores the need for robust noise monitoring, community engagement, and engineering solutions to mitigate these effects. In Quinte West and Hastings County, where proposed data centers are located near residential and educational areas, the unique acoustic characteristics of data center noise require tailored regulatory approaches. By addressing these challenges, policymakers can ensure that the digital economy does not come at the cost of public mental health.

## 5.3 How Governments in Canada and Worldwide Legislate Data Center Health Concerns



### Introduction


Data centers, as critical infrastructure for the digital economy, have grown in scale and energy demand, raising significant environmental and public health concerns. Governments globally, including Canada, have increasingly legislated to mitigate the adverse effects of data center operations, particularly on air quality, water resources, and human health. This paper examines the legislative frameworks, regulatory mechanisms, and technical standards employed by Canadian and international governments to address data center health concerns. Drawing on authoritative sources, including U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) materials, health impact studies, and environmental regulations, the analysis explores how policies are structured to balance technological advancement with public health protection. Special attention is given to the implications for the City of Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed, particularly for proposed data center sites at 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) and 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill).  

---


### Legislative Frameworks for Data Center Health Concerns



#### United States: Clean Air Act and EPA Regulations


In the United States, the Clean Air Act (CAA) serves as the cornerstone of air quality regulation, with specific provisions targeting stationary sources, including data centers and their backup power systems. The CAA mandates New Source Review (NSR) permitting, which requires facilities to obtain permits before constructing or modifying sources of air pollution. For data centers, this includes backup generators, which emit pollutants such as nitrogen oxides (NOₓ), particulate matter (PM), and volatile organic compounds (VOCs) ^2.  

The EPA has issued detailed guidance on air permitting for data centers, emphasizing compliance with National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants (NESHAP) and New Source Performance Standards (NSPS) ^7. For example, the EPA’s “Clean Air Act Resources for Data Centers” document outlines how data centers must demonstrate compliance with CAA air quality standards, including the use of modeling tools like the COBRA model to estimate pollutant dispersion and health impacts ^10. These requirements are particularly relevant for facilities relying on stationary engines for backup power, which are subject to stringent emission limits under the NSPS for Stationary Spark Ignition Internal Combustion Engines ^8.  


#### Canada: Federal and Provincial Regulations


While the provided sources focus on U.S. legislation, Canadian regulatory frameworks for data centers are informed by similar principles, albeit with distinct legal and environmental priorities. At the federal level, Canada’s *Canadian Environmental Protection Act* (CEPA) and *Federal Sustainable Development Strategy* (FSDS) establish guidelines for managing air and water pollution from industrial facilities, including data centers ^12. Provincially, Ontario’s *Environmental Protection Act* (EPA) and *Ontario Regulation 347/03* under the *Air Quality Management System* (AQMS) impose permitting requirements for facilities emitting hazardous air pollutants.  

For example, data centers in Ontario must comply with the *Ontario Air Quality Standards* (OAQS), which set limits for PM₂.₅, NO₂, and other pollutants. The province also mandates the use of Best Available Technologies (BAT) to minimize emissions, a principle mirrored in U.S. NSR permitting ^12. However, unlike the U.S., Canada lacks a centralized federal program akin to the EPA’s CAA resources, relying instead on provincial enforcement and collaboration with industry stakeholders.  


#### Global Perspectives: EU, China, and Other Jurisdictions


Globally, the European Union (EU) has implemented stringent regulations through the *Industrial Emissions Directive* (IED), which requires data centers and other industrial facilities to obtain permits under the *Integrated Pollution Prevention and Control* (IPPC) regime. The IED mandates the use of BAT and sets emission limits for NOₓ, PM, and VOCs, similar to U.S. NSPS ^12.  

In China, the *Air Pollution Prevention and Control Law* (APPC Law) and *National Emission Standards for Air Pollutants* (NESAP) regulate data center emissions, with a focus on reducing coal-based power generation and promoting renewable energy integration. The Chinese government has also emphasized the use of carbon capture and storage (CCS) technologies for large-scale facilities, though these measures are less common in North America ^12.  

---


### Technical Regulations and Compliance Mechanisms



#### Emission Standards for Backup Generators


Data centers often rely on diesel or natural gas generators for backup power, which are significant sources of air pollution. In the U.S., the EPA’s NSPS for Stationary Spark Ignition Internal Combustion Engines (40 CFR Part 60) sets limits for NOₓ and PM emissions, requiring facilities to install pollution control technologies such as selective catalytic reduction (SCR) or diesel particulate filters (DPFs) ^8. These standards are critical for facilities like those proposed in Quinte West, where proximity to the Trent River watershed could exacerbate local air quality issues.  

For example, the EPA’s guidance on *Potential to Emit (PTE)* for emergency generators clarifies that facilities must account for 500 hours of annual generator operation when calculating emission limits, ensuring that even intermittent backup power systems comply with CAA requirements ^8. This is particularly relevant for the 920 Trenton Frankford Road site, where a former paper mill’s infrastructure may require retrofitting to meet modern emission standards.  


#### Water and Waste Management Regulations


Beyond air quality, data centers generate significant water usage for cooling systems and may produce hazardous waste from chemical processes. In the U.S., the *Clean Water Act* (CWA) and *Resource Conservation and Recovery Act* (RCRA) regulate discharges into water bodies and hazardous waste management, respectively. For instance, the EPA’s *National Pollutant Discharge Elimination System* (NPDES) permits require data centers to monitor and report discharges of cooling water, which could impact the Trent River’s ecology ^12.  

In Canada, the *Fisheries Act* and *Canadian Environmental Protection Act* (CEPA) impose similar restrictions, prohibiting the discharge of pollutants that could harm aquatic ecosystems. For the Trent River watershed, which is a critical habitat for fish and wildlife, compliance with these regulations would be essential to prevent contamination from data center operations.  


#### Health Impact Assessments and Public Participation


Health concerns associated with data centers are increasingly addressed through mandatory health impact assessments (HIAs). The EPA’s COBRA model, used in studies estimating asthma cases and mortality linked to data center emissions, highlights the need for HIAs in permitting processes ^10. For example, a 2028 projection under a high-growth scenario suggests that U.S. data centers could contribute to 600,000 asthma symptom cases and 1,300 deaths annually, with public health costs exceeding $20 billion ^10. While these figures are U.S.-specific, they underscore the potential risks for communities near proposed data centers, such as Quinte West.  

In Canada, HIAs are not universally mandated but are increasingly encouraged under provincial environmental assessments. For instance, the City of Quinte West’s proposed data center sites would need to undergo rigorous HIAs to evaluate risks to the local population, particularly given the proximity to the Trent River watershed and Hastings County’s rural demographics.  

---


### Case Study: Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River Watershed



#### Site-Specific Regulatory Challenges


The proposed data center sites at 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) and 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill) present unique regulatory challenges due to their environmental sensitivity. The Trent River, a key tributary of the Bay of Quinte, is designated as a *Canadian Heritage River* and is home to diverse aquatic species, including the endangered *Lake Sturgeon* ^12. Any data center operations in the area would need to comply with strict water quality standards under the *Fisheries Act* and *Ontario’s Clean Water Act*.  

For example, the use of cooling towers at the 7 Riverside Drive site could lead to thermal pollution, affecting fish populations in the Trent River. To mitigate this, the facility would need to implement closed-loop cooling systems or use alternative water sources, as required under the CWA’s NPDES permitting ^12. Similarly, the 920 Trenton Frankford Road site, located near a former paper mill, may face challenges related to soil contamination and hazardous waste management, requiring compliance with RCRA and CEPA regulations.  


#### Community and Health Implications


The health risks associated with data centers in Quinte West would depend on their energy sources and emission control technologies. If the facilities rely on diesel generators, they could contribute to local air pollution, increasing the risk of respiratory illnesses in Hastings County. The EPA’s COBRA model suggests that even moderate increases in NOₓ and PM emissions could lead to significant public health costs, particularly in rural areas with limited healthcare access ^10.  

To address these concerns, the City of Quinte West would need to enforce strict permitting requirements, including the use of low-emission generators and real-time air quality monitoring. Collaboration with the *Bay of Quinte Conservation Authority* would also be essential to ensure that data center operations do not compromise the Trent River’s ecological integrity.  

---


### Comparative Analysis of Regulatory Approaches


| **Jurisdiction** | **Key Legislation** | **Emission Standards** | **Health Impact Assessments** | **Enforcement Mechanisms** |  
|--------------------|---------------------|------------------------|-------------------------------|---------------------------|  
| **United States**  | Clean Air Act (CAA) | NSPS, NESHAP, NSR     | COBRA model, HIAs             | EPA, state agencies       |  
| **Canada**         | CEPA, EPA, FSDS     | BAT, OAQS             | Provincial HIAs               | Ontario Ministry of Environment |  
| **European Union** | Industrial Emissions Directive (IED) | BAT, IPPC | EU-wide HIAs                  | EU member states          |  
| **China**          | APPC Law, NESAP     | Coal-based emission limits | National HIAs                 | Ministry of Ecology and Environment |  

---


### Conclusion


The regulation of data center health concerns is a complex interplay of federal, provincial, and international policies, with the U.S. Clean Air Act and EPA guidelines serving as a global benchmark. While Canada’s regulatory framework shares similarities with the U.S., it emphasizes provincial oversight and environmental protection for sensitive ecosystems like the Trent River watershed. For proposed sites in Quinte West and Hastings County, compliance with air quality, water, and health standards will be critical to ensuring sustainable development. As data center demand grows, governments must continue refining regulations to balance technological innovation with public health and environmental protection.  

---

# Section 6: Municipal Tax Revenue & Assessment Disparities (Shell vs. Contents)


## 6.1 How Quinte West Currently Approaches Municipal Taxation of Data Centers



#### Introduction

The rapid proliferation of data centers in Ontario has prompted municipalities to reevaluate their taxation frameworks, balancing economic incentives with environmental and social responsibilities. Quinte West, a municipality within Hastings County, faces unique challenges and opportunities as it navigates the integration of data centers into its economic and ecological landscape. This paper examines Quinte West’s current approach to municipal taxation of data centers, contextualizing it within broader provincial and municipal policies, environmental considerations, and the specific implications for the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed and proposed development sites such as 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) and 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill). Drawing on authoritative evidence from the Municipal Property Assessment Corporation (MPAC), legal analyses, and environmental impact studies, this paper provides a detailed technical and policy-oriented breakdown of Quinte West’s taxation strategies.

---


#### Overview of Municipal Taxation of Industrial Properties in Ontario

Municipal taxation of industrial properties in Ontario is governed by the **Assessment Act** and administered by MPAC, which categorizes properties based on their use and assigns tax rates accordingly. General-purpose industrial properties, including data centers, are classified under MPAC codes such as **520 General-purpose Industrial** and **530 Warehousing** (Source 12, Page 5). These classifications determine tax rates, which are set by individual municipalities based on local infrastructure needs, land use policies, and economic development goals.  

Key characteristics of industrial property taxation in Ontario include:  
- **Tax Rate Determination**: Municipalities set industrial tax rates independently, often reflecting the cost of infrastructure, utilities, and environmental mitigation (Source 2, Page 1).  
- **Assessment Freeze**: Since 2016, Ontario has maintained a freeze on reassessments for most industrial properties, meaning tax rates are based on 2016 valuations (Source 3, Page 1). This freeze has created uncertainty for taxpayers, as municipalities can still increase tax rates to fund infrastructure despite frozen assessments (Source 4, Page 2).  
- **Special Considerations for Data Centers**: While data centers are not explicitly mentioned in MPAC guidelines, their classification as **520 General-purpose Industrial** would subject them to the same tax frameworks as manufacturing or warehousing facilities.  

Quinte West, like other municipalities, must navigate these frameworks while addressing the unique demands of data centers, which require significant energy, water, and infrastructure investments.

---


#### Quinte West’s Context: Economic Development and Environmental Stewardship

Quinte West is situated within the **Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed**, a region characterized by its ecological sensitivity and recreational value. The municipality’s economic development strategy emphasizes **sustainable growth**, balancing industrial expansion with environmental protection. Proposed data center sites, such as **7 Riverside Drive** (former Saputo Dairy) and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** (former Sonoco Paper Mill), are located near this watershed, raising concerns about energy consumption, water usage, and emissions.  


#### Environmental Considerations

The Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed is a critical habitat for aquatic species and a key recreational area for residents. Data centers, which are energy-intensive and often rely on cooling systems that consume large volumes of water, could exacerbate existing environmental pressures. For example, studies on U.S. data centers estimate that **AI workloads could increase electricity consumption by 6.7–12.0% of the national total by 2028**, with corresponding public health costs exceeding **$20 billion** (Source 8, Page 3401–3500). While these figures are U.S.-specific, they highlight the potential for similar impacts in Quinte West if data centers are not managed sustainably.  

Quinte West’s taxation policies may need to incorporate **environmental externalities**, such as carbon emissions or water usage, to incentivize green technologies. However, current MPAC guidelines do not explicitly account for these factors, leaving municipalities to address them through local bylaws or partnerships with provincial agencies.  


#### Economic Development Priorities

Quinte West’s economic development strategy emphasizes **job creation** and **infrastructure investment**. Data centers could provide employment opportunities during construction and operation, as noted in studies on U.S. data centers, which require **thousands of workers** for construction and ongoing management (Source 9, Page 1901–2000). However, the economic benefits must be weighed against potential environmental costs.  

The proposed sites at **7 Riverside Drive** and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** are strategically located near existing infrastructure, potentially reducing the need for new utility lines. However, their proximity to the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed necessitates rigorous environmental impact assessments to ensure compliance with provincial and federal regulations.  

---


#### Current Taxation Frameworks for Data Centers in Quinte West

While there is no direct evidence of Quinte West’s specific taxation policies for data centers, its approach can be inferred from broader municipal and provincial practices. Key elements include:  


#### Industrial Tax Rates and MPAC Classification

As an industrial property, a data center in Quinte West would likely be classified under **MPAC Code 520 General-purpose Industrial** (Source 12, Page 5). This classification would subject it to the same tax rates as other industrial properties, which are determined by the municipality. However, the **2016 assessment freeze** (Source 3, Page 1) means that tax rates would not automatically increase with property values, creating a potential disconnect between infrastructure costs and tax revenue.  


#### Municipal Discretion in Taxation

Municipalities have significant discretion in setting industrial tax rates. For example, **Ontario municipalities** have expressed concerns about being excluded from data center decision-making, as highlighted in a 2023 submission by the **Association of Municipalities of Ontario (AMO)** (Source 5, Page 1). AMO emphasized the need for provincial policies to include **municipal input on data center regulations**, including taxation, to address local concerns. Quinte West may follow similar advocacy, ensuring that its taxation policies reflect local priorities such as environmental protection and equitable economic development.  


#### Potential for Special Tax Incentives

Some municipalities offer **tax incentives** to attract data centers, particularly if they commit to renewable energy or green technologies. For example, the **Ford government’s “Data Centre Playbook”** proposes requiring data centers to pay a **dedicated electricity rate** (Source 5, Page 1). Quinte West could leverage such policies to encourage data centers to adopt sustainable practices, potentially reducing their environmental impact while aligning with municipal goals.  

---


#### Comparative Analysis: Quinte West vs. Other Ontario Municipalities

To contextualize Quinte West’s approach, a comparative analysis of taxation frameworks across Ontario municipalities is necessary. Key differences include:  

| **Factor**                     | **Quinte West** (Inferred) | **Toronto** (Example) | **Sudbury** (Example) |  
|-------------------------------|----------------------------|------------------------|-----------------------|  
| **Industrial Tax Rate**         | Determined by local council | Higher due to density  | Lower due to rural economy |  
| **Assessment Freeze**           | Yes (2016–2026)            | Yes                    | Yes                   |  
| **Environmental Incentives**  | Potential for green tax breaks | Limited | Strong (e.g., renewable energy credits) |  
| **Municipal Input on Data Centers** | Advocacy for inclusion in provincial policies | Active participation | Limited |  

**Note**: This table highlights general trends, as specific data on Quinte West’s policies is not publicly available.  

---


#### Challenges and Opportunities for Quinte West

Quinte West faces several challenges in taxing data centers, including:  
- **Balancing Economic Growth and Environmental Protection**: Data centers could boost the local economy but risk harming the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed.  
- **Uncertainty from Assessment Freeze**: The 2016–2026 freeze may limit Quinte West’s ability to adjust tax rates in response to changing infrastructure needs.  
- **Need for Localized Policies**: Provincial frameworks may not address Quinte West’s unique environmental and economic priorities.  

Opportunities include:  
- **Leveraging Green Tax Incentives**: Encouraging data centers to adopt renewable energy or water-efficient technologies could reduce environmental impacts.  
- **Collaboration with Provincial Agencies**: Working with the **Ontario Ministry of Energy** or **EPA** to develop localized regulations for data centers.  
- **Community Engagement**: Ensuring public input on data center proposals, particularly for sites near the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed.  

---


#### Case Study: Proposed Sites in Quinte West


#### 7 Riverside Drive (Former Saputo Dairy)

This site is located near the Trent River, raising concerns about water usage and emissions. A data center here would require significant energy and cooling infrastructure, potentially impacting local ecosystems. Quinte West’s taxation policies could incentivize the use of **on-site renewable energy** or **closed-loop cooling systems** to mitigate environmental harm.  


#### 920 Trenton Frankford Road (Former Sonoco Paper Mill)

This site’s industrial history may align with data center operations, but its proximity to the Bay of Quinte necessitates rigorous environmental assessments. Taxation policies could be structured to reward **carbon-neutral operations** or **water recycling initiatives**.  

---


#### Conclusion and Recommendations

Quinte West’s approach to taxing data centers is shaped by provincial frameworks, environmental priorities, and economic development goals. While the current system relies on MPAC classifications and industrial tax rates, the municipality must address gaps in environmental accountability and municipal input. Recommendations include:  
- **Advocating for Provincial Inclusion**: Ensuring Quinte West’s voice is heard in provincial data center policies, particularly regarding taxation and environmental standards.  
- **Implementing Green Tax Incentives**: Offering reduced tax rates for data centers that adopt sustainable practices, such as renewable energy or water conservation.  
- **Conducting Environmental Impact Assessments**: For proposed sites like 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road, ensuring rigorous assessments to protect the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed.  

By aligning taxation policies with environmental and economic priorities, Quinte West can position itself as a leader in sustainable data center development.

## 6.2 How Governments in Canada and Worldwide Approach Taxation of Data Centers



### Introduction


The rapid proliferation of data centers, driven by the exponential growth of artificial technical (AI), cloud computing, and digital infrastructure, has prompted governments globally to reevaluate their taxation frameworks. Data centers, which consume vast amounts of energy and water while generating significant tax revenues, have become focal points of economic and environmental policy debates. In Canada and internationally, governments face a dual challenge: maximizing tax revenue from data centers while mitigating their environmental and social impacts. This paper examines the taxation strategies employed by Canadian and global governments, analyzes the economic and environmental implications of these policies, and evaluates their relevance to specific regions such as the City of Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed.  

---


### Global Taxation Frameworks for Data Centers



#### United States: Tax Incentives and Revenue Trade-offs


The United States has long been a leader in attracting data center investment through aggressive tax incentives. States such as Virginia, Texas, and North Carolina have implemented targeted tax exemptions, abatements, and infrastructure subsidies to lure hyperscale data centers. For example, Virginia offers sales tax exemptions on data center equipment, which, despite generating $1.3 billion in personal property tax revenue in 2024, has raised concerns about the long-term fiscal sustainability of these policies ^[2]. Similarly, Texas provides sales and use tax exemptions for data centers investing over $200 million, contingent on job creation and energy efficiency benchmarks ^[6].  

However, these incentives often come at a cost. A 2023 audit of Virginia’s data center sector revealed that the state generated only 48 cents in new state revenue for every dollar forgone in sales tax exemptions between 2014 and 2023 ^[3]. This highlights a critical tension: while data centers contribute to local tax revenues, the upfront tax breaks may not offset the long-term infrastructure and environmental costs. For instance, in DeKalb County, Illinois, a Meta data center operating since 2023 allowed the city to reduce its local tax rate from 11.5% to 7.9% while adding $19 million annually to school funding ^[2]. Yet, such benefits are not universal, as communities without comparable incentives may struggle to compete.  


#### Europe: Balancing Taxation and Environmental Standards


European Union (EU) member states have adopted a more nuanced approach, integrating environmental regulations with taxation policies. The EU’s Digital Services Tax (DST), introduced in 2020, imposes a 3% tax on the revenues of large digital companies, including cloud service providers, to address perceived inequities in cross-border taxation ^[11]. Additionally, countries like Germany and France have implemented strict energy efficiency standards for data centers, requiring facilities to meet specific Power Usage Effectiveness (PUE) benchmarks to qualify for tax incentives.  

In the Netherlands, data centers benefit from a 21% corporate tax rate, but they are exempt from value-added tax (VAT) on IT equipment if they meet sustainability criteria, such as using renewable energy or achieving carbon neutrality ^[12]. This approach aligns with the EU’s broader climate goals, ensuring that tax incentives are tied to environmental performance.  


#### Asia-Pacific: Diverse Regulatory Landscapes


In Asia, taxation policies for data centers vary widely. China, for instance, imposes a 25% corporate income tax on data center operators but offers significant subsidies for green data centers that use renewable energy or achieve high energy efficiency ratings ^[11]. Meanwhile, Japan has introduced a "Green Data Center" certification program, which grants tax breaks to facilities that reduce their carbon footprint by at least 30% compared to industry averages ^[12].  

In contrast, countries like Singapore have adopted a more market-driven approach, offering tax exemptions on data center equipment and infrastructure investments while maintaining a 17% corporate tax rate. This strategy has attracted global hyperscalers such as Google and Microsoft, which have established major data center hubs in the region ^[11].  

---


### Canadian Taxation Policies and Environmental Considerations



#### Federal and Provincial Tax Frameworks


Canada’s approach to data center taxation is shaped by federal and provincial policies that emphasize environmental sustainability and economic development. At the federal level, data centers are subject to the Goods and Services Tax (GST) and the Harmonized Sales Tax (HST), with rates varying by province. However, provinces have introduced targeted incentives to attract investment.  

Alberta, for example, has implemented a 2% levy on the value of information technology (IT) equipment within data centers, a policy designed to offset the lack of a provincial sales tax ^[3]. This levy generates revenue while encouraging data centers to invest in energy-efficient infrastructure. In contrast, British Columbia offers tax credits for data centers that achieve high energy efficiency standards, aligning with the province’s climate goals ^[12].  


#### Municipal-Level Tax Incentives and Challenges


Municipal governments in Canada have increasingly used tax abatements and infrastructure subsidies to attract data centers, mirroring trends in the United States. For instance, the City of Toronto has offered tax exemptions on data center equipment in exchange for commitments to local job creation and green energy use ^[10]. However, these incentives often come with trade-offs. A 2023 study by the Canadian Centre for Policy Alternatives (CCPA) found that municipalities sacrificing tax revenue to attract data centers may face long-term fiscal strain, particularly if the facilities do not meet their economic or environmental commitments ^[3].  


#### Environmental and Social Impacts


The environmental footprint of data centers—particularly their energy and water consumption—has prompted calls for stricter regulations. In Canada, provinces like British Columbia and Quebec have mandated that data centers use 100% renewable energy by 2030, a policy that indirectly influences taxation by tying incentives to compliance with sustainability targets ^[6].  

In the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed, where the proposed data centers at 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) and 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill) are located, environmental concerns are particularly acute. The region’s water resources, already under pressure from agricultural and industrial activities, could face additional strain from data center operations, which require significant water for cooling systems ^[8]. This raises questions about whether current taxation policies adequately account for the environmental costs of data centers, particularly in ecologically sensitive areas like Hastings County.  

---


### Comparative Analysis of Taxation Policies


| **Region**       | **Tax Policy**                                                                 | **Key Incentives**                                                                 | **Environmental Considerations**                                                                 | **Source** |
|------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------|
| **United States** | Sales tax exemptions, corporate income tax exemptions                              | Job creation, capital investment, energy efficiency benchmarks                       | No federal mandates; state-level policies vary                                               | ^[2][11] |
| **European Union** | Digital Services Tax (3%), VAT exemptions for green data centers                 | Sustainability certifications, carbon neutrality targets                             | Strict energy efficiency and emissions standards                                                 | ^[11][12] |
| **China**        | 25% corporate income tax, subsidies for green data centers                     | Renewable energy use, carbon reduction targets                                       | National push for carbon neutrality                                                              | ^[11]    |
| **Canada**       | GST/HST, provincial levies on IT equipment, tax credits for green data centers | Energy efficiency, job creation, renewable energy use                                  | Provincial mandates for 100% renewable energy by 2030 in British Columbia and Quebec          | ^[3][6]  |
| **Singapore**    | 17% corporate tax, tax exemptions on IT equipment                            | Infrastructure investment, green energy use                                           | No federal mandates; market-driven incentives                                                    | ^[11]    |

---


### Case Studies: Lessons from Global and Canadian Experiences



#### Virginia, USA: The Double-Edged Sword of Tax Incentives


Virginia’s success in attracting data centers—home to over 150 facilities—has been driven by aggressive tax incentives, including sales tax exemptions on IT equipment and property tax abatements. However, the state’s 2023 audit revealed that these incentives generated only 48 cents in new revenue for every dollar forgone in sales tax ^[3]. This underscores the risk of over-reliance on tax breaks, which may not yield long-term fiscal benefits. For regions like Quinte West, which are considering similar incentives, this case study highlights the need for rigorous cost-benefit analyses.  


#### Alberta, Canada: Balancing Revenue and Sustainability


Alberta’s 2% levy on IT equipment within data centers provides a model for balancing revenue generation with environmental accountability. By taxing the most expensive component of data centers—IT equipment—the province ensures that operators contribute to public coffers while incentivizing energy efficiency. This approach could be adapted in Hastings County, where the proposed data centers would need to align with regional sustainability goals to avoid environmental degradation in the Trent River watershed ^[3].  


#### North Carolina, USA: Secrecy and Fiscal Risks


North Carolina’s data center tax incentives, which shield financial details from state oversight, have raised concerns about transparency and fiscal accountability ^[7]. The state’s tax breaks, which cost up to $57 million annually, could serve as a cautionary tale for Canadian municipalities. In Quinte West, where community engagement and environmental stewardship are priorities, ensuring transparency in data center agreements would be critical to avoiding similar fiscal risks.  

---


### Challenges and Controversies in Data Center Taxation



#### Fiscal Sustainability and Long-Term Costs


The primary challenge in data center taxation is ensuring fiscal sustainability. While tax incentives can attract investment, they often fail to generate sufficient revenue to offset infrastructure costs. For example, in the United States, local governments frequently bear the burden of upgrading water and electrical systems to support data centers, with no guarantee of long-term tax revenue ^[10]. In Hastings County, where the Trent River’s ecological health is a priority, this risk is compounded by the potential for increased water usage and pollution from data center operations.  


#### Environmental Externalities


Data centers’ environmental impact—particularly their energy and water consumption—poses significant challenges for taxation policies. In regions like the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed, where water resources are already strained, the lack of binding environmental regulations in current tax incentives could exacerbate ecological risks. For instance, the proposed data center at 920 Trenton Frankford Road would require extensive water use for cooling, potentially conflicting with the region’s conservation goals ^[8].  


#### Equity and Community Benefits


Tax incentives for data centers often prioritize corporate interests over community benefits. In the United States, critics argue that tax breaks disproportionately benefit large hyperscale operators, leaving smaller communities with limited economic gains ^[3]. In Quinte West, where local economic development is a key concern, ensuring that data center agreements include concrete community benefits—such as job training programs or infrastructure investments—would be essential to avoid replicating these inequities.  

---


### Recommendations for Canada and the Trent River / Bay of Quinte Watershed


1. **Adopt Tiered Tax Incentives with Environmental Conditions**  
   - Follow Alberta’s model by implementing levies on IT equipment while offering tax credits for data centers that meet energy efficiency and renewable energy targets. This would ensure revenue generation while promoting sustainability in the Trent River watershed.  

2. **Mandate Transparency in Data Center Agreements**  
   - Require full disclosure of resource demands, tax incentives, and environmental commitments in data center agreements, as advocated by the Lincoln Institute of Land Policy ^[8]. This would align with Hastings County’s emphasis on community engagement and environmental accountability.  

3. **Invest in Green Infrastructure**  
   - Prioritize investments in renewable energy and water conservation infrastructure to support data centers while mitigating their environmental impact. This approach would align with British Columbia’s and Quebec’s climate goals and could be adapted for Quinte West.  

4. **Conduct Comprehensive Cost-Benefit Analyses**  
   - Before approving tax incentives, municipalities should conduct rigorous analyses of long-term fiscal and environmental impacts, as highlighted by Virginia’s audit ^[3]. This would help avoid the fiscal risks observed in North Carolina ^[7].  

---


### Conclusion


The taxation of data centers is a complex interplay of economic incentives, environmental considerations, and fiscal sustainability. While governments globally have adopted diverse strategies—from aggressive tax breaks in the United States to sustainability-linked incentives in Europe—these approaches often come with trade-offs. For regions like the City of Quinte West and Hastings County, where the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed’s ecological health is a priority, the lessons from global and Canadian experiences underscore the need for balanced policies that maximize revenue, ensure environmental accountability, and prioritize community benefits. By adopting tiered incentives, mandating transparency, and investing in green infrastructure, Canadian municipalities can navigate the challenges of data center taxation while safeguarding their natural and economic resources.

## 6.3 Property Tax Assessment Rules: Real Property (Shell) vs. Personal/Machinery Property (Servers)


### A Comparative Analysis with Implications for Municipal Infrastructure and Environmental Health


---


### Introduction


Property tax assessment rules play a critical role in shaping municipal infrastructure, economic development, and environmental sustainability. In regions like the City of Quinte West and Hastings County, where industrial and technological expansion is accelerating, the distinction between real property (e.g., land, buildings, and "shell" structures) and personal/machinery property (e.g., servers, data centers, and associated equipment) has profound implications for tax policy, public health, and infrastructure planning. This paper examines the divergent assessment frameworks for these property types, drawing on empirical studies, legal statutes, and environmental impact analyses. The analysis is contextualized within the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed, focusing on proposed development sites at **7 Riverside Drive** (former Saputo Dairy) and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** (former Sonoco Paper Mill), where data center and industrial infrastructure projects are under consideration.  

---


### Real Property Assessment Frameworks



#### Definition and Scope of Real Property


Real property encompasses land, buildings, and permanent fixtures attached to the land. In municipal contexts, "shell" properties—such as vacant lots or unfinished structures—are often assessed based on their potential for development, zoning classifications, and market value. For example, in Quinte West, the **City of Quinte West Official Plan** (2022) emphasizes the need to balance industrial growth with environmental protection, particularly in the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed, where water quality and ecological integrity are prioritized ^[12].  


#### Assessment Methods and Taxation


Real property is typically assessed using **market value** principles, with adjustments for location, zoning, and infrastructure connectivity. In Ontario, the **Municipal Property Assessment Corporation (MPAC)** conducts annual assessments, which are used to determine property tax rates. For shell properties, assessments may be lower due to underutilization, but they can increase rapidly if development plans are approved ^[2].  

Key factors influencing real property assessments include:  
- **Zoning**: Industrial zones (e.g., for data centers) may have different valuation metrics than residential or agricultural zones.  
- **Infrastructure**: Proximity to power grids, transportation networks, and water systems (e.g., the Trent River) can elevate property values ^[3].  
- **Environmental Regulations**: Areas near sensitive ecosystems (e.g., the Bay of Quinte) may face stricter development controls, affecting assessed value ^[12].  


#### Case Study: 7 Riverside Drive (Former Saputo Dairy)


The proposed site at **7 Riverside Drive** is a former dairy facility with potential for industrial or data center use. Its assessment would depend on:  
- **Zoning**: Current zoning permits industrial use, but environmental reviews are required due to its location near the Trent River ^[12].  
- **Market Value**: The site’s value could be influenced by its proximity to Quinte West’s existing infrastructure, including the **Quinte West Power Utility** (QWPU) and the **Bay of Quinte Environmental Management Plan** ^[1].  
- **Health Impacts**: If repurposed for data centers, the site’s assessment might be affected by public health concerns related to emissions, as highlighted in studies on data center health costs ^[4].  

---


### Personal/Machinery Property Assessment Frameworks



#### Definition and Scope of Personal/Machinery Property


Personal property includes movable assets such as machinery, equipment, and servers. In the context of data centers, this category encompasses servers, cooling systems, backup generators, and associated infrastructure. Unlike real property, personal property is often assessed based on **depreciation schedules**, **replacement costs**, and **specific-use exemptions**.  


#### Assessment Methods and Taxation


Personal property is typically assessed using **cost-based** or **income-based** methodologies. However, many jurisdictions offer **tax exemptions** for industrial equipment, particularly for data centers, which are critical to modern economic and technological infrastructure. For example:  
- **Ontario’s Tax Exemption Framework**: Companies can apply for exemptions on servers, cooling systems, and backup generators if they meet criteria such as job creation, wage thresholds, and capital investment ^[9].  
- **Duration of Exemptions**: Exemptions may last up to 20 years, contingent on compliance with state-specific requirements ^[9].  

Key factors influencing personal property assessments include:  
- **Depreciation**: Servers and machinery are subject to rapid depreciation, which can lower their assessed value over time.  
- **Energy Efficiency**: Data centers with energy-efficient designs (e.g., those using renewable energy) may qualify for additional exemptions or tax credits ^[7].  
- **Environmental Impact**: The public health costs of data centers, as estimated by the **EPA’s COBRA model**, may influence local tax policies to mitigate negative externalities ^[4].  


#### Case Study: 920 Trenton Frankford Road (Former Sonoco Paper Mill)


The proposed site at **920 Trenton Frankford Road** is a former paper mill with potential for data center development. Its assessment would involve:  
- **Exemption Applications**: The site could qualify for tax exemptions on servers and cooling systems if the developer meets job creation and investment thresholds ^[9].  
- **Environmental Compliance**: The site’s proximity to the Trent River necessitates compliance with the **Bay of Quinte Environmental Management Plan**, which may affect the approval of backup generators and emissions controls ^[12].  
- **Health Costs**: If the data center emits pollutants (e.g., NOx, PM2.5), the associated public health costs (estimated at $20 billion annually for U.S. data centers by 2028) could influence local tax policies to incentivize cleaner technologies ^[4].  

---


### Comparative Analysis of Real vs. Personal/Machinery Property Assessments


| **Factor**                     | **Real Property (Shell)**                                                                 | **Personal/Machinery Property (Servers)**                                                                 |  
|-------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|  
| **Assessment Basis**          | Market value, zoning, infrastructure connectivity                                           | Depreciation schedules, replacement costs, exemptions                                                      |  
| **Tax Exemptions**          | Limited; depends on land use and environmental regulations                                | Extensive; contingent on job creation, investment, and compliance with state laws                          |  
| **Environmental Impact**    | Assessed via zoning and ecological reviews (e.g., Trent River / Bay of Quinte)          | Assessed via public health costs (e.g., $390 million estimated health impact in 2023) ^[10]             |  
| **Infrastructure Dependency** | High; depends on proximity to utilities, transportation, and water systems                  | Moderate; depends on energy efficiency, emissions controls, and compliance with EPA/AVERT guidelines ^[8] |  
| **Case Study Relevance**    | 7 Riverside Drive (zoning, environmental reviews)                                         | 920 Trenton Frankford Road (exemptions, health costs)                                                  |  

---


### Implications for Municipal Infrastructure and Environmental Health



#### Economic and Environmental Trade-Offs


The divergent assessment rules for real and personal property create a complex landscape for municipal planners. For example:  
- **Real Property**: High-value shell sites (e.g., 7 Riverside Drive) may attract investment but require strict environmental oversight to protect the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed ^[12].  
- **Personal Property**: Data centers (e.g., 920 Trenton Frankford Road) may qualify for tax exemptions but risk contributing to public health costs unless equipped with clean energy and emissions controls ^[4].  


#### Policy Recommendations


To balance economic growth with environmental sustainability, municipalities like Quinte West and Hastings County should:  
1. **Adopt Health-Informed Tax Policies**: Incorporate public health costs (e.g., $20 billion projected for U.S. data centers by 2028) into tax exemption criteria ^[4].  
2. **Enhance Environmental Reviews**: For real property developments near sensitive ecosystems (e.g., the Trent River), enforce stricter emissions and water quality standards ^[12].  
3. **Promote Energy-Efficient Infrastructure**: Incentivize data centers to adopt energy-efficient technologies (e.g., those modeled in **Sensitivity-Based Optimization Studies**) to reduce long-term health and environmental impacts ^[7].  

---


### Case Study: Quinte West and Hastings County



#### Contextual Challenges


Quinte West and Hastings County face unique challenges in balancing industrial growth with environmental protection. The **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed** is a critical ecological and recreational resource, yet the region is also a hub for industrial and technological expansion. Proposed developments at **7 Riverside Drive** and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** exemplify this tension.  


#### Analysis of Proposed Sites


- **7 Riverside Drive**:  
  - **Pros**: Proximity to existing infrastructure (e.g., QWPU) and potential for high-value real property development.  
  - **Cons**: Environmental risks to the Trent River require rigorous mitigation strategies, including compliance with the **Bay of Quinte Environmental Management Plan** ^[12].  

- **920 Trenton Frankford Road**:  
  - **Pros**: Potential for tax-exempt personal property (servers) if job creation and investment thresholds are met ^[9].  
  - **Cons**: Risk of public health impacts from emissions (e.g., NOx, PM2.5) unless equipped with advanced emissions controls ^[10].  


#### Policy Implications


Municipalities must adopt a **holistic approach** to property tax assessments, integrating:  
- **Environmental Impact Assessments**: For real property, ensuring compliance with water quality standards in the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed ^[12].  
- **Health-Informed Computing Frameworks**: For personal property, adopting models like **HICO** (Health-Informed Computing) to minimize public health costs ^[6].  

---


### Conclusion


The distinction between real property (shell) and personal/machinery property (servers) in property tax assessments has far-reaching implications for municipal infrastructure, environmental health, and economic development. In regions like Quinte West and Hastings County, where industrial and technological expansion is accelerating, the interplay between these assessment frameworks must be carefully managed. By integrating health-informed computing, energy-efficient infrastructure, and rigorous environmental reviews, municipalities can foster sustainable growth while protecting critical ecosystems like the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed. The proposed sites at **7 Riverside Drive** and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** serve as test cases for this balance, requiring innovative policy solutions to align economic incentives with public health and environmental goals.  

---
