## 8.2 Types of Contamination a Data Center Can Cause to a Surrounding Area



### Introduction

Data centers, as critical infrastructure for digital economies, consume vast amounts of energy and generate significant heat, necessitating complex cooling systems. While their environmental impact is often discussed in terms of carbon emissions and energy consumption, the potential for localized contamination—chemical, thermal, and electromagnetic—remains underexplored. This paper examines the types of contamination a data center can cause to surrounding areas, with a focus on the City of Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed. Specific attention is given to the proposed sites at 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) and 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill), where data center development could intersect with sensitive ecological and municipal systems.  

---


### Chemical Contamination: Glycol Leaks and Spill Risks



#### Propylene Glycol and Ethylene Glycol in Cooling Systems

Data centers rely on glycols (propylene glycol and ethylene glycol) for cooling, as these compounds are effective antifreeze agents and thermal transfer fluids. However, their leakage into the environment poses significant risks. Propylene glycol, while less toxic than ethylene glycol, can still contaminate water systems and soil, particularly in closed-loop cooling systems where glycol concentrations are high ^[2]^[3]^[4].  

- **Toxicity and Environmental Impact**:  
  - Propylene glycol is classified as GRAS (Generally Recognized as Safe) by the FDA for food and pharmaceutical use but exhibits low toxicity in aquatic systems. However, high concentrations can disrupt aquatic life, particularly in freshwater ecosystems like the Trent River ^[3]^[5].  
  - Ethylene glycol, though more toxic, is less commonly used in modern data centers due to its higher environmental risk. Its presence in water systems can lead to bioaccumulation and long-term ecological damage ^[4]^[5].  

- **Spill Risks and Mitigation**:  
  - Data centers using glycols must implement secondary containment systems to prevent spills, similar to the measures taken during the Rivers Inlet Diesel Spill in British Columbia ^[6].  
  - In Quinte West, where the Trent River flows into the Bay of Quinte, a spill of glycol into the river could affect fish populations and water quality, with potential implications for local fisheries and recreational use ^[5]^[7].  


#### Case Study: Glycol Contamination in Industrial Systems

Industrial systems, such as those in the former Saputo Dairy site (now proposed for data center use), may already have infrastructure that could facilitate glycol leaks. For example, glycol contamination in chillers and HVAC loops can lead to corrosion, fouling, and mechanical degradation, increasing the risk of unintended discharges ^[5].  

- **Regulatory Context**:  
  - Ontario’s Spills Action Centre mandates immediate reporting of any chemical spills, including glycol leaks, to local municipalities and environmental agencies ^[8].  
  - The proposed data center at 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill) would need to comply with these regulations, given its proximity to the Trent River watershed ^[8].  

---


### Thermal Contamination: Heat Dissipation and Water Usage



#### Thermal Pollution from Cooling Systems

Data centers generate substantial heat, often requiring cooling systems that discharge excess heat into the environment. Common methods include:  
- **Air Cooling**: Discharging warm air into the atmosphere, which can contribute to local temperature increases.  
- **Water Cooling**: Using nearby water sources (e.g., rivers, lakes) for heat dissipation, which can raise water temperatures and disrupt aquatic ecosystems ^[9].  

- **Impact on the Trent River**:  
  - The Trent River, a key waterway in Hastings County, is part of the Bay of Quinte watershed. If a data center uses the river for cooling, elevated water temperatures could harm fish species such as lake sturgeon and walleye, which are sensitive to thermal changes ^[10].  
  - Studies on thermal pollution in similar contexts (e.g., data centers in the Great Lakes region) show that even small temperature increases (1–2°C) can reduce dissolved oxygen levels, affecting aquatic life ^[11].  


#### Water Consumption and Depletion

Data centers require large volumes of water for cooling, which can strain local water resources. For example:  
- **Cooling Tower Usage**: Evaporative cooling towers can consume millions of liters of water annually, potentially depleting groundwater or surface water sources ^[12].  
- **Impact on Quinte West**:  
  - The City of Quinte West relies on the Trent River for municipal water supply. A data center at 7 Riverside Drive could increase water demand, exacerbating pressure on existing infrastructure and potentially reducing water availability for residential and agricultural use ^[13].  

---


### Electromagnetic and Radio Frequency (RF) Contamination



#### Electromagnetic Fields (EMFs) from Server Hardware

Data centers house thousands of servers, which emit low-frequency electromagnetic fields (EMFs). While the health risks of EMFs are debated, concerns include:  
- **Potential Health Effects**: Some studies suggest prolonged exposure to EMFs may be linked to neurological effects, though evidence remains inconclusive ^[14].  
- **Impact on Surrounding Areas**:  
  - The proposed data center at 920 Trenton Frankford Road would be located near residential areas, raising questions about EMF exposure for nearby communities ^[15].  


#### Radio Frequency (RF) Pollution from 5G and IoT Infrastructure

Modern data centers often support 5G networks and IoT devices, which emit RF radiation. Key concerns include:  
- **Signal Interference**: RF emissions could interfere with local radio communications, emergency services, or scientific research (e.g., radio astronomy) ^[16].  
- **Health and Safety**: While RF exposure from 5G is generally considered safe by regulatory bodies, some communities express concerns about long-term exposure ^[17].  

- **Quinte West Context**:  
  - The City of Quinte West has no current 5G infrastructure, but a data center could accelerate deployment, necessitating compliance with RF exposure limits set by Health Canada and the International Commission on Non-Ionizing Radiation Protection (ICNIRP) ^[18].  

---


### Air Quality and Particulate Contamination



#### Emissions from Backup Power Systems

Data centers often use diesel generators for backup power during outages. These generators emit particulate matter (PM), nitrogen oxides (NOx), and sulfur dioxide (SO2), which can degrade air quality.  

- **Impact on Quinte West**:  
  - The Trent River watershed is a recreational area, and PM emissions could affect air quality for residents and visitors. For example, a 2022 power outage in Quinte West highlighted the reliance on backup generators, raising concerns about long-term emissions ^[4].  


#### Dust and Particulate Matter from Construction

During the construction of a data center, dust and particulate matter from excavation and material transport can contaminate the air. This is particularly relevant for the former Saputo Dairy site, where soil stabilization may be necessary to prevent dust emissions ^[19].  

---


### Noise Pollution and Vibration Contamination



#### Noise from Cooling Systems and Servers

Data centers generate noise from:  
- **Cooling Towers and Fans**: Continuous operation of cooling systems can produce noise levels exceeding 60 decibels (dB), potentially disturbing nearby residents ^[20].  
- **Server Racks and HVAC Units**: High-density server rooms may require additional noise mitigation measures ^[21].  

- **Quinte West Context**:  
  - The proposed site at 7 Riverside Drive is near residential areas, necessitating noise barriers or sound-dampening technologies to comply with local noise ordinances ^[22].  


#### Vibration from Heavy Machinery

Vibrations from cooling pumps, generators, and HVAC units can transmit through the ground, potentially affecting nearby buildings or infrastructure. This is a concern for the former Sonoco Paper Mill site, where existing structures may be sensitive to vibration ^[23].  

---


### Comparative Analysis of Contamination Types


| **Contamination Type**       | **Source**                          | **Environmental Impact**                                                                 | **Mitigation Strategies**                                                                 | **Relevance to Quinte West**                                                                 |
|-----------------------------|------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Chemical (Glycols)**      | Cooling systems                    | Toxicity to aquatic life; soil and water contamination                                   | Secondary containment; regular inspections; spill response plans                            | High risk for Trent River; compliance with Ontario spill regulations ^[5]^[8]              |
| **Thermal Pollution**        | Water cooling systems              | Elevated water temperatures; reduced dissolved oxygen                                    | Use of closed-loop systems; heat recovery technologies                                      | Threat to Trent River fish populations; need for water usage permits ^[10]^[13]         |
| **Electromagnetic (EMF)**  | Server hardware                    | Potential health concerns; interference with communication systems                    | Shielding; compliance with ICNIRP guidelines                                              | Proximity to residential areas; need for EMF monitoring ^[15]^[18]                        |
| **Air Quality (PM/NOx)**    | Diesel generators                  | Respiratory issues; contribution to smog                                                 | Use of renewable energy; emission controls                                                  | Risk during power outages; alignment with Quinte West air quality standards ^[4]^[19]     |
| **Noise/Vibration**         | Cooling systems, machinery       | Disturbance to residents; structural damage                                               | Sound barriers; vibration dampeners                                                       | Proximity to residential areas; need for noise mitigation at 7 Riverside Drive ^[22]     |

---


### Case Study: Rivers Inlet Diesel Spill and Lessons for Data Centers


The 2021 Rivers Inlet Diesel Spill in British Columbia, where 7,000 L of diesel was released into a river, highlights the risks of chemical contamination from industrial infrastructure ^[6]. Key lessons for data centers include:  
- **Preventive Measures**: Secondary containment systems, regular maintenance, and employee training are critical to preventing spills ^[6].  
- **Response Protocols**: Immediate deployment of containment booms and collaboration with local authorities (e.g., First Nations representatives) can minimize ecological damage ^[6].  
- **Quinte West Application**: A data center in Quinte West must adopt similar protocols to prevent glycol or diesel spills into the Trent River, given the watershed’s ecological sensitivity ^[5]^[6].  

---


### Conclusion and Recommendations


Data centers pose multifaceted contamination risks, including chemical, thermal, electromagnetic, and air quality impacts. In Quinte West and the Trent River watershed, these risks are amplified by the proximity of proposed sites to sensitive ecosystems and residential areas. To mitigate contamination:  
1. **Adopt Closed-Loop Cooling Systems**: Reduce glycol leakage and thermal pollution by using closed-loop systems with minimal water usage ^[4]^[10].  
2. **Implement Spill Prevention Plans**: Follow Ontario’s spill reporting requirements and invest in secondary containment ^[8].  
3. **Conduct Environmental Impact Assessments (EIAs)**: Evaluate the specific risks of the proposed sites (7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road) to the Trent River and Hastings County ^[5]^[13].  
4. **Engage with Local Communities**: Address concerns about EMFs, noise, and air quality through transparent communication and compliance with health guidelines ^[15]^[18].  

By integrating these strategies, data centers can minimize their environmental footprint while supporting the digital infrastructure needs of communities like Quinte West.

## 8.3 How Data Centers Have Caused Contamination in Canada



### Introduction


Data centers, the backbone of the digital economy, have become a focal point of environmental and regulatory scrutiny in Canada. As artificial technical (AI) and cloud computing demand unprecedented energy and resource consumption, the sector’s expansion has raised concerns about contamination, including water overuse, air pollution, and land degradation. This paper examines the evidence linking data centers to contamination in Canada, with a specific focus on Alberta and Ontario, while explicitly connecting findings to the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed, Quinte West, Hastings County, and proposed sites at **7 Riverside Drive** (former Saputo Dairy) and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** (former Sonoco Paper Mill). Drawing on primary studies, regulatory frameworks, and environmental impact assessments, this analysis evaluates the contamination risks posed by data centers and their implications for municipal infrastructure and ecological health.  

---


### Environmental Impact of Data Centers in Canada



#### Energy Consumption and Carbon Emissions


Data centers are among the most energy-intensive facilities globally, with AI-driven operations escalating demand. In Ontario, for instance, modern data centers now require **hundreds of megawatts of power**, far exceeding the capacity of older facilities (Source [4]^). This surge in energy demand has significant environmental consequences, particularly in regions reliant on fossil fuels for electricity generation.  

Alberta, which has prioritized natural gas as a primary energy source for data centers (Source [7]^), faces unique challenges. The province’s **Artificial Technical Data Centres Strategy** (2024) explicitly leverages its abundant natural gas reserves to attract AI infrastructure, but this reliance on fossil fuels has been criticized for exacerbating carbon monoxide (CO) emissions. According to national data from Environment and Climate Change Canada (Source [9]^), Alberta accounted for **56% of its CO emissions reduction** between 1990 and 2024, yet its data centers remain a growing source of pollution. Gas-powered data centers, such as the proposed **Wonder Valley project** (Source [12]^), release harmful pollutants, including nitrogen oxides (NOx) and particulate matter, which can degrade air quality and pose health risks to nearby communities.  

In Ontario, the **Data Centre Playbook** (2026) mandates that new projects contribute to economic development and data sovereignty while paying the full cost of electricity usage (Source [5]^). However, the province’s reliance on a mix of renewable and non-renewable energy sources means that data centers could still contribute to contamination through emissions from coal or natural gas plants. For example, the **Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed**, which spans Hastings County and Quinte West, could face increased contamination risks if data centers in the region draw power from non-renewable sources, potentially affecting water quality through runoff or thermal pollution.  


#### Water Usage and Contamination Risks


Cooling systems in data centers consume vast quantities of water, raising concerns about contamination from chemical discharge and thermal pollution. In Ontario, data centers are estimated to require **millions of liters of water daily** for cooling, with the **Trent River** and **Bay of Quinte** potentially at risk if local facilities adopt similar practices (Source [10]^). For instance, the proposed sites at **7 Riverside Drive** and **920 Trenton Frankford Road**—both located near the Trent River—could strain local water resources and introduce contaminants such as **heavy metals** or **cooling chemicals** into the watershed.  

Alberta’s data centers, while benefiting from its cold climate (which reduces cooling needs), still face scrutiny over water usage. The **Wonder Valley project**, for example, has been criticized for its potential to become one of “Earth’s largest heat sources” (Source [6]^), which could alter local hydrology and affect aquatic ecosystems. In Quinte West, where the Trent River is a critical ecological and recreational resource, similar risks could arise if data centers adopt inefficient cooling technologies or fail to implement stringent water management practices.  


#### Land Use and Habitat Disruption


The expansion of data centers often requires large land areas, leading to habitat fragmentation and soil degradation. In Alberta, local opposition to projects like the **Wonder Valley AI data center** has highlighted concerns about land use (Source [6]^). While Alberta’s regulatory framework includes independent approvals by municipal councils and regulators (Source [3]^), similar scrutiny is needed in Quinte West and Hastings County to prevent habitat disruption near the Trent River. For example, the **920 Trenton Frankford Road site**, formerly a paper mill, may require extensive land clearing, potentially affecting local flora and fauna.  

---


### Regulatory Frameworks and Contamination Mitigation



#### Alberta’s Approach to Data Center Regulation


Alberta has positioned itself as a leader in attracting AI data centers through a **laissez-faire regulatory environment** and a **“data center concierge service”** (Source [7]^). However, this approach has been criticized for insufficient environmental oversight. For instance, the **Alberta Utilities Commission** closed a data center power plant application in 2026 due to incomplete filings (Source [3]^), indicating gaps in regulatory enforcement. While Alberta’s grid operator sets limits on data center load to prevent overburdening the system (Source [3]^), the lack of mandatory environmental assessments for data centers (Source [6]^) raises concerns about contamination risks.  


#### Ontario’s Data Centre Playbook and Environmental Safeguards


Ontario’s **Data Centre Playbook** (2026) represents a more structured approach, requiring projects to deliver economic benefits while paying the full cost of electricity usage (Source [5]^). The province’s **Protect Ontario by Securing Affordable Energy for Generations Act** (Bill 40) mandates that data centers contribute to grid stability and avoid overburdening local infrastructure (Source [4]^). However, the approval process can take **up to three years**, raising concerns about delays in addressing contamination risks (Source [4]^).  

In Quinte West and Hastings County, the **Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed** could benefit from Ontario’s emphasis on **grid access frameworks** and **economic development contributions**. However, the absence of specific regulations targeting contamination from data centers in these regions means that local municipalities may need to adopt **additional measures**, such as requiring environmental impact assessments for proposed sites like **7 Riverside Drive** and **920 Trenton Frankroad Road**.  

---


### Case Studies: Alberta vs. Ontario


| **Factor**                | **Alberta**                                                                 | **Ontario**                                                                 |  
|--------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------|  
| **Energy Source**        | Relies heavily on natural gas; promotes fossil fuel-based data centers (Source [7]^) | Mix of renewable and non-renewable sources; mandates full cost of electricity (Source [5]^) |  
| **Regulatory Oversight** | Independent approvals by councils and regulators; no mandatory environmental assessments (Source [6]^) | Structured approval process with emphasis on grid stability and economic benefits (Source [5]^) |  
| **Contamination Risks**  | High risk of air pollution from gas-powered data centers (Source [12]^)       | Potential water contamination from cooling systems; reliance on non-renewable energy (Source [10]^) |  
| **Community Opposition** | Local councils have rejected projects (e.g., Rocky View County, 2025) (Source [3]^) | Municipalities raise concerns about grid pressure and water use (Source [11]^) |  

---


### Contamination Risks in Quinte West and Hastings County


While the evidence provided does not explicitly mention **Quinte West** or **Hastings County**, the general risks associated with data centers in Alberta and Ontario can be extrapolated to these regions. The **Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed**, a vital ecological and recreational area, could face contamination from:  

1. **Thermal Pollution**: If data centers at **7 Riverside Drive** or **920 Trenton Frankford Road** use water for cooling, the discharge of heated water into the Trent River could harm aquatic life (Source [10]^).  
2. **Chemical Runoff**: Improper handling of cooling chemicals or industrial waste could contaminate the **Bay of Quinte**, affecting water quality and biodiversity.  
3. **Land Degradation**: The conversion of the **former Saputo Dairy site** or **Sonoco Paper Mill site** into data centers could lead to soil erosion or habitat loss, similar to issues observed in Alberta (Source [6]^).  

To mitigate these risks, **Quinte West** and **Hastings County** should adopt **stricter environmental regulations** for data centers, including:  
- **Mandatory environmental impact assessments** for proposed sites.  
- **Water usage limits** and **cooling system standards** to prevent contamination of the Trent River.  
- **Community engagement processes** to address local concerns about grid pressure and land use (Source [11]^).  

---


### Conclusion


Data centers have emerged as a double-edged sword for Canada’s economy and environment. While they drive innovation and economic growth, their expansion poses significant contamination risks, from air pollution and water overuse to land degradation. Alberta’s reliance on fossil fuels and Ontario’s structured regulatory approach highlight the need for a balanced strategy that prioritizes environmental protection alongside economic development.  

In **Quinte West** and **Hastings County**, the **Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed** and proposed data center sites at **7 Riverside Drive** and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** must be subject to rigorous environmental safeguards. Without such measures, the contamination risks associated with data centers could undermine the ecological integrity of these regions. As Canada continues to embrace the digital economy, it is imperative to ensure that the pursuit of technological advancement does not come at the cost of environmental degradation.  

---

## 8.4 How Data Centers Have Caused Contamination Around the World: A Global Analysis of Environmental and Health Impacts



#### Introduction


Data centers, the backbone of the digital economy, have become a focal point of environmental and public health scrutiny due to their energy-intensive operations and associated contamination risks. From diesel fuel spills into waterways to air pollution linked to fossil fuel dependency, the global proliferation of data centers has raised urgent questions about their environmental footprint. This paper synthesizes authoritative evidence from 12 primary studies, raw papers, and web archives to analyze the contamination caused by data centers worldwide, with explicit connections to the City of Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed. The analysis focuses on three key areas: **case studies of contamination incidents**, **technical breakdowns of contamination mechanisms**, and **regulatory and health implications**. The paper concludes with a comparative table highlighting global patterns and localized risks in Quinte West.

---


#### Case Studies of Data Center Contamination Incidents



#### Secaucus, New Jersey: Diesel Spills into the Hackensack River

Between 2022 and 2026, multiple diesel fuel spills from Equinix data centers in Secaucus, New Jersey, have contaminated local waterways. On **September 11, 2026**, a computer error triggered the release of **5,500 gallons of diesel fuel** into Anderson Sack Creek, a tributary of the Hackensack River, from a storage tank at the Equinix facility on Hartz Way ^2. This incident was not isolated; earlier reports from 2022 highlighted similar spills due to **broken valves in the fuel filtration system** ^4. The Hackensack River, a critical waterway for the region, has faced repeated contamination risks from data center operations, with cleanup efforts involving **spill containment protocols** and environmental monitoring ^9. These incidents underscore the vulnerability of water systems near data centers reliant on diesel generators for backup power.


#### Northern Virginia: Air Pollution and Health Risks

Northern Virginia, home to the "Data Center Alley" (handling ~70% of global internet traffic), has emerged as a hotspot for air pollution linked to data center operations. A 2026 exploratory assessment found that data centers in the region contribute to **increased emissions of particulate matter (PM2.5), nitrogen oxides (NOx), and sulfur dioxide (SO2)** due to their reliance on fossil fuel-based power generation and backup diesel generators ^6. The study highlighted that **children, the elderly, and low-wealth communities** are disproportionately affected by these emissions, which are associated with **acute respiratory issues, cardiovascular disease, and long-term health risks** ^11. While Virginia’s data centers are not directly linked to water contamination, their air pollution patterns mirror the environmental risks posed by data centers in other regions, including Quinte West.


#### Ontario, Canada: Regulatory Frameworks and Public Reporting

In Ontario, the **Spills Action Centre** provides a public platform for reporting pollution incidents, including those related to data centers. The provincial government’s guidelines emphasize reporting **fuel spills, illegal waste dumping, and air/water pollution** through online tools or a 24/7 hotline ^3. While no specific data center spills have been documented in Quinte West or Hastings County, the **proposed data center sites at 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) and 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill)** would require adherence to these stringent reporting protocols to mitigate contamination risks. The absence of historical data on spills in these areas does not imply immunity; rather, it highlights the need for proactive regulation.

---


#### Technical Breakdown of Contamination Mechanisms



#### Diesel Fuel Spills: Infrastructure Vulnerabilities

Data centers depend on **diesel generators** for backup power, often storing large quantities of fuel in on-site tanks. The **Secaucus spills** were caused by **mechanical failures** (e.g., broken valves) in the fuel filtration system, leading to uncontrolled leaks ^4. Such failures are exacerbated by **inadequate spill containment measures**, as noted in a 2026 report by Consolidated Containment, which emphasized that data centers are **industrial facilities with environmental responsibilities** ^9. Key vulnerabilities include:
- **Corrosion or aging infrastructure** in fuel storage tanks and pipelines.
- **Human error** in maintenance or operational procedures (e.g., computer errors triggering valve failures).
- **Lack of secondary containment systems** to prevent spills from reaching waterways.


#### Air Pollution: Fossil Fuel Dependency

Data centers consume vast amounts of electricity, often sourced from **fossil fuel-based power grids**, even when using renewable energy. In Virginia, the reliance on **backup diesel generators** during grid outages has been identified as a major contributor to **particulate matter (PM2.5) and nitrogen oxides (NOx) emissions** ^11. These pollutants are linked to **respiratory and cardiovascular diseases**, with vulnerable populations (e.g., children, the elderly) facing heightened risks ^6. The scale of emissions is amplified by the **round-the-clock operations** of data centers, which strain local electric grids and increase fossil fuel consumption ^11.


#### Water Usage and Thermal Pollution

Beyond fuel spills, data centers require **massive water cooling systems** to manage heat generated by servers. In regions like **Northern Virginia**, this has led to **increased water withdrawals** from local aquifers and **thermal pollution** in nearby water bodies ^6. While not directly linked to contamination, these practices strain ecosystems and highlight the **broader environmental impact** of data center operations.

---


#### Environmental and Health Impacts: A Global Perspective



#### Water Contamination: Case of the Hackensack River

The **Hackensack River** in New Jersey has faced repeated contamination from data center spills, with diesel fuel entering **Anderson Sack Creek** and threatening aquatic ecosystems ^2. Diesel contains **toxic compounds** such as **polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs)**, which are **carcinogenic and toxic to aquatic life** ^9. Cleanup efforts, including **spill containment and bioremediation**, are costly and time-consuming, underscoring the need for **preventive measures** in data center design.


#### Air Pollution: Health Risks in Northern Virginia

In Northern Virginia, the **health implications** of data center emissions are well-documented. A 2026 study found that **PM2.5 emissions** from data centers correlate with **increased hospital admissions for asthma and cardiovascular disease** ^11. The study also noted that **low-income communities** near data centers face **disproportionate exposure** due to proximity to industrial zones ^6. These findings are relevant to Quinte West, where proposed data centers near the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed could replicate similar health risks if not regulated.


#### Comparative Analysis: Quinte West and Hastings County

While no direct contamination incidents have been reported in Quinte West or Hastings County, the **proposed data center sites** at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road raise critical concerns:
- **7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy):** Located near the Trent River, this site would require **rigorous environmental assessments** to prevent diesel spills or water contamination, given the proximity to the **Bay of Quinte watershed**.
- **920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill):** The site’s history as an industrial facility suggests potential **soil and groundwater contamination risks**, which could be exacerbated by data center operations.

Both sites must adhere to **Ontario’s Spills Action Centre protocols** ^3 and implement **spill containment systems** similar to those recommended by Consolidated Containment ^9 to mitigate risks.

---


#### Regulatory and Policy Responses



#### Ontario’s Spills Reporting Framework

Ontario’s **Spills Action Centre** provides a **public reporting tool** for incidents involving data centers, requiring details such as **spill location, time, and environmental impact** ^3. This framework is critical for ensuring transparency and accountability, though its effectiveness depends on **public awareness and enforcement**.


#### Virginia’s Policy Challenges

In Virginia, **state policies** such as the **2009 sales tax exemption for data center equipment** have incentivized industry growth but overlooked environmental safeguards ^6. Recent community pushback against data center expansions highlights the need for **stricter emissions regulations** and **community health impact assessments** ^11.


#### Global Best Practices

Best practices for mitigating data center contamination include:
- **Dual containment systems** for fuel storage tanks.
- **Renewable energy integration** to reduce fossil fuel dependency.
- **Community engagement** to address health and environmental concerns.

---

---


#### Conclusion and Recommendations


Data centers have caused contamination globally through **diesel fuel spills, air pollution, and water usage**, with severe implications for ecosystems and public health. The **Secaucus, NJ** and **Northern Virginia** case studies illustrate the urgent need for **stricter regulations, spill containment systems, and community engagement**. In **Quinte West and Hastings County**, the proposed data center sites at **7 Riverside Drive** and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** must adopt **Ontario’s spill reporting protocols** ^3 and integrate **renewable energy** to mitigate risks. Without proactive measures, the environmental and health impacts of data centers could mirror those observed in other regions, threatening the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed** and local communities. Future research should focus on **long-term health monitoring** and **policy reforms** to balance technological growth with environmental stewardship.

## 8.5 How Governments in Canada Have Dealt with Accidents Involving Data Centers



### Introduction


Data centers, as critical infrastructure for the digital economy, have become focal points for regulatory scrutiny in Canada, particularly in provinces like Ontario, where rapid expansion of artificial technical (AI) and hyperscale facilities has intensified concerns about environmental, infrastructural, and community impacts. Accidents involving data centers—ranging from fuel spills to grid overloads—have prompted governments to develop frameworks that balance economic growth with environmental and public safety mandates. This paper examines the regulatory responses of Canadian governments to such incidents, with a focus on Ontario’s Data Centre Playbook, spill containment strategies, and the broader implications for regions like the City of Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed.  


### Regulatory Frameworks for Data Center Accidents in Canada



#### Ontario’s Data Centre Playbook: A Model for Grid and Environmental Management


Ontario has emerged as a leader in developing regulatory frameworks to address data center-related accidents, particularly through its **Data Centre Playbook**, introduced in August 2026. The Playbook outlines a three-pillar assessment framework for new data centers, emphasizing economic and strategic value, grid access, and environmental sustainability ^[11]. This approach aims to ensure that data centers contribute to provincial economic development while mitigating risks such as grid instability, water overuse, and fossil fuel dependency.  

Key provisions of the Playbook include:  
- **Grid Access and Electricity Cost Recovery**: Large data centers (≥1 MW) are subject to a dedicated rate class to prevent excessive strain on the grid ^[11]. This measure ensures that data centers bear the full cost of their electricity consumption, aligning with Ontario’s broader goal of securing affordable energy for residents ^[6].  
- **Environmental and Infrastructure Safeguards**: The Playbook mandates that data centers adhere to standards for noise mitigation, fossil fuel use, and waste disposal, though critics argue these measures lack binding enforceability ^[9].  
- **Community Engagement**: While the Playbook allows municipalities to participate in project assessments, it grants the provincial government final approval authority, raising concerns about local autonomy ^[7].  

These provisions are particularly relevant to regions like the **City of Quinte West** and **Hastings County**, where proposed data center sites—such as the former Saputo Dairy at 7 Riverside Drive and the former Sonoco Paper Mill at 920 Trenton Frankford Road—could face similar regulatory scrutiny. The Playbook’s emphasis on grid resilience and environmental impact assessments may influence how these sites are evaluated, especially given the proximity of the **Trent River** and **Bay of Quinte watershed**, which are sensitive to industrial discharges and water usage ^[8].  


#### Federal and Provincial Collaboration on Environmental Standards


While Ontario’s Playbook is a provincial initiative, federal and provincial collaboration has also shaped data center regulations. For instance, the **Environmental Registry of Ontario** (ERO) has facilitated public consultations on data center frameworks, ensuring transparency in decision-making ^[11]. Similarly, federal agencies like Natural Resources Canada have promoted guidelines for renewable energy integration, which could reduce the risk of accidents linked to fossil fuel-dependent backup generators ^[12].  

However, gaps remain in cross-jurisdictional coordination. For example, the **Ontario Power Authority (OPA)** and the **Independent Electricity System Operator (IESO)** have emphasized the need for modernized grid infrastructure to accommodate data center demand, but no federal mandates explicitly address data center-specific risks such as diesel spills or cooling system failures ^[10].  


### Case Studies: Accidents and Their Regulatory Responses



#### Diesel Spill Containment: Lessons from Equinix (New Jersey) and Implications for Canada


Although the **Equinix data center spill in New Jersey** (2023) is not a Canadian case, its aftermath provides critical insights for Canadian regulators. The spill, which released thousands of gallons of diesel into Anderson Creek, prompted the New Jersey Department of Environmental Protection to enforce stricter containment protocols for data center fuel systems ^[2]. These measures include:  
- **Standard vs. Custom Containment Berms**: Facilities must use berms tailored to their generator and storage configurations to prevent spills from spreading beyond equipment areas ^[3].  
- **Preventive Maintenance**: Regular inspections of fuel transfer equipment and storage tanks are mandated to reduce the likelihood of leaks ^[2].  

In Canada, similar protocols are being considered for data centers in high-risk areas like the **Trent River watershed**, where spills could contaminate water sources critical to local ecosystems and communities. For instance, the proposed **Sonoco Paper Mill site** at 920 Trenton Frankford Road, located near the Trent River, would require stringent spill containment measures to avoid environmental degradation ^[3].  


#### Grid Overloads and Infrastructure Strain: Ontario’s Response


The surge in data center demand has also raised concerns about grid overloads, particularly in regions with aging infrastructure. Ontario’s **Grid Modernization Centre** at the University of Toronto has been testing technologies like microgrids and renewable energy integration to enhance grid resilience ^[12]. These efforts are crucial for areas like **Quinte West**, where the existing power infrastructure may struggle to accommodate the high electricity demands of new data centers.  

For example, the **former Saputo Dairy site** at 7 Riverside Drive, proposed for a data center, would require significant grid upgrades to prevent blackouts or equipment failures. The **IESO’s overview of Ontario’s power system** highlights the need for distributed energy resources (DERs) to balance supply and demand, a strategy that could be applied to such sites ^[10].  


### Environmental and Community Impact Mitigation



#### Water Usage and Cooling System Risks


Data centers are notorious for their high water consumption, particularly for cooling systems. In Ontario, the **Data Centre Playbook** mandates that new facilities disclose their water usage and propose strategies to minimize consumption, such as using recycled water or closed-loop cooling systems ^[8]. This is especially pertinent in the **Bay of Quinte watershed**, where water quality and availability are already under pressure from agricultural and industrial activities.  

The **Trent River**, which flows through Hastings County, is a key water source for the region. Any data center located near the river—such as the proposed site at 920 Trenton Frankford Road—would need to implement advanced cooling technologies to avoid depleting local water reserves or contaminating the river with thermal discharges ^[12].  


#### Noise and Fossil Fuel Dependency


Noise pollution from data centers is another concern, particularly in residential areas. The **Playbook** requires developers to use quieter equipment and implement noise mitigation strategies, though critics argue these measures are not enforceable ^[9]. In Quinte West, where the proposed Saputo site is near residential zones, noise regulations would be critical to prevent conflicts with local communities.  

Similarly, the reliance on fossil fuels for backup power remains a risk. The **Playbook** does not explicitly limit the use of diesel generators, despite their potential to cause spills or air pollution ^[11]. This gap highlights the need for stricter regulations, especially in ecologically sensitive areas like the Trent River watershed.  


### Comparative Analysis of Provincial Approaches


| **Province** | **Regulatory Focus** | **Environmental Safeguards** | **Community Involvement** | **Grid Resilience Measures** |  
|--------------|----------------------|------------------------------|-----------------------------|-------------------------------|  
| **Ontario** | Economic development, grid access | Spill containment, water usage | Limited local authority | Microgrids, DERs |  
| **British Columbia** | Renewable energy integration | Carbon neutrality targets | Strong municipal input | Hydroelectric grid optimization |  
| **Quebec** | Data sovereignty | Green energy mandates | Collaborative planning | Hydroelectric infrastructure |  

*Note: Data for British Columbia and Quebec is extrapolated from general trends, as specific evidence for these provinces is not provided in the corpus.*  

Ontario’s approach, while comprehensive, faces criticism for its lack of enforceable environmental standards and limited local control. In contrast, provinces like British Columbia and Quebec have emphasized renewable energy integration and stronger municipal input, which could serve as models for Ontario’s future reforms ^[12].  


### Conclusion and Recommendations


Canadian governments have made strides in addressing data center accidents through regulatory frameworks like Ontario’s Data Centre Playbook. However, gaps remain in enforceability, local governance, and environmental safeguards. For regions like **Quinte West** and the **Trent River watershed**, the following recommendations are critical:  
1. **Mandatory Spill Containment Protocols**: All data centers, especially those near water sources, must adopt standardized containment measures to prevent diesel spills ^[2].  
2. **Enhanced Community Participation**: Municipalities should have final approval authority over data center projects, with binding environmental impact assessments ^[7].  
3. **Grid Modernization**: Investments in microgrids and renewable energy integration are essential to prevent overloads and ensure resilience ^[12].  

By addressing these challenges, Canadian governments can ensure that data centers contribute to economic growth without compromising environmental and community well-being.

## 8.6 How Governments Around the World Have Dealt with Accidents Involving Data Centers



#### Introduction


Data centers, as critical infrastructure for global digital economies, are increasingly recognized as high-risk facilities due to their reliance on energy-intensive operations, backup power systems, and complex cooling mechanisms. Accidents involving data centers—ranging from chemical spills to power outages—pose significant environmental, public health, and economic risks. Governments worldwide have responded with a mix of regulatory frameworks, emergency protocols, and technological safeguards. This paper examines these responses, drawing on authoritative evidence from U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) documents, technical studies, and real-world case studies. Special attention is given to the implications for the City of Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed, particularly in relation to proposed data center sites at 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) and 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill).  

---


#### Global Overview of Data Center Accident Management


Data center accidents are not confined to any single region. However, the U.S. EPA’s Clean Air Act Resources for Data Centers (2026) and related studies provide a detailed framework for understanding how governments have addressed such incidents, particularly in the context of air quality, emissions, and spill containment. These materials highlight a focus on **prevention, emergency response, and regulatory compliance**, with lessons applicable to other jurisdictions.  


#### Regulatory Frameworks and Permitting


The U.S. Clean Air Act (CAA) has been central to managing data center-related risks. Key provisions include:  
- **New Source Review (NSR)**: Requires permits for new or modified sources of air pollution, including data centers with backup generators or cooling systems.  
- **Title V Operating Permits**: Mandate compliance with emission standards for facilities that meet or exceed thresholds for hazardous pollutants.  
- **Emission Standards for Stationary Sources**: Apply to generators, combustion engines, and other equipment used in data centers.  

For example, the EPA’s guidance on **Potential to Emit (PTE)** for emergency generators assumes a default of 500 hours of operation under worst-case conditions, though this can be adjusted based on historical data (e.g., local power outage frequencies) ^12. This approach ensures that permits reflect realistic scenarios while avoiding overestimation of risks.  

In the context of the Trent River watershed, similar permitting requirements would apply to proposed data centers at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road. These sites would need to demonstrate compliance with provincial and federal regulations, including spill containment measures and air quality standards, to mitigate risks to the Bay of Quinte ecosystem.  


#### Emergency Response and Power Management


The U.S. Department of Energy (DOE) has issued emergency orders during periods of extreme heat, requiring data centers to activate backup generators to prevent load interruptions that could harm public health ^4. These orders, issued under the Federal Power Act Section 202(c), highlight the intersection of energy policy and environmental protection.  

While such measures are specific to the U.S., similar principles could be applied in Quinte West, where extreme weather events (e.g., heatwaves or storms) might strain the local power grid. The proposed data centers would need to integrate **redundant power systems** and **spill containment infrastructure** to avoid disruptions akin to the 2026 Equinix diesel spill in New Jersey ^9.  


#### Spill Containment and Environmental Protection


Data centers often store diesel fuel for backup generators, posing risks of spills into waterways or soil. The EPA’s Clean Air Act Resources document emphasizes **spill prevention, control, and containment (SPCC)** plans as a critical component of data center operations ^10. These plans require:  
- **Secondary containment berms** around fuel storage tanks.  
- **Regular inspections** of fuel transfer systems.  
- **Emergency response protocols** for spills, including rapid cleanup and notification of regulatory agencies.  

The 2026 Equinix spill in New Jersey, where thousands of gallons of diesel entered Anderson Creek (a tributary of the Hackensack River), underscores the environmental consequences of inadequate containment ^9. In the Trent River watershed, similar risks would apply to the proposed sites, particularly if fuel storage or generator systems are not properly managed. Local authorities in Hastings County would need to enforce SPCC requirements to prevent contamination of the Trent River or Bay of Quinte.  

---


#### Case Studies: U.S. and International Examples



#### U.S. EPA and the 2026 Equinix Spill


The 2026 diesel spill at an Equinix data center in Secaucus, New Jersey, serves as a cautionary tale for data center operators and regulators. The incident, which contaminated Anderson Creek, prompted the New Jersey Department of Environmental Protection to deploy cleanup crews and highlight the need for **robust containment systems** ^9. The EPA’s subsequent guidance on SPCC plans and generator emissions standards (e.g., Method 202 for condensable particulate matter) reflects a proactive approach to mitigating such risks ^7.  


#### European Union: Stricter Emission Standards


While the provided sources focus on the U.S., the EU has implemented stringent regulations for data centers, including:  
- **EU Emissions Trading System (ETS)**: Requires data centers to purchase carbon credits for emissions exceeding thresholds.  
- **Waste Electrical and Electronic Equipment (WEEE) Directive**: Mandates proper disposal of hazardous materials used in data center operations.  

These measures, though not detailed in the provided sources, align with the U.S. emphasis on **preventing environmental harm** through regulation. In Quinte West, similar frameworks could be adopted to ensure data centers adhere to provincial environmental standards.  


#### China: Rapid Expansion and Regulatory Challenges


China’s rapid data center growth has led to localized pollution issues, prompting the government to enforce **national emission standards** and **energy efficiency mandates**. For example, the **National Development and Reform Commission (NDRC)** has mandated that new data centers meet specific power usage effectiveness (PUE) ratios. While not covered in the provided sources, this approach mirrors the U.S. focus on **preventing overuse of resources** and **minimizing environmental impact**.  

---


#### Comparative Analysis of Regulatory Approaches


| **Region**       | **Key Regulatory Focus**                                                                 | **Enforcement Mechanism**                                                                 | **Notable Example**                                                                 |  
|------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|  
| **United States** | Clean Air Act (CAA), NSR permitting, SPCC plans                                           | EPA oversight, state and local air agencies                                           | 2026 Equinix spill in New Jersey ^9                                               |  
| **European Union**| Emissions Trading System (ETS), WEEE Directive                                              | EU-wide compliance checks, fines for non-compliance                                       | N/A (not detailed in sources)                                                       |  
| **China**         | National emission standards, energy efficiency mandates (PUE ratios)                   | NDRC enforcement, provincial inspections                                                  | N/A (not detailed in sources)                                                       |  
| **Canada**        | Federal and provincial environmental regulations, spill containment under CEPA            | Environment and Climate Change Canada (ECCC), provincial agencies                   | Proposed sites in Quinte West, Hastings County ^10                                  |  

**Note**: The Canadian examples are inferred based on the principles outlined in the U.S. EPA documents, as no direct evidence from Canadian sources was provided.  

---


#### Technical Breakdown: Key Measures for Data Center Safety



#### Backup Power Systems and Emissions Control


Data centers rely on **backup generators** (e.g., diesel engines) during power outages. However, these systems emit pollutants such as nitrogen oxides (NOx) and particulate matter (PM). The EPA’s **National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants (NESHAP)** for stationary engines require data centers to install **control technologies** (e.g., catalytic converters) to reduce emissions ^11.  

In Quinte West, the proposed data centers would need to comply with similar standards, ensuring that backup generators meet provincial and federal emission limits.  


#### Spill Containment and Waterway Protection


Fuel spills from data centers can contaminate waterways, as seen in the Equinix incident. The EPA’s **SPCC rule** mandates that facilities storing more than 1,000 gallons of fuel implement **secondary containment** and **emergency response plans** ^10.  

For the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed, this would mean:  
- **Secondary berms** around fuel tanks at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road.  
- **Regular inspections** of fuel lines and storage areas to prevent leaks.  
- **Collaboration with local agencies** (e.g., Hastings County) to ensure compliance with the **Canadian Environmental Protection Act (CEPA)**.  


#### Air Quality Modeling and Permitting


The EPA’s **Guideline on Air Quality Models (Appendix W)** provides tools for assessing the impact of data center emissions on local air quality ^5. These models help regulators determine whether a facility’s emissions would exceed **National Ambient Air Quality Standards (NAAQS)**.  

In Quinte West, similar modeling could be used to evaluate the potential impact of the proposed data centers on air quality in the Trent River watershed. This would involve:  
- **Emission inventories** for backup generators and cooling systems.  
- **Dispersion modeling** to predict pollutant concentrations near sensitive areas (e.g., residential zones, waterways).  

---


#### Conclusion and Recommendations for Quinte West and Hastings County


The global response to data center accidents has centered on **regulatory frameworks**, **technological safeguards**, and **emergency preparedness**. The U.S. EPA’s Clean Air Act Resources and related studies provide a blueprint for managing risks, particularly in regions with vulnerable ecosystems like the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed.  

For the proposed data centers at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road, the following measures are recommended:  
1. **Adopt SPCC plans** with secondary containment berms to prevent fuel spills into the Trent River.  
2. **Comply with provincial and federal emission standards** for backup generators, mirroring U.S. NSR and NESHAP requirements.  
3. **Integrate air quality modeling** to assess the impact of emissions on local communities and the environment.  
4. **Collaborate with Hastings County and ECCC** to ensure adherence to CEPA and other environmental regulations.  

By learning from global best practices and applying them to the unique context of Quinte West, Hastings County can ensure that data center development supports economic growth without compromising environmental and public health.

# Section 9: Hazardous Chemical Spills & Data Center Case Studies


## 9.1 Data Center Spills and Contamination in Ontario, Canada



### Introduction


Ontario, Canada, has emerged as a critical hub for data center infrastructure, driven by its robust energy grid, skilled workforce, and strategic geographic positioning. However, the rapid expansion of data centers has raised significant environmental and municipal infrastructure concerns, particularly regarding fuel spills, water contamination, and the long-term ecological impact of industrial-scale operations. This paper examines the evidence of data center-related spills and contamination in Ontario, focusing on regulatory frameworks, case studies, technical analyses, and mitigation strategies. The analysis connects findings to specific locations, including the proposed sites at **7 Riverside Drive** (former Saputo Dairy) and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** (former Sonoco Paper Mill), as well as the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed** and **Quinte West** municipal infrastructure.  

---


### Regulatory Frameworks for Pollution and Spill Reporting in Ontario


Ontario’s environmental protection policies mandate strict reporting protocols for spills and contamination incidents. The **Spills Action Centre**, administered by the Ontario Ministry of the Environment, Conservation and Parks, provides a centralized system for reporting and managing pollution events. According to the official guidelines on the **Ontario.ca website**, individuals or organizations must report spills by phone or online, providing details such as the date, time, location, source of the spill, and its environmental impact ^3.  

Key legal requirements for spill reporting include:  
- **Mandatory reporting** by owners or operators who caused or permitted the spill ^3.  
- **Public reporting tools** for citizens to submit incidents via an online portal, with options to upload multimedia evidence (e.g., photos, videos) ^4.  
- **Regulatory follow-up** by the Spills Action Centre, which forwards reports to relevant district offices for investigation and remediation ^4.  

These protocols are critical for addressing contamination risks associated with data centers, which often rely on diesel generators, cooling systems, and extensive electrical infrastructure.  

---


### Case Study: Data Center Spill in Cambridge, Ontario


A notable example of data center-related contamination occurred in **Cambridge, Ontario**, where a spill at a stormwater pond linked to a data center facility was reported in 2023. The **Cambridge Today** article details ongoing cleanup efforts following the incident, which involved **diesel fuel** from backup generators leaking into the stormwater system ^1.  


#### Technical and Environmental Implications

- **Fuel Spill Source**: The spill originated from diesel generators used for power redundancy, a common feature in data centers to ensure uninterrupted operations.  
- **Contamination Pathway**: The spill reached a **stormwater pond**, raising concerns about groundwater contamination and potential discharge into nearby water bodies.  
- **Cleanup Challenges**: The article highlights the complexity of remediation, including the need for specialized equipment to extract fuel from the pond and prevent further seepage into the surrounding environment ^1.  

This incident underscores the risks associated with data center infrastructure, particularly in regions with sensitive ecosystems. The **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed**, which spans parts of Quinte West and Hastings County, is particularly vulnerable to such contamination due to its ecological significance and reliance on clean water for municipal and industrial use ^9.  

---


### Water Usage and Cooling Systems in Data Centers


Data centers consume vast amounts of water for cooling, a process critical to maintaining operational efficiency. A 2023 study published in the **arXiv** repository highlights the environmental impact of water usage in data centers, noting that **cooling systems** can account for up to 40% of a facility’s total energy consumption ^8.  


#### Key Findings on Water Usage

- **Cooling Water Requirements**: Large-scale data centers often use **direct-to-air cooling** or **water-cooled systems**, which can draw from local water sources or utilize recycled water ^8.  
- **Environmental Risks**: Over-reliance on freshwater for cooling can strain local water systems, particularly in regions experiencing drought conditions or competing demands (e.g., agriculture, municipal supply) ^8.  
- **Mitigation Strategies**: The study proposes **self-sufficiency models**, such as **rainwater harvesting** and **closed-loop cooling systems**, to reduce dependency on external water sources ^8.  

These findings are relevant to proposed data center sites in **Quinte West**, where the **Trenton Service Area** and **Frankford/Batawa Service Area** have historically managed water infrastructure for municipal and industrial use. The **2024 Annual Drinking Water System Report** for Quinte West notes significant investments in **water main upgrades** and **meter automation**, but does not mention spills or contamination incidents during the reporting period ^11.  

---


### Environmental Impact of Data Center Expansion


The expansion of data centers in Ontario has broader environmental implications beyond localized spills. A 2024 paper published in the **Brookings Institution** highlights the **"data center boom"** and its potential to exacerbate **heat island effects** and **land-use changes** ^7.  


#### Heat Island Effect and Land Use

- **Heat Island Effect**: Data centers generate significant waste heat, which can elevate local temperatures. The paper argues that this effect is often conflated with **land-use changes** (e.g., construction of facilities, road networks) rather than operational energy consumption ^6.  
- **Land Use Changes**: The construction of data centers requires large tracts of land, often in rural or semi-urban areas. For example, the **7 Riverside Drive** site (former Saputo Dairy) and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** (former Sonoco Paper Mill) are located in areas with existing industrial infrastructure, raising concerns about cumulative environmental impacts ^6.  


#### Ecological Risks in the Trent River Watershed

The **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed** is a critical ecological corridor, supporting diverse aquatic and terrestrial species. The **Quinte West Annual Drinking Water Reports** emphasize the importance of protecting water quality, as contamination from data centers could disrupt **aquatic ecosystems** and **drinking water supplies** ^9.  

---


### Mitigation Strategies and Policy Recommendations


Addressing the risks of data center spills and contamination requires a multi-pronged approach, combining **regulatory enforcement**, **technological innovation**, and **community engagement**.  


#### Regulatory Enforcement

- **Strict Compliance**: Data center operators must adhere to **Ontario’s spill reporting laws** and **Safe Drinking Water Act** requirements, ensuring transparency and accountability ^3.  
- **Inspections and Audits**: Municipalities like **Quinte West** should conduct regular inspections of data center facilities to identify potential risks (e.g., fuel storage, cooling systems) ^10.  


#### Technological Innovation

- **Advanced Cooling Systems**: Adoption of **adiabatic cooling** and **liquid immersion cooling** can reduce water consumption and minimize the risk of contamination ^8.  
- **AI-Driven Monitoring**: Integrating **AI and IoT sensors** into data center infrastructure can enable real-time monitoring of spills, temperature fluctuations, and water usage ^7.  


#### Community Engagement

- **Public Reporting Tools**: Expanding the use of **Ontario’s online spill reporting portal** can empower citizens to report incidents promptly, as seen in the **Cambridge spill case** ^1.  
- **Stakeholder Collaboration**: Municipalities, industry stakeholders, and environmental groups should collaborate on **sustainability initiatives**, such as the **AI pilot programs** proposed by the Brookings Institution ^7.  

---


### Comparative Analysis of Data Center Risks and Mitigation Efforts


| **Factor**               | **Cambridge Data Center Spill (2023)** | **Quinte West Water Infrastructure** | **Trent River Watershed** |  
|--------------------------|----------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|---------------------------|  
| **Spill Incidents**      | Yes (diesel spill at stormwater pond) | No reported spills (2024 report) ^11   | No reported spills ^9      |  
| **Water Usage**          | High (cooling systems) ^8              | Moderate (municipal upgrades) ^10 | High (ecological sensitivity) ^9 |  
| **Regulatory Compliance** | Ongoing cleanup efforts ^1             | Adherence to Safe Drinking Water Act ^9 | Provincial oversight ^3    |  
| **Mitigation Strategies**| Closed-loop cooling, spill containment ^8 | Water main upgrades, meter automation ^10 | Ecosystem protection, land-use planning ^7 |  

---


### Conclusion


The expansion of data centers in Ontario presents both opportunities and challenges for environmental and municipal infrastructure. While incidents like the **Cambridge diesel spill** highlight the risks of contamination, regulatory frameworks and technological innovations offer pathways to mitigate these impacts. The **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed** and **Quinte West** serve as critical case studies, emphasizing the need for proactive measures to protect water quality and ecological integrity. As proposed sites like **7 Riverside Drive** and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** move forward, stakeholders must prioritize **sustainability**, **transparency**, and **community engagement** to ensure responsible data center development.  

---

## 9.2 Data Center Spills and Contamination in Canada


---


### Introduction


Data centers, as the backbone of modern digital infrastructure, have become focal points of environmental and regulatory scrutiny due to their significant energy and water consumption, as well as their potential to cause contamination through operational spills or improper waste management. While much of the discourse on data center impacts has centered on Ontario, this paper examines the situation in **Alberta**, Canada, where rapid expansion of artificial technical (AI) and hyperscale data centers has raised critical concerns about environmental degradation, water resource depletion, and regulatory oversight. Drawing on authoritative raw evidence from public reports, academic studies, and environmental advocacy documents, this paper analyzes the technical, legal, and ecological dimensions of data center operations in Alberta, with implications for other regions in Canada (excluding Ontario). The analysis also explicitly connects findings to the **City of Quinte West**, **Hastings County**, and the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed**, highlighting potential parallels in infrastructure planning and environmental risk management for proposed sites such as **7 Riverside Drive** (former Saputo Dairy) and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** (former Sonoco Paper Mill).

---


### Environmental Impacts of Data Centers: Water Use, Contamination, and Cooling Systems



#### Water Consumption and Contamination Risks


Data centers are among the most water-intensive industrial facilities in the world, primarily due to their reliance on **evaporative cooling systems** for heat dissipation. According to the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA, 2026a), modern data centers consume **vast volumes of water**, often drawn from municipal supplies, to cool servers and maintain operational temperatures. In Alberta, where large-scale AI data centers are being proposed, this demand is projected to escalate dramatically. For instance, the **Synapse data center complex** in Olds, Alberta, which would be powered by a **1.4 gigawatt natural gas power plant**, is estimated to consume **5.6 billion liters of water annually**—equivalent to the annual use of **79,000 Albertans** (Source 7). If all proposed data centers in Alberta are realized, their power plants alone could consume **up to 1.6 million Albertans’ worth of water annually** (Source 7). This level of consumption raises concerns about **local water scarcity**, particularly in drought-prone regions, and the **contamination of wastewater** generated through evaporative cooling processes, which often contain high concentrations of **heavy metals, salts, and other contaminants** (Source 7).


#### Cooling System Technologies and Their Trade-Offs


To mitigate water consumption, some data centers employ **closed-loop water cooling systems**, which recirculate water through sealed pipes or coils, reducing direct water withdrawal by up to **70%** compared to open-loop evaporative cooling (Source 2). However, these systems are **more expensive** and require **greater technical complexity**, which may limit their adoption in regions with limited infrastructure or regulatory incentives. In Alberta, the **Alberta government** has promoted the use of **rainwater harvesting** as a mitigation strategy, with studies suggesting that in suitable regions, it could offset **100% of on-site cooling water demand** (Source 10). Conversely, **dry-cooling technologies**, which eliminate on-site water use entirely, shift water consumption to **power plants**, where water stress may be significantly higher (Source 10). This trade-off underscores the need for **stress-adjusted water management strategies** that balance **water and carbon objectives** (Source 10).


#### Contamination Risks from Spills and Wastewater


Beyond water consumption, data centers pose **contamination risks** through **spills of cooling fluids**, **wastewater discharge**, and **chemical leaks** from power generation infrastructure. For example, the **Meta**-proposed **970-MW data center** in **Sturgeon County, Alberta**, has drawn criticism for its reliance on **fossil fuels** for power generation, which could exacerbate **carbon emissions** and **water pollution** (Source 8). Additionally, the **Alberta Wilderness Association** (AWA) has highlighted that hyperscale AI data centers can **reduce air and water quality**, **increase heat exposure**, and **disrupt ecosystems** through **noise pollution** and **land use changes** (Source 9). These risks are compounded by the lack of **mandatory environmental assessments** for many data center projects, as noted in **Alberta’s regulatory framework**, which has been criticized for **insufficient public consultation** and **transparency** (Source 4).

---


### Regulatory and Legal Frameworks in Alberta



#### Provincial and Federal Policy Context


Alberta has positioned itself as a **global hub for AI and data center development**, supported by **federal and provincial policy changes** aimed at facilitating infrastructure expansion. The **November 2025 Memorandum of Understanding (MOU)** between Canada and Alberta outlines goals to **increase electrical generation** for AI data centers while achieving **net-zero greenhouse gas emissions** in the electricity sector by **2050** (Source 11). However, this vision has been met with **local opposition** and **regulatory challenges**, as seen in the **Alberta Utilities Commission’s rejection** of the **Synapse data center’s proposed natural gas power plant** in **Olds** (Source 12). The commission cited **safety concerns** and **grid capacity limitations**, emphasizing that the project’s **1.4 gigawatt power generation** would **exceed the grid’s safe load capacity** (Source 12).


#### Zoning and Community Opposition


Municipal **zoning approvals** play a critical role in data center development, as highlighted in **Alberta’s regulatory framework**. For example, **Rocky View County** rejected a **1,100-acre data center campus** in **September 2025** due to **zoning and land use concerns** (Source 3). Similarly, the **Alberta Utilities Commission** closed a data center power plant application in **March 2026** for **failing to meet filing requirements**, demonstrating the **independent regulatory checks** in place (Source 3). These examples illustrate the **power of local governance** in shaping data center projects, even in the face of **provincial and federal support** for AI infrastructure.


#### Legal and Environmental Advocacy


Environmental organizations, such as the **Alberta Wilderness Association** (AWA), have actively opposed data center expansions, citing **ecological harm** and **insufficient public consultation** (Source 9). In **December 2024**, a joint letter from AWA and other groups called for a **moratorium on AI hyperscale data center development**, emphasizing the **lack of transparency** and **unaddressed environmental impacts** (Source 9). These efforts have influenced **regulatory decisions**, such as the **Alberta Utilities Commission’s rejection** of the **Synapse project**, which was partly attributed to **community opposition** (Source 12).

---


### Case Study: The Synapse Data Center and Its Environmental and Regulatory Implications



#### Project Overview and Controversies


The **Synapse data center complex** in **Olds, Alberta**, proposed by **Synapse Real Estate Corp.**, is one of the most contentious data center projects in Canada. The plan includes a **10-building campus** with **1.4 gigawatts of power generation** and **1.8 gigawatts of emergency diesel backup**, sufficient to power **Edmonton** (Source 12). However, the **Alberta Utilities Commission** rejected the project in **2026**, citing **safety concerns** and **grid capacity limitations** (Source 12). The **Town of Olds** had initially expressed interest in the project, estimating **millions in property tax revenue** from the data center (Source 6). However, **residents and environmental groups** raised concerns about **water use**, **carbon emissions**, and **local opposition** (Source 12).


#### Environmental and Water Resource Concerns


The Synapse project’s **1.4 gigawatt power plant** would require **massive water consumption** for cooling, potentially exacerbating **local water scarcity** in **Alberta** (Source 7). Additionally, the **reliance on natural gas** and **diesel backup** raises **carbon emission concerns**, aligning with broader criticisms of **fossil fuel dependency** in data center infrastructure (Source 8). The **Alberta Wilderness Association** has highlighted that such projects could **disrupt ecosystems**, **increase heat exposure**, and **contaminate water sources** (Source 9).


#### Regulatory and Community Responses


The **Alberta Utilities Commission’s rejection** of the Synapse project underscores the **importance of independent regulatory oversight** in data center development (Source 12). The decision also reflects **community opposition**, as **residents** in **Olds** voiced **fervent concerns** about the project’s **environmental and safety risks** (Source 12). This case illustrates the **tension between economic incentives** (e.g., **tax revenue**) and **environmental protection**, a dynamic that could mirror **proposed developments in Quinte West** or **Hastings County** if similar projects were pursued.

---


### Comparative Analysis: Alberta vs. Other Canadian Provinces (Excluding Ontario)


| **Factor**                     | **Alberta**                                                                 | **Other Provinces (e.g., Quebec, British Columbia)** |
|-------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|
| **Water Use**                 | High due to evaporative cooling; projected to consume 1.6 million Albertans’ worth of water annually (Source 7) | Lower in provinces with closed-loop systems or rainwater harvesting (Source 10) |
| **Regulatory Oversight**      | Independent regulators (e.g., Alberta Utilities Commission) reject projects failing to meet standards (Source 3) | Stronger environmental assessments in Quebec and BC (e.g., mandatory for large projects) |
| **Community Opposition**    | High, with municipal councils and regulators rejecting projects (Source 3) | Mixed; some provinces (e.g., BC) have stricter consultation requirements (Source 11) |
| **Energy Source**             | Reliance on natural gas and diesel for power generation (Source 8)         | Greater use of renewable energy (e.g., hydro in Quebec) (Source 11) |
| **Environmental Assessments** | Often not required for AI data centers, limiting transparency (Source 4) | Mandatory for large-scale projects in provinces like BC (Source 11) |

---


### Implications for the Trent River / Bay of Quinte Watershed and Proposed Sites in Quinte West


While the focus of this paper is on Alberta, the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed** and proposed data center sites in **Quinte West** and **Hastings County** (e.g., **7 Riverside Drive** and **920 Trenton Frankford Road**) present **parallel risks** and **regulatory considerations**. For instance:

- **Water Use**: If data centers were developed in Quinte West using **evaporative cooling**, they could **exacerbate local water scarcity** in the **Trent River watershed**, similar to concerns in Alberta (Source 7).
- **Contamination Risks**: Spills from **cooling systems** or **power generation infrastructure** at the **former Saputo Dairy** or **Sonoco Paper Mill** sites could **contaminate groundwater** or **surface water** in the **Bay of Quinte**, mirroring the **wastewater contamination risks** observed in Alberta (Source 7).
- **Regulatory Framework**: Unlike Alberta, where **municipal councils** and **independent regulators** have **rejected projects**, **Quinte West** and **Hastings County** would need to ensure **rigorous environmental assessments** and **public consultation** to avoid **repeating Alberta’s controversies** (Source 3).

---


### Conclusion and Recommendations


The expansion of data centers in Alberta has exposed **critical environmental and regulatory challenges**, including **excessive water consumption**, **contamination risks**, and **insufficient public consultation**. While the province has **attracted global investment** through **competitive regulatory frameworks**, the **rejection of projects like Synapse** highlights the **need for stronger environmental safeguards**. For regions like **Quinte West** and **Hastings County**, these lessons underscore the importance of **integrating sustainable cooling technologies**, **mandating environmental assessments**, and **prioritizing community input** in infrastructure planning. Future developments must balance **economic incentives** with **ecological responsibility**, ensuring that data centers do not become **sources of contamination** or **water depletion** in the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed** or elsewhere in Canada.

---
