## 9.3 Data Center Spills and Contamination in the USA: Environmental, Health, and Regulatory Implications



### Introduction


The rapid expansion of data centers in the United States has introduced new environmental and public health challenges, particularly concerning spills of hazardous substances such as diesel fuel, ethylene glycol, and other industrial chemicals. These incidents, often linked to the construction and operation of data centers, pose significant risks to water quality, ecosystems, and human health. This paper examines documented cases of data center-related spills, their environmental and health impacts, and the regulatory frameworks governing such incidents. Special attention is given to the potential implications for the City of Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed, particularly in relation to proposed data center sites at 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) and 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill).  

---


### Case Study: The 5,500-Gallon Diesel Spill in Secaucus, New Jersey



#### Incident Overview


On Friday night, a significant diesel fuel spill occurred at the Equinix data center under construction on Hartz Way in Secaucus, New Jersey. Approximately **5,500 gallons of diesel** spilled from a storage tank into **Anderson Sack Creek**, a tributary of the **Hackensack River**. This event highlights the risks associated with the storage and handling of large volumes of fuel at data centers, particularly those located near water bodies ^2.  


#### Environmental Impact


Diesel fuel contains toxic hydrocarbons, including polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs), which are known to contaminate waterways and harm aquatic life. The spill into Anderson Sack Creek, which flows into the Hackensack River, raises concerns about the long-term ecological damage to the region. The Hackensack River is a critical waterway for both recreational and commercial activities, and contamination could disrupt local fisheries and biodiversity ^2.  


#### Regulatory and Mitigation Measures


Following the spill, local authorities and the **U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA)** would likely initiate cleanup efforts under the **Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation, and Liability Act (CERCLA)**. Such incidents also underscore the need for stringent permitting and spill prevention protocols, particularly for data centers located in ecologically sensitive areas ^9.  

---


### Health Implications of Data Center Spills



#### Toxicological Risks of Spill-Related Chemicals


Data center spills often involve substances such as **ethylene glycol** and **propylene glycol**, which are used in cooling systems and fire suppression technologies. Ethylene glycol, in particular, is highly toxic to humans and animals, with acute exposure leading to kidney failure, central nervous system depression, and even death ^1. Chronic exposure to low concentrations of these chemicals can result in long-term health effects, including respiratory issues, liver damage, and increased cancer risk ^3.  


#### Air and Water Quality Concerns


Spills of diesel fuel and other hydrocarbons can release volatile organic compounds (VOCs) into the air, contributing to poor air quality and exacerbating respiratory conditions such as asthma. The **World Health Organization (WHO)** has linked ambient air pollution to **7 million premature deaths annually**, with VOCs playing a significant role in this burden ^8. In regions like the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed**, where water quality is already sensitive to industrial activity, such spills could further degrade aquatic ecosystems and threaten drinking water sources ^3.  


#### Public Health and Community Impact


The **Health-Informed Computing** study highlights the broader public health implications of data center expansion, including increased energy consumption, air pollution, and the potential for chemical spills ^5. In communities near data centers, such as those in **Quinte West** and **Hastings County**, residents may face heightened risks of exposure to contaminants if spill prevention measures are not strictly enforced ^7.  

---


### Regulatory Frameworks and Compliance Challenges



#### Clean Air Act and EPA Guidelines


The **Clean Air Act (CAA)** provides a legal framework for regulating emissions and spills from industrial facilities, including data centers. Under the CAA, the **EPA** has issued guidelines for air quality modeling, permitting, and compliance with **National Ambient Air Quality Standards (NAAQS)** ^9. These guidelines are critical for ensuring that data centers do not contribute to exceedances of pollutant thresholds, particularly in areas with existing air quality issues ^10.  


#### Permitting and Environmental Assessments


Data centers require **air permits** under the **New Source Review (NSR)** program, which mandates that facilities demonstrate compliance with air quality standards before construction ^10. However, the **EPA’s** recent focus on reducing regulatory burdens, such as the **repeal of 2024 power plant regulations**, may inadvertently weaken protections against spills and emissions ^9. This raises concerns about the adequacy of current permitting processes in preventing incidents like the Secaucus diesel spill.  


#### Local and State-Level Regulations


In addition to federal regulations, **state and local authorities** play a crucial role in enforcing environmental protections. For example, **Virginia’s** **Department of Environmental Quality (DEQ)** issues air permits for data centers, ensuring that facilities adhere to state-specific guidelines ^7. In **Ontario**, where the **City of Quinte West** and **Hastings County** are located, similar regulations would be necessary to mitigate risks at the proposed data center sites.  

---


### Comparative Analysis: Secaucus Spill vs. Potential Risks in Quinte West


| **Factor**                     | **Secaucus, NJ Diesel Spill**                                                                 | **Quinte West, Ontario (Hypothetical Scenario)**                                                                 |
|-------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Location**                  | Anderson Sack Creek (Hackensack River watershed)                                              | Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed (proposed sites: 7 Riverside Drive, 920 Trenton Frankford Road)           |
| **Spill Volume**              | 5,500 gallons of diesel fuel                                                                  | Potential spills of diesel, ethylene glycol, or other industrial chemicals (depending on facility operations)       |
| **Environmental Sensitivity**| Hackensack River is a major waterway with ecological and recreational significance            | Trent River is a key source of drinking water and supports diverse aquatic life; contamination could harm the Bay of Quinte ecosystem |
| **Regulatory Oversight**      | EPA and New Jersey DEP involved in cleanup and enforcement                                    | Ontario Ministry of the Environment, Conservation and Parks (MOECC) would oversee compliance and spill response    |
| **Health Risks**              | Acute and chronic health effects from hydrocarbon exposure                                  | Similar risks to residents and workers near data centers, with potential for long-term contamination of water supplies |

---


### Case Study: Health Impacts in Virginia and Implications for Quinte West



#### Virginia’s Data Center Expansion and Public Health


A 2023 study on the **health implications of data center expansion in Virginia** found that increased energy consumption and associated emissions could exacerbate air pollution, leading to higher rates of respiratory and cardiovascular diseases ^3. The study also highlighted the need for **zero-emission power generation** to mitigate these risks ^3.  


#### Relevance to Quinte West


If data centers are constructed at the proposed sites in **Quinte West**, similar public health risks could emerge, particularly if the facilities rely on diesel generators or other fossil fuel-based energy sources. The **Trent River**’s proximity to these sites increases the likelihood of water contamination from spills, which could affect both human populations and the **Bay of Quinte** ecosystem ^7.  

---


### Mitigation Strategies and Recommendations



#### Engineering and Operational Safeguards


To prevent spills, data centers should adopt **closed-loop cooling systems**, redundant fuel storage tanks, and real-time monitoring technologies. The **EPA’s** **COBRA (Co-benefits Risk Assessment)** tool can help quantify the health benefits of such measures, providing a cost-benefit analysis for facility operators ^18.  


#### Community Engagement and Transparency


Data centers must engage with local communities to address concerns about environmental risks. In **Quinte West**, this could involve public consultations on spill prevention plans and emergency response protocols for the **Trent River** watershed.  


#### Strengthening Regulatory Frameworks


Federal and state regulators should reinforce permitting requirements for data centers, ensuring that facilities in ecologically sensitive areas (like the **Bay of Quinte**) meet the highest standards for spill prevention and environmental protection. The **EPA’s** **Guideline on Air Quality Models** should be updated to reflect the unique challenges posed by data center operations ^10.  

---


### Conclusion


The **Secaucus diesel spill** and other documented incidents underscore the urgent need for robust environmental safeguards in the data center industry. As **Quinte West** and **Hastings County** consider proposals for new data centers, lessons from past spills must inform regulatory decisions to protect the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte** watershed and public health. By integrating advanced engineering solutions, community engagement, and strengthened regulations, the risks associated with data center operations can be minimized, ensuring sustainable development in the digital age.

## 9.4 News Articles and Official Reports: Data Center Spills and Contamination in Europe



### Introduction


Data centers, as critical infrastructure for the digital economy, have become increasingly prevalent across Europe and North America. However, their reliance on diesel generators for backup power and the associated risks of fuel spills and contamination have raised significant environmental and regulatory concerns. While the majority of the evidence presented here pertains to incidents in North America—specifically New Jersey and British Columbia—these cases provide a cautionary framework for understanding potential risks in Europe, particularly in regions with similar geographic, industrial, or regulatory contexts. This paper examines the technical, environmental, and regulatory dimensions of data center-related spills, drawing on authoritative sources, and explicitly connects these findings to the City of Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed, where proposed data center developments at 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) and 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill) may face analogous challenges.  

---


### Case Studies: Data Center Spills and Contamination



#### Secaucus, New Jersey: 5,500-Gallon Diesel Spill


In September 2026, a mechanical failure at the Equinix data center in Secaucus, New Jersey, resulted in the release of approximately 5,500 gallons of diesel fuel into Anderson Sack Creek, a tributary of the Hackensack River. The spill occurred due to the failure of two valves in the fuel filtration system, which allowed diesel to leak from a storage tank into the creek. The incident was reported by the New Jersey Department of Environmental Protection (NJDEP), and cleanup crews were deployed to contain the spill and remove contaminated fuel from the waterway ^[3]^[7]^[8]^[9]^[230].  

This event highlights the vulnerability of data centers to mechanical failures in fuel systems, particularly when located near water bodies. The Hackensack River, a critical ecosystem and drinking water source for millions, is particularly sensitive to such contamination, as diesel spills can introduce toxic hydrocarbons, heavy metals, and nitrogen oxides into aquatic environments, harming aquatic life and degrading water quality ^[11].  


#### Rivers Inlet, British Columbia: Diesel Spill from Storage Tank


In February 2021, a diesel spill of approximately 1,000 liters occurred in Rivers Inlet, British Columbia, due to a failure in a storage tank. The spill was reported by the Wuikinuxv and Heiltsuk First Nations, who reside in the area, and involved multiple agencies, including the B.C. Ministry of Environment and Climate Change Strategy, Indigenous Services Canada, and the Canadian Coast Guard. The spill contaminated local waterways and raised concerns about the impact on Indigenous communities and traditional fishing grounds ^[5].  

This incident underscores the importance of robust spill containment systems and the need for real-time monitoring in remote or ecologically sensitive areas. The proximity of the spill to Indigenous communities also highlights the social and cultural dimensions of such environmental risks, which must be addressed through inclusive regulatory frameworks.  


#### General Trends in Data Center Spill Incidents


Data centers commonly use diesel generators for backup power to ensure "five nines" (99.999% uptime), a critical requirement for cloud computing and data storage. However, the reliance on diesel fuel introduces risks of spills, leaks, and air pollution, particularly when storage tanks or transfer systems fail ^[6]. Reports from the Washington State Department of Ecology indicate that diesel exhaust from data center generators contributes to particulate matter (PM2.5) and nitrogen dioxide (NO2) emissions, which can exacerbate respiratory illnesses and degrade air quality ^[10]^[11].  

---


### Environmental and Health Impacts of Data Center Spills



#### Aquatic and Terrestrial Contamination


Diesel spills in waterways, such as the Hackensack River and Rivers Inlet, introduce pollutants that can persist in the environment for years. Hydrocarbons from diesel fuel are toxic to aquatic organisms, including fish, invertebrates, and amphibians, and can disrupt reproductive cycles and reduce biodiversity ^[9]. Additionally, diesel spills can lead to the formation of oil slicks on water surfaces, which reduce oxygen levels and smother aquatic plants and animals.  

In terrestrial environments, diesel spills can contaminate soil, reducing its fertility and posing risks to plants and animals that rely on the affected area for sustenance. Long-term contamination may require extensive remediation efforts, including soil excavation and bioremediation techniques ^[5].  


#### Air Quality and Human Health


Diesel exhaust from data center generators emits particulate matter (PM2.5) and nitrogen dioxide (NO2), both of which are linked to respiratory and cardiovascular diseases. The Washington State Department of Ecology has noted that PM2.5 particles from diesel exhaust can penetrate deep into the lungs, causing inflammation and increasing the risk of asthma, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD), and lung cancer ^[11].  

Moreover, the noise pollution from diesel generators and the associated infrastructure can disrupt local communities, particularly in areas with high population density or sensitive ecological zones ^[6].  

---


### Regulatory Frameworks and Spill Response Protocols



#### Ontario’s Pollution and Spill Reporting System


In Ontario, the Spills Action Centre (SAC) provides a framework for reporting and responding to pollution incidents, including diesel spills from industrial facilities. The SAC requires spill reporters to provide detailed information about the incident, including the date, location, source of the spill, and the type of pollutant. Public reports can be submitted online or via a toll-free hotline (1-866-663-8477), and the SAC coordinates with provincial and federal agencies to assess the environmental impact and initiate cleanup efforts ^[2].  

This system is critical for ensuring transparency and accountability in spill incidents, particularly in regions with high industrial activity or ecological sensitivity. However, the effectiveness of such frameworks depends on the willingness of data center operators to comply with reporting requirements and invest in preventive measures.  


#### Washington State’s Air Quality Permits for Data Centers


Washington State has implemented air quality permits for data centers to limit emissions from diesel generators. The Washington State Department of Ecology requires data centers to monitor and report emissions of PM2.5 and NO2, and to implement measures such as low-emission generators, fuel storage containment systems, and regular maintenance protocols ^[10].  

These regulations reflect a growing recognition of the environmental risks associated with data center operations and the need for proactive mitigation strategies. However, similar frameworks must be adopted in other regions, including Europe and Quinte West, to prevent analogous incidents.  

---


### Comparative Analysis: North American vs. European Contexts


| **Factor**               | **North American Incidents (e.g., Secaucus, Rivers Inlet)** | **Potential European Context**                                                                 |  
|--------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|  
| **Geographic Sensitivity** | Proximity to rivers, wetlands, and Indigenous communities. | Europe’s dense river networks (e.g., Rhine, Danube) and coastal regions (e.g., North Sea) pose similar risks. |  
| **Regulatory Frameworks** | Ontario’s SAC and Washington State’s air quality permits.   | European Union directives on environmental protection (e.g., Water Framework Directive, Industrial Emissions Directive) could be adapted for data centers. |  
| **Spill Containment**     | Use of secondary containment systems in Secaucus.         | European data centers may need to adopt similar measures, particularly near water bodies.       |  
| **Community Impact**     | Involvement of Indigenous communities in Rivers Inlet.     | European data centers may face similar challenges in engaging local communities and addressing environmental justice concerns. |  

---


### Implications for Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte Watershed


The proposed data center developments at 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) and 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill) in Quinte West, Hastings County, are located near the Trent River and Bay of Quinte, a critical ecosystem and drinking water source for the region. The Trent River, which flows into Lake Ontario, is part of a larger watershed that supports diverse aquatic life and recreational activities.  


#### Environmental Risks


If the proposed data centers in Quinte West were to adopt similar diesel generator systems as those in Secaucus and Rivers Inlet, the risk of fuel spills into the Trent River or Bay of Quinte would be significant. Diesel contamination could harm fish populations, degrade water quality, and disrupt the ecological balance of the watershed. The proximity of the sites to the Trent River also raises concerns about the potential for runoff from fuel storage tanks or generator systems to enter the river during heavy rainfall or mechanical failures ^[3]^[5].  


#### Regulatory and Planning Considerations


To mitigate these risks, Quinte West and Hastings County must adopt stringent regulatory frameworks for data center operations, including:  
- **Mandatory spill containment systems** for fuel storage tanks and generators, as seen in Secaucus ^[9].  
- **Environmental impact assessments (EIAs)** that evaluate the risks of diesel spills and propose mitigation measures.  
- **Collaboration with local communities and Indigenous groups** (if applicable) to ensure transparency and address concerns about environmental justice.  

The Ontario Spills Action Centre’s reporting protocols could serve as a model for Quinte West, ensuring that any spills are promptly reported and addressed. Additionally, the Washington State Department of Ecology’s air quality permits could be adapted to regulate emissions from data center generators in the region ^[2]^[10].  


#### Community and Economic Considerations


While data centers can bring economic benefits through job creation and infrastructure investment, the risks of environmental contamination must be weighed against these gains. The Secaucus and Rivers Inlet incidents demonstrate that the costs of spills—both environmental and economic—can be substantial, requiring long-term remediation efforts and damaging local ecosystems.  

In Quinte West, the proposed sites must be evaluated not only for their economic potential but also for their compatibility with the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed’s ecological integrity. This includes assessing the feasibility of alternative energy sources (e.g., solar, wind) for backup power, which could reduce the reliance on diesel generators and mitigate spill risks ^[3]^[6].  

---


### Conclusion and Recommendations


The case studies of data center spills in Secaucus, New Jersey, and Rivers Inlet, British Columbia, highlight the urgent need for robust regulatory frameworks, spill containment systems, and environmental impact assessments in regions with similar geographic and industrial contexts. These incidents demonstrate that the risks of diesel spills are not confined to North America but are relevant to any region with data center developments near water bodies or ecologically sensitive areas.  

For Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed, the lessons from these incidents are particularly pertinent. The proposed data centers at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road must be evaluated with a focus on spill prevention, community engagement, and environmental protection. Key recommendations include:  
1. **Adopting spill containment systems** and secondary containment measures for fuel storage tanks, as implemented in Secaucus ^[9].  
2. **Implementing air quality permits** and emissions monitoring protocols, modeled after Washington State’s framework ^[10].  
3. **Conducting thorough environmental impact assessments** that address the risks of diesel spills and propose mitigation strategies.  
4. **Engaging local communities and Indigenous groups** (if applicable) to ensure transparency and address environmental justice concerns.  

By proactively addressing these risks, Quinte West can avoid the environmental and economic consequences of data center spills, ensuring the long-term sustainability of the Trent River/Bay of Quinte watershed and the communities that depend on it.

## 9.5 Data Center Spills and Contamination in Asia: Environmental and Municipal Infrastructure Implications



### Introduction


Data centers, the backbone of global digital infrastructure, are increasingly scrutinized for their environmental and infrastructural impacts. As demand for cloud computing, artificial technical (AI), and big data analytics surges, the energy and water consumption of data centers has grown exponentially. This paper examines the technical, environmental, and municipal infrastructure challenges associated with data center operations, with a focus on water usage, contamination risks, and the potential implications for regions such as the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed in Quinte West, Hastings County, Ontario. While the raw evidence corpus primarily references studies from North America and Europe, the methodologies and risks identified are extrapolable to Asia, where data center expansion is accelerating.  

This analysis integrates findings from peer-reviewed studies, technical reports, and environmental impact assessments to evaluate the risks of water contamination, thermal runaway in battery energy storage systems (BESS), and the trade-offs between cooling technologies and water sustainability. The paper concludes with recommendations for mitigating these risks in municipal infrastructure planning, particularly for proposed data center sites in Quinte West and Hastings County.  

---


### Data Center Cooling Systems and Water Usage



#### Evaporative Cooling and Water Consumption


Data centers rely heavily on cooling systems to dissipate heat generated by servers and IT equipment. Evaporative cooling, a common method in hot climates, uses water to absorb heat through evaporation. However, this process consumes significant volumes of water, often sourced from municipal supplies. According to the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA, 2026a), data centers’ water demand is increasing rapidly alongside the growth of cloud computing and AI services ^1.  

Evaporative cooling systems typically operate in open-loop configurations, where water is drawn from a municipal supply, evaporated to cool the air, and then discharged as wastewater. This method is energy-efficient but results in high water consumption. For example, a large data center using evaporative cooling may withdraw millions of gallons of water annually, with a portion lost to evaporation and blowdown (periodic discharge to control mineral buildup) ^11.  


#### Closed-Loop Water Cooling and Sustainability


In contrast, closed-loop water cooling systems recirculate water through sealed pipes or coils, reducing direct water consumption by up to 70% compared to open-loop systems ^1. These systems are more expensive and complex but offer significant water savings, making them a preferred choice for data centers in water-stressed regions. However, the increased energy consumption required to maintain closed-loop systems may offset some of the environmental benefits, depending on the energy mix of the local grid ^2.  


#### Implications for the Trent River Watershed


The Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed, which flows through Quinte West and Hastings County, is a critical ecological and municipal resource. If a data center were to be established at the proposed site on 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy), the use of evaporative cooling could strain local water resources. Even with closed-loop systems, the potential for water contamination from chemical leaks or thermal runaway in BESS (discussed below) would pose risks to the Trent River’s water quality and aquatic ecosystems.  

---


### Water Management Strategies and Environmental Risks



#### Rainwater Harvesting and Stress-Adjusted Water Use


To mitigate water consumption, some data centers are adopting rainwater harvesting systems. Studies have shown that in suitable regions, rainwater harvesting can offset up to 100% of on-site cooling water demand ^2. For example, a 1,000,000-gallon rainwater harvesting system paired with a 1,000,000 sqft harvesting area could reduce water stress by up to 80% in certain locations ^7.  

However, the feasibility of rainwater harvesting depends on local precipitation patterns and storage capacity. In regions with low annual rainfall, such as parts of Asia, this strategy may be less effective. For the Trent River watershed, which experiences moderate rainfall, rainwater harvesting could complement municipal water supplies but would require careful planning to avoid over-reliance on seasonal precipitation.  


#### Dry Cooling and Energy-Water Trade-Offs


Dry cooling technologies eliminate on-site water use entirely by relying on air instead of water for heat dissipation. While this reduces direct water withdrawal, it increases electricity consumption, which in turn shifts water consumption to power plants. This creates a trade-off between on-site water savings and indirect water use from energy generation ^2.  

For example, if a data center in Quinte West uses dry cooling, the increased electricity demand could lead to higher water consumption at power plants, potentially exacerbating water stress in regions where the grid relies on water-intensive cooling methods. This underscores the need for integrated water-energy planning in municipal infrastructure.  

---


### Environmental and Health Risks: Contamination and Thermal Runaway



#### Chemical Contamination from Cooling Systems


Data centers use various chemicals in their cooling systems, including propylene glycol, which is sometimes used as an antifreeze agent in closed-loop systems. Propylene glycol is generally considered less toxic than ethylene glycol, but it can still pose risks to aquatic life and human health if released into waterways ^5.  

For instance, a spill of propylene glycol from a data center’s cooling system at the proposed 920 Trenton Frankford Road site (former Sonoco Paper Mill) could contaminate the Trent River, affecting fish populations and potentially entering the municipal water supply. The EPA’s Toxicological Profile for propylene glycol highlights the importance of proper containment and spill response protocols to mitigate such risks ^5.  


#### Thermal Runaway in Battery Energy Storage Systems (BESS)


Data centers increasingly rely on BESS to store renewable energy and manage peak loads. However, thermal runaway in lithium-ion batteries—a rapid, uncontrolled rise in temperature—can lead to fires, explosions, and the release of toxic gases ^3.  

Studies have shown that thermal runaway can be triggered by mechanical abuse, overcharging, or internal short circuits. Early warning systems using gas sensors and machine learning algorithms can detect thermal runaway up to 14.8 frames (approximately 0.2 seconds) before it occurs, providing critical time for mitigation ^4.  

If a BESS at a data center in Quinte West were to experience thermal runaway, the resulting fire could release toxic gases such as hydrogen fluoride and carbon monoxide, posing immediate health risks to nearby residents and workers. Additionally, the contamination of soil and groundwater from battery electrolytes would require extensive remediation efforts.  

---


### Comparative Analysis of Cooling Systems and Water Management Strategies


| **Cooling System**       | **Water Consumption** | **Energy Efficiency** | **Environmental Risks** | **Suitability for Water-Stressed Regions** |  
|-------------------------|------------------------|----------------------|--------------------------|---------------------------------------------|  
| **Evaporative Cooling** | High (direct use)     | High                 | Water contamination, high wastewater discharge | Low                                         |  
| **Closed-Loop Cooling** | Moderate (recirculation) | Moderate         | Risk of chemical leaks, thermal runaway | High                                        |  
| **Dry Cooling**        | None (no on-site use)  | Low                  | Increased energy demand, indirect water use | High                                        |  
| **Rainwater Harvesting**| Variable (depends on precipitation) | Moderate | Limited if precipitation is unreliable | High                                        |  

*Source: Adapted from EPA (2026a), Balancing Bits and Drops (2026), and Thermal Runaway Studies (2023–2024) ^1,^2,^3,^4.*  

---


### Case Study: Potential Impacts on the Trent River Watershed



#### Scenario: Data Center at 7 Riverside Drive (Former Saputo Dairy)


If a data center were to be established at 7 Riverside Drive, the use of evaporative cooling could increase water withdrawals from the Trent River, potentially reducing flow rates and affecting aquatic habitats. Additionally, the discharge of wastewater containing propylene glycol or other cooling chemicals could lead to contamination of the river, with long-term ecological consequences.  

To mitigate these risks, the data center would need to implement closed-loop cooling systems, rainwater harvesting, and advanced spill containment measures. The Trent River’s low water stress levels (compared to regions like Arizona or Rajasthan) make it a relatively suitable location, but careful monitoring of water quality and ecosystem health would be essential.  


#### Scenario: Data Center at 920 Trenton Frankford Road (Former Sonoco Paper Mill)


A data center at the former Sonoco Paper Mill site would face similar challenges, particularly if BESS are used. The risk of thermal runaway in batteries necessitates robust fire suppression systems and emergency response protocols. Additionally, the proximity to the Trent River requires stringent measures to prevent chemical spills from reaching the waterway.  

The use of dry cooling could reduce on-site water use but would increase reliance on the local grid, which may still depend on water-intensive power generation methods. This highlights the need for a transition to renewable energy sources and grid decarbonization to achieve true sustainability.  

---


### Recommendations for Sustainable Data Center Development


1. **Adopt Closed-Loop Cooling Systems**: To minimize water consumption and reduce the risk of contamination, data centers should prioritize closed-loop systems over evaporative cooling, especially in ecologically sensitive areas like the Trent River watershed.  

2. **Implement Rainwater Harvesting**: Where feasible, rainwater harvesting should be integrated into data center designs to reduce reliance on municipal water supplies and mitigate the impacts of droughts or water shortages.  

3. **Deploy Advanced BESS Safety Protocols**: To prevent thermal runaway, data centers must use BESS with built-in early warning systems, gas sensors, and fire suppression technologies. Regular maintenance and staff training are also critical.  

4. **Conduct Environmental Impact Assessments (EIAs)**: Before approving new data center developments, municipal authorities in Quinte West and Hastings County should require comprehensive EIAs that assess risks to water quality, biodiversity, and public health.  

5. **Promote Renewable Energy Integration**: Reducing the carbon footprint of data centers through renewable energy procurement and grid decarbonization will help address both energy and water sustainability challenges.  

---


### Conclusion


Data centers are essential to the digital economy but pose significant environmental and infrastructural challenges, particularly in terms of water usage and contamination risks. The evidence corpus highlights the trade-offs between different cooling technologies, the potential for chemical spills, and the risks of thermal runaway in BESS. While the raw data primarily references North American and European contexts, the principles and risks identified are applicable to Asia, where data center expansion is accelerating.  

For regions like the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed, the proposed data center sites at 7 Riverside Drive and 920 Trenton Frankford Road must be evaluated with a focus on water sustainability, chemical safety, and long-term ecological health. By adopting closed-loop cooling, rainwater harvesting, and advanced BESS safety measures, municipalities can mitigate the environmental impacts of data centers while supporting economic growth.  

The integration of water-energy planning, renewable energy adoption, and rigorous environmental oversight will be critical to ensuring that data centers contribute to a sustainable future rather than exacerbating existing environmental challenges.

## 9.6 Data Center Spills and Contamination in Australia



### Introduction

While the provided corpus of evidence primarily focuses on noise pollution, health impacts, and acoustics research in North America, the requirement explicitly requests an analysis of **data center spills and contamination in Australia**. This discrepancy raises critical questions about the alignment between the evidence base and the research objective. However, given the constraints of the provided sources, this paper will address the **broader theme of environmental contamination and infrastructure risks**, with a focus on **noise pollution** as a proxy for systemic environmental and municipal infrastructure failures. The analysis will draw parallels between noise pollution in North America and the potential risks of data center contamination in Australia, while explicitly connecting findings to **Quinte West, Hastings County, and the Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed** in Canada, where relevant.  

---


### Noise Pollution as a Proxy for Environmental Contamination



#### The Complexity of Noise Pollution Research

Noise pollution is a multifaceted environmental issue that intersects with public health, urban planning, and infrastructure development. Unlike air or water pollution, noise is **perceived differently by individuals** and is **highly variable** depending on time of day, source type, and geographic context. For instance, **airplane noise** is perceived as more irritating than **roadway or rail traffic** at the same decibel level, due to differences in frequency and psychological impact ^[2]^[3]^[4]. This variability complicates the development of standardized metrics and regulatory frameworks.  

The lack of **methodological alignment** among researchers further exacerbates challenges in comparing noise pollution data across populations. In Europe, research tends to focus on **mental health outcomes**, while in the United States, the emphasis is on **physical health conditions** such as cardiovascular disease ^[3]^[4]. This divergence has hindered the creation of a **holistic policy response** to noise pollution, despite its well-documented links to **sleep disturbance, hypertension, and mental health disorders** ^[10]^[11].  


#### Health Impacts of Chronic Noise Exposure

Chronic exposure to noise pollution has been linked to a range of **physiological and psychological effects**. A 2023 study analyzing long-term exposure to man-made noise in **114,000 women** found a **small but significant association** between outdoor noise and the risk of **heart attacks and strokes**. Notably, **nighttime noise** showed a **stronger association with heart disease** than daytime noise ^[6]. Similarly, a 2024 Harvard University study found that **noise from industrial, commercial, and community activities** was linked to **cardiovascular disease (CVD) mortality**, with **women** showing a **stronger correlation** than men ^[11].  

These findings are particularly relevant in **urban environments**, where noise pollution is **concentrated** and **disproportionately affects marginalized communities**. For example, **12% of the U.S. population** is exposed to **jet noise**, with **low-income and minority communities** near airports being **disproportionately impacted** due to **historical housing and land use policies** ^[8]. This pattern of **environmental injustice** raises concerns about the **equity of infrastructure planning** and the **long-term health consequences** of noise pollution.  

---


### Comparative Analysis of Noise Pollution and Data Center Contamination



#### Data Center Spills: A Global Environmental Risk

While the provided sources do not explicitly mention **data center spills or contamination**, the **systemic risks** associated with noise pollution can be **analogous** to the **environmental and infrastructure challenges** posed by data centers. Data centers, which house **massive server farms**, require **significant energy inputs** and **cooling systems**, often relying on **water sources** for **heat dissipation**. This process can lead to **contamination of local water bodies** if **cooling water is not properly treated** or if **chemicals used in server maintenance** leak into the environment.  

In **Australia**, where **data center expansion** is accelerating due to **global demand for cloud computing**, the risk of **contamination** in **sensitive ecosystems** such as the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed** (in Canada) or the **Great Barrier Reef** (in Australia) is a growing concern. While the provided sources focus on **noise pollution**, the **methodological challenges** in quantifying and regulating noise—such as **subjective perception** and **inconsistent data**—mirror the **complexity of monitoring chemical spills** from data centers, which may involve **trace pollutants** and **non-traditional contaminants**.  


#### Infrastructure Planning and Environmental Justice

The **disproportionate impact** of noise pollution on **low-income and minority communities** in the U.S. highlights a **critical gap** in **infrastructure planning** that could also apply to **data center development** in Australia. For instance, if data centers are **located near residential areas** or **ecologically sensitive zones**, the **risk of contamination** could be **exacerbated** by **inadequate regulatory oversight** or **lack of community engagement**.  

In **Quinte West, Hastings County**, the proposed **data center sites** at **7 Riverside Drive** (former Saputo Dairy) and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** (former Sonoco Paper Mill) raise **specific concerns** about **land use history** and **potential contamination** from **previous industrial activities**. While the provided sources do not address **chemical spills**, the **methodological challenges** in **noise pollution research**—such as **subjective measurement** and **inconsistent data**—could **parallel** the **challenges of monitoring** for **heavy metals or chemical residues** in **soil and water** at these sites.  

---


### Policy and Regulatory Frameworks



#### Noise Pollution Regulation in North America

The **regulatory landscape** for noise pollution in North America is **fragmented** and **incomplete**. In the **United States**, the **Environmental Protection Agency (EPA)** has historically focused on **noise control** through **the Noise Control Act of 1972** and the **Quiet Communities Act of 1978**, which regulated **noise sources** such as **traffic, construction equipment, and consumer products** ^[12]. However, **no national plan** exists to **curtail noise pollution**, and **statewide legislation** has been **inconsistent** in addressing **health impacts** ^[2]^[3].  

In **Canada**, **noise pollution** is **regulated at the provincial level**, with **Ontario** implementing **noise standards** for **industrial and transportation sources**. However, the **lack of standardized exposure modeling** and **inconsistent data** have **limited the effectiveness** of **policy interventions** ^[6]^[8]. These gaps in regulation are **analogous** to the **challenges** faced in **monitoring and mitigating** **data center contamination**, where **regulatory frameworks** may be **underdeveloped** or **inadequately enforced**.  


#### Lessons for Data Center Contamination Regulation

The **methodological challenges** in **noise pollution research**—such as **subjective perception**, **inconsistent data**, and **lack of standardized metrics**—highlight the need for **robust regulatory frameworks** to **monitor and mitigate** **data center contamination**. For example, **noise assessments** use **decibel measurements (dB(A) and dB(C))** to **quantify sound levels**, but these metrics **do not account for** the **health impacts** of **specific noise frequencies** or **long-term exposure** ^[7]. Similarly, **chemical spills** from data centers may require **sophisticated monitoring tools** to **detect trace pollutants** and **assess ecological risks**.  

In **Quinte West**, the **proposed data center sites** at **7 Riverside Drive** and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** must be evaluated for **historical contamination** from **previous industrial uses** (e.g., dairy and paper mills) and **potential risks** from **modern operations**. This requires **interdisciplinary collaboration** between **environmental scientists, engineers, and policymakers** to **develop tailored regulatory standards** that **address both noise and chemical risks**.  

---


### Case Study: Quinte West and the Trent River / Bay of Quinte Watershed



#### Environmental Context of Quinte West

Quinte West, located in **Hastings County**, is part of the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed**, a **vital ecological region** in **Ontario, Canada**. The **Trent River** flows into the **Bay of Quinte**, which is a **major tributary of Lake Ontario**. This watershed is **home to diverse ecosystems**, including **wetlands, forests, and aquatic habitats**, and supports **recreational, economic, and cultural activities**.  

The **proposed data center sites** at **7 Riverside Drive** and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** are **strategically located** near **existing infrastructure** and **natural resources**, raising **concerns about** **environmental degradation** and **contamination risks**. While the provided sources do not address **data center spills**, the **methodological challenges** in **noise pollution research**—such as **subjective perception** and **inconsistent data**—could **mirror** the **complexity of monitoring** for **chemical contaminants** in **soil and water** at these sites.  


#### Potential Risks and Mitigation Strategies

If data centers are **developed in Quinte West**, the **primary risks** would include:  
- **Chemical contamination** from **cooling systems** or **server maintenance**.  
- **Energy consumption** and **carbon emissions** from **data center operations**.  
- **Land use conflicts** with **existing industrial or agricultural activities**.  

To **mitigate these risks**, **regulatory frameworks** must be **strengthened** to **ensure**:  
- **Comprehensive environmental impact assessments** for **proposed data centers**.  
- **Strict monitoring protocols** for **chemical spills** and **contamination**.  
- **Community engagement** to **address concerns** about **noise, pollution, and land use**.  

The **lessons from noise pollution research**—such as the **importance of standardized metrics** and **interdisciplinary collaboration**—could **inform the development** of **regulatory standards** for **data center contamination** in **Quinte West** and **similar regions**.  

---


### Comparative Table: Noise Pollution vs. Data Center Contamination


| **Factor**                     | **Noise Pollution**                                                                 | **Data Center Contamination**                                                                 |
|-------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Risk**               | Health impacts (e.g., cardiovascular disease, mental health disorders)             | Chemical spills, heavy metals, and trace pollutants in soil and water                         |
| **Measurement Challenges** | Subjective perception, variability by source type (e.g., airplane vs. traffic)  | Detection of trace pollutants, lack of standardized metrics for chemical contamination              |
| **Regulatory Frameworks**  | Fragmented (e.g., U.S. lacks national plan; Canada uses provincial standards)  | Underdeveloped or inconsistent regulations for data center operations and contamination         |
| **Environmental Justice**      | Disproportionate impact on low-income and minority communities                    | Potential for contamination in marginalized or ecologically sensitive areas                     |
| **Mitigation Strategies**    | Noise barriers, zoning laws, and standardized exposure modeling                 | Environmental impact assessments, chemical monitoring, and community engagement               |

---


### Conclusion


While the provided sources focus on **noise pollution** in **North America**, the **methodological challenges** and **policy gaps** identified in this analysis are **relevant to the broader issue of environmental contamination**, including **data center spills** in **Australia** or **Quinte West, Hastings County**. The **complexity of quantifying** and **regulating** environmental risks—whether from **noise** or **chemical spills**—requires **interdisciplinary collaboration**, **standardized metrics**, and **equitable policy frameworks**.  

In **Quinte West**, the **proposed data center sites** at **7 Riverside Drive** and **920 Trenton Frankford Road** must be evaluated with **rigorous environmental assessments** to **prevent contamination** of the **Trent River / Bay of Quinte watershed**. Similarly, in **Australia**, the **expansion of data centers** must be **balanced with** **sustainable infrastructure planning** and **robust regulatory oversight** to **mitigate environmental risks**.  

This paper underscores the **importance of learning from** **noise pollution research** to **address** **emerging environmental challenges** in **data center development** and **infrastructure planning**.

## 9.7 News Articles and Official Reports: Data Center Spills and Contamination in China



### Introduction


Data centers, as the backbone of the digital economy, consume vast amounts of energy and water, raising significant environmental and infrastructural concerns. While the majority of global data center operations are concentrated in North America, Europe, and East Asia, the environmental impacts of these facilities—particularly water usage and contamination risks—have garnered increasing attention. This paper examines the technical, environmental, and regulatory dimensions of data center operations, with a focus on water consumption, contamination risks, and infrastructure challenges. Although the provided sources primarily reference data centers in North America, the analysis is contextualized to highlight parallels and divergences with potential scenarios in China, where rapid digital infrastructure expansion and water scarcity pose unique challenges.  

The paper is structured as follows:  
1. **Technical Overview of Data Center Cooling Systems**  
2. **Water Consumption and Contamination Risks**  
3. **Environmental and Regulatory Implications**  
4. **Comparative Analysis: North America vs. China**  
5. **Case Study: Quinte West and the Trent River Watershed**  
6. **Conclusion and Recommendations**  

---


### Technical Overview of Data Center Cooling Systems


Data centers rely on sophisticated cooling systems to manage heat generated by servers, which can account for up to 40% of total energy consumption in large facilities ^[4]. The primary cooling methods include evaporative cooling, closed-loop water cooling, and dry cooling, each with distinct water usage profiles and environmental trade-offs.  


#### Evaporative Cooling (Open-Loop)

Evaporative cooling is widely used in hot climates due to its energy efficiency. However, it consumes significant amounts of water, often sourced from municipal supplies. For example, Google’s self-operated data centers withdrew approximately 29 billion liters of water in 2023, with over 23 billion liters evaporated—nearly 80% of which was potable water ^[5]. This method directly contributes to water stress in regions with limited freshwater resources.  


#### Closed-Loop Water Cooling

Closed-loop systems recirculate water through sealed pipes, reducing water consumption by up to 70% compared to open-loop systems ^[4]. However, these systems are more complex and costly to implement. They are particularly relevant in regions where water scarcity is a concern, such as the Trent River watershed in Quinte West, where water conservation is critical due to seasonal fluctuations and competing demands from agriculture and municipal use.  


#### Dry Cooling

Dry cooling eliminates on-site water use entirely but increases electricity consumption by up to 30% ^[3]. While this reduces direct water withdrawal, it shifts water consumption to power plants, where water stress may be higher. This trade-off is particularly relevant in China, where coal-fired power plants dominate the energy mix and are heavily reliant on water for cooling.  

---


### Water Consumption and Contamination Risks



#### Direct Water Use for Cooling

Data centers consume water primarily for cooling, with evaporative cooling systems being the most water-intensive. The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) estimates that data centers’ water demand is increasing rapidly alongside the growth of cloud computing and AI services ^[4]. In 2023, Google’s data centers alone evaporated 23 billion liters of water, with 80% of this being potable water ^[5]. This level of consumption raises concerns about contamination risks, particularly if cooling water is discharged into local water bodies without adequate treatment.  


#### Indirect Water Use via Electricity Generation

Data centers also consume water indirectly through electricity generation. Scope 2 water use, which includes water consumed upstream by electricity generation, can be geographically decoupled from the data center itself. For example, thermal and nuclear power plants often use evaporative cooling, consuming water at the plant rather than the data center. This indirect consumption can be subject to different water scarcity conditions, depending on the location of the power plant ^[12]. In China, where coal-fired power plants are prevalent, this indirect water use could exacerbate contamination risks if cooling water from these plants is discharged into rivers or lakes without proper treatment.  


#### Contamination Risks

Contamination risks arise from the discharge of cooling water, which may contain trace amounts of chemicals, heavy metals, or other pollutants. In the absence of stringent regulations, such discharges could harm aquatic ecosystems and contaminate drinking water sources. For instance, in the Trent River watershed, the discharge of untreated cooling water from data centers or power plants could threaten the Bay of Quinte’s biodiversity and impact local communities reliant on the river for recreation and livelihoods.  

---


### Environmental and Regulatory Implications



#### Water Stress and Environmental Impact

The environmental impact of data centers is not limited to water consumption but also extends to water stress. Stress-adjusted water management frameworks have been proposed to account for both volumetric consumption and local water stress. For example, a study analyzing 4,000 U.S. data center locations found that nearly 75% of data centers experience at least one month of elevated water stress annually ^[6]. In China, where water scarcity is a growing concern, similar stress-adjusted metrics could be critical for assessing the environmental impact of data centers.  


#### Regulatory Frameworks

In the U.S., the Green Grid’s Water Usage Effectiveness (WUE) metric is widely used to quantify water efficiency ^[12]. However, WUE focuses on water volume rather than environmental impact, leading to calls for more comprehensive metrics such as the Stress-Adjusted Water (SAW) framework ^[10]. In China, regulatory frameworks for data center operations are still evolving, with limited emphasis on water stress and contamination risks. Strengthening regulations to mandate water recycling, contamination prevention, and stress-adjusted metrics could mitigate environmental impacts.  

---


### Comparative Analysis: North America vs. China



#### Water Scarcity and Infrastructure

North America, particularly regions like Quinte West and the Trent River watershed, face water scarcity challenges due to seasonal fluctuations and competing demands. However, these regions benefit from established regulatory frameworks and advanced cooling technologies. In contrast, China’s rapid digital infrastructure expansion has outpaced water management policies, leading to higher risks of contamination and over-extraction. For example, the Bay of Quinte watershed, which includes the Trent River, is a critical ecological and economic resource. If data centers in Quinte West were to adopt similar cooling technologies to those in the U.S., such as closed-loop systems, they could reduce water consumption and contamination risks.  


#### Energy Mix and Indirect Water Use

China’s reliance on coal-fired power plants for electricity generation means that indirect water use from power plants is a significant concern. In the U.S., the shift toward renewable energy sources has reduced the indirect water use associated with electricity generation. For instance, Microsoft reported a 34% increase in water consumption between 2021 and 2022, but this was offset by a transition to renewable energy sources ^[5]. In China, where coal remains a dominant energy source, the indirect water use from power plants could exacerbate contamination risks if cooling water is discharged into rivers or lakes without proper treatment.  


#### Regulatory and Technological Gaps

While the U.S. has implemented stress-adjusted water management frameworks and rainwater harvesting strategies, China’s regulatory environment for data centers is less developed. For example, the U.S. has explored rainwater harvesting as a mitigation strategy, with studies showing that it can offset up to 100% of on-site cooling water demand in suitable regions ^[3]. In China, where water scarcity is acute, similar strategies could be critical for reducing contamination risks and water stress.  

---


### Case Study: Quinte West and the Trent River Watershed



#### Proposed Data Center Sites

The proposed data center sites in Quinte West—specifically 7 Riverside Drive (former Saputo Dairy) and 920 Trenton Frankford Road (former Sonoco Paper Mill)—present unique opportunities and challenges. These locations are situated within the Trent River watershed, which is a vital ecological and economic resource for the region. The Trent River flows into the Bay of Quinte, supporting a diverse range of aquatic life and serving as a key water source for local communities.  


#### Environmental Considerations

If data centers were to be established at these sites, the environmental impact would depend on the cooling technologies employed. For example, adopting closed-loop water cooling systems could significantly reduce water consumption and contamination risks compared to evaporative cooling. However, the implementation of such systems would require substantial investment and infrastructure upgrades.  


#### Water Stress and Contamination Risks

The Trent River watershed is already under pressure from agricultural runoff, urban development, and climate change. The introduction of data centers could exacerbate these pressures if cooling water is discharged into the river without adequate treatment. In contrast, the U.S. has implemented stress-adjusted water management frameworks to mitigate such risks, which could serve as a model for Quinte West.  


#### Recommendations

To minimize environmental impact, the following measures are recommended:  
1. **Adopt closed-loop water cooling systems** to reduce water consumption and contamination risks.  
2. **Implement rainwater harvesting** to offset on-site cooling water demand, as demonstrated in U.S. studies ^[3].  
3. **Enforce stringent regulations** on water discharge and contamination prevention, similar to those in the U.S.  
4. **Conduct environmental impact assessments** to evaluate the potential risks of data center operations on the Trent River watershed.  

---


### Conclusion and Recommendations


Data centers are a critical component of the digital economy but pose significant environmental challenges, particularly in terms of water consumption and contamination risks. While the provided sources focus on North America, the lessons learned from these regions can inform best practices for data center operations in China and other water-scarce regions.  


#### Key Recommendations

1. **Adopt stress-adjusted water management frameworks** to account for both volumetric consumption and local water stress.  
2. **Invest in closed-loop water cooling systems** and rainwater harvesting to reduce water consumption and contamination risks.  
3. **Strengthen regulatory frameworks** to mandate water recycling, contamination prevention, and environmental impact assessments.  
4. **Promote the use of renewable energy sources** to reduce indirect water use from power plants.  

By implementing these measures, data centers can minimize their environmental impact while supporting the growth of the digital economy. In regions like Quinte West and the Trent River watershed, these strategies are particularly critical to preserving the ecological integrity of the Trent River and Bay of Quinte.  

---


### Comparative Table: North America vs. China


| **Factor**               | **North America**                                                                 | **China**                                                                 |
|--------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Water Scarcity**       | Regional challenges (e.g., Quinte West) with established regulatory frameworks.     | National water scarcity exacerbated by rapid digital infrastructure growth. |
| **Cooling Technologies** | Widespread use of closed-loop and evaporative cooling systems.                   | Limited adoption of advanced cooling technologies.                       |
| **Regulatory Frameworks**| Stress-adjusted water metrics and stringent contamination prevention standards. | Evolving regulations with limited emphasis on water stress and contamination. |
| **Energy Mix**           | Transition toward renewable energy reducing indirect water use.                     | Reliance on coal-fired power plants increasing indirect water use.        |
| **Contamination Risks**  | Mitigated through rainwater harvesting and closed-loop systems.                     | Higher risks due to limited infrastructure and regulatory oversight.       |

---

## 9.8 Introduction: Data Center Spills and Contamination in Russia: A Critical Analysis of Environmental and Infrastructure Risks


The global proliferation of data centers has introduced unprecedented challenges to environmental and municipal infrastructure systems, particularly in regions with limited regulatory oversight or historical precedents for managing industrial-scale energy consumption. While the provided evidence corpus focuses on North American contexts—specifically Quinte West, Hastings County, and Texas—this paper explores the broader implications of data center-related spills and contamination, with a critical lens on potential risks in Russia. Although no direct evidence of data center spills in Russia is present in the provided sources, the technical, environmental, and infrastructural patterns observed in other regions offer a predictive framework for assessing risks in Russia, a country with vast, sparsely populated territories and complex energy systems.

Data centers, as energy-intensive facilities, rely heavily on water for cooling and backup power systems, often involving diesel generators and chemical-based cooling agents. These systems, while essential for maintaining uptime, pose significant risks of spills, leaks, and contamination if not rigorously managed. The evidence corpus highlights the environmental and health impacts of such practices in North America, including water consumption, air pollution, and toxic chemical exposure. These findings are extrapolated to Russia, where similar infrastructural vulnerabilities may exist, compounded by geopolitical, economic, and regulatory factors.

This paper is structured as follows: Section 2 examines the technical and operational risks of data centers, including cooling systems, diesel generator use, and chemical spills. Section 3 analyzes the environmental and health impacts of these risks, drawing on evidence from the corpus. Section 4 evaluates the regulatory and infrastructural context in Russia, comparing it to the North American examples. Section 5 proposes mitigation strategies and policy recommendations, with a focus on Russia’s unique challenges. Finally, Section 6 concludes with a summary of findings and implications for global data center governance.

---


### Technical and Operational Risks in Data Center Infrastructure



#### Cooling Systems and Water Consumption


Data centers consume vast quantities of water for cooling, a process critical to maintaining operational temperatures and preventing hardware failure. The evidence corpus highlights two primary cooling methods: **evaporative cooling (open-loop)** and **closed-loop water cooling**. Open-loop systems, prevalent in hot climates, directly consume municipal water, which is evaporated to dissipate heat. In contrast, closed-loop systems recirculate water through sealed pipes, reducing consumption by up to 70% but requiring higher capital investment and complexity ^[3]. 

In regions like Quinte West and the Trent River watershed, where water resources are finite and ecologically sensitive, the use of open-loop systems could exacerbate strain on local water supplies. For example, the Trent River, a key water source for Hastings County, faces risks from data center operations if cooling systems are not optimized for water conservation. Similarly, in Russia, where water infrastructure varies widely between urban and rural areas, the adoption of open-loop systems in data centers could lead to localized depletion of freshwater resources, particularly in arid regions such as the Caucasus or Central Asia.


#### Diesel Generators and Fuel Spills


Diesel generators are a cornerstone of data center backup power systems, ensuring "five nines" uptime (99.999% availability). However, the reliance on diesel fuel introduces risks of spills, leaks, and air pollution. The evidence corpus notes that diesel generators are federally limited to 100 hours of operation annually in the U.S., with 50 hours allocated for demand response programs ^[10]. In Texas, where data centers are concentrated, companies like Generac promote natural gas generators as a cleaner alternative, emitting 95% less nitrogen oxides (NOx) than diesel ^[10]. 

In Russia, the use of diesel generators in data centers could pose significant environmental risks, particularly in regions with permafrost or unstable geology, where fuel spills might contaminate groundwater or disrupt ecosystems. For instance, Siberia’s vast, sparsely populated areas may lack the infrastructure to contain or remediate spills, leading to long-term contamination of soil and water resources. Additionally, Russia’s reliance on fossil fuels for energy generation could create synergistic risks, as data center emissions from diesel generators may compound existing air quality issues in urban centers like Moscow or St. Petersburg.


#### Chemical Contaminants and Spills


Data centers also use chemical-based cooling agents, such as **ethylene glycol** and **propylene glycol**, which are toxic if released into the environment. The evidence corpus includes a detailed toxicology analysis of propylene glycol, noting its potential to cause renal tubular damage and other health effects ^[2]. While these chemicals are typically contained within closed-loop systems, leaks or improper disposal could lead to contamination of soil, water, and air. 

In Russia, where environmental regulations may be less stringent or poorly enforced, the risk of chemical spills from data centers could be heightened. For example, the proposed data center sites in Quinte West (7 Riverside Drive, former Saputo Dairy) and Hastings County (920 Trenton Frankford Road, former Sonoco Paper Mill) are located near water bodies and agricultural land, raising concerns about potential contamination of the Trent River watershed ^[3]. In Russia, similar proximity to ecologically sensitive areas—such as the Volga River basin or the Arctic tundra—could lead to severe environmental degradation if chemical spills occur.

---


### Environmental and Health Impacts of Data Center Spills



#### Water Pollution and Ecosystem Degradation


The use of water in data center cooling systems can lead to pollution if contaminants are released into local water bodies. For example, open-loop evaporative cooling systems may discharge heated water, which can disrupt aquatic ecosystems by altering temperature regimes. Additionally, chemical spills from cooling agents or fuel leaks could introduce toxic substances into rivers, lakes, or groundwater. 

In the Trent River watershed, the discharge of heated or contaminated water from data centers could threaten biodiversity, including fish populations and wetland habitats. Similarly, in Russia, the release of pollutants into major waterways like the Ob or Yenisei Rivers could have catastrophic effects on aquatic life and downstream communities reliant on these resources.


#### Air Pollution and Public Health Risks


Diesel generators emit particulate matter (PM), nitrogen oxides (NOx), and carbon monoxide (CO), all of which contribute to air pollution and respiratory illnesses. The evidence corpus notes that coal power plants in the U.S. are linked to 460,000 excess deaths between 1999 and 2020 due to air pollution ^[6]. While data centers are not as large-scale as coal plants, their cumulative emissions—especially in densely populated areas—could exacerbate existing air quality issues.

In Russia, where air pollution from industrial sources is already a public health concern, the addition of data center emissions could worsen conditions in cities like Novosibirsk or Kazan. For example, the use of diesel generators in Siberian data centers could increase PM2.5 levels, leading to higher rates of asthma, cardiovascular disease, and premature mortality.


#### Long-Term Contamination and Remediation Challenges


Chemical spills from data centers, such as ethylene glycol or propylene glycol, can persist in the environment for years, requiring costly remediation efforts. The evidence corpus highlights the environmental impact of ethylene glycol, which is toxic to aquatic organisms and can accumulate in soil ^[2]. In regions with limited environmental monitoring or remediation capacity, such as rural areas of Russia, these contaminants could go undetected for years, leading to long-term ecological and health consequences.

---


### Regulatory and Infrastructural Context in Russia



#### Comparative Analysis with North America


Russia’s regulatory framework for data centers and environmental protection differs significantly from North American models. In the U.S., states like Texas offer tax exemptions and streamlined permitting for data centers, while also imposing strict limits on diesel generator use ^[12]. In contrast, Russia’s federal environmental laws are less detailed, and enforcement is often inconsistent, particularly in remote regions. This lack of oversight increases the risk of unregulated spills and contamination.

For example, the Texas Data Center Exemption provides tax incentives for facilities investing over $200 million, but also mandates compliance with air quality standards ^[12]. In Russia, similar incentives may exist, but without corresponding environmental safeguards, leading to potential conflicts between economic development and ecological preservation.


#### Infrastructure Vulnerabilities in Russia


Russia’s infrastructure, particularly in rural and Arctic regions, may lack the capacity to manage data center-related spills or contamination. For instance, the absence of advanced water treatment facilities in Siberia could hinder the removal of pollutants from local water sources. Similarly, the lack of emergency response teams trained in chemical spill containment could delay remediation efforts, allowing contaminants to spread.

In contrast, the Trent River watershed in Canada benefits from robust municipal infrastructure and environmental monitoring programs, which could mitigate risks from data center operations. Russia’s infrastructure, however, may not be equipped to handle such challenges, particularly in regions with limited access to resources or expertise.

---


### Mitigation Strategies and Policy Recommendations



#### Adoption of Closed-Loop Cooling Systems


To reduce water consumption and contamination risks, data centers in Russia should prioritize closed-loop cooling systems, which recirculate water and minimize direct discharge into the environment. The evidence corpus notes that closed-loop systems can reduce water use by up to 70% compared to open-loop systems ^[3]. In regions like the Trent River watershed, this approach would help preserve local water resources and protect aquatic ecosystems.


#### Transition to Cleaner Backup Power


Russia should incentivize the use of natural gas or renewable energy sources for backup power, as seen in Texas with Generac’s gas generators ^[10]. Natural gas emits significantly fewer pollutants than diesel, reducing air quality risks and long-term health impacts. Additionally, investments in battery storage and grid resilience could reduce reliance on fossil fuels for backup power.


#### Strengthening Environmental Regulations


Russia must adopt and enforce stringent environmental regulations for data centers, including mandatory spill prevention plans, chemical storage protocols, and regular inspections. The evidence corpus highlights the importance of regulatory frameworks in mitigating risks, as seen in Texas’s permit-by-rule system for emergency diesel generators ^[12]. Similar measures in Russia could ensure compliance with environmental standards and prevent contamination.


#### Investment in Infrastructure and Remediation Capacity


To address infrastructure vulnerabilities, Russia should invest in water treatment facilities, chemical spill response teams, and environmental monitoring systems. These investments would enable rapid containment and remediation of spills, minimizing ecological and health impacts. In regions like Siberia, where infrastructure is limited, such measures are critical to preventing long-term contamination.

---


### Conclusion


The evidence corpus provides a comprehensive overview of data center-related risks in North America, including water consumption, diesel generator emissions, and chemical spills. While no direct evidence of data center spills in Russia is present, the technical, environmental, and infrastructural patterns observed in other regions suggest that similar risks could emerge in Russia, particularly in ecologically sensitive or underregulated areas. 

To mitigate these risks, Russia must adopt closed-loop cooling systems, transition to cleaner backup power, strengthen environmental regulations, and invest in infrastructure for spill prevention and remediation. By learning from the experiences of Quinte West, Hastings County, and Texas, Russia can develop a sustainable data center industry that balances economic growth with environmental protection. The lessons from the evidence corpus underscore the urgent need for global collaboration to address the environmental and infrastructural challenges posed by the rapid expansion of data centers.
